<meta>This page follows the title page (which contained library stamps and a faint "ghost" impression of the work's title). We now enter the formal dedication of the book, written by the scholar-philosopher Francesco Patrizi (Franciscus Patricius) to his friend, the physician Antonio Maria Parolario. Patrizi is introducing his collection of ancient "lost" wisdom.</meta>

<note>A decorative woodcut border frames the entire page, featuring cherubs (putti) and intricate floral motifs, a common style for high-quality 16th-century publications.</note>

# TO ANTONIO MARIA
# PAROLARIO,
# DISTINGUISHED PHYSICIAN, AND
# EXCEPTIONAL FRIEND.

Francesco Patrizi sends his fondest greetings. <note>original: "S.P.D." stands for "Salutem Plurimam Dicit," a standard Latin greeting formula meaning "sends many greetings."</note>

<note>A large historiated initial V serves as the start of the text. It depicts a figure kneeling before a seated figure within the letterform, possibly representing a student receiving knowledge from a master.</note> **V**OLUMES three in number, and quite small, regarding the remnants of the ancients, have been left to us by time—that devourer of all things—which ought to be pursued with the total admiration of the soul. <note>original: "tempus edax rerum," a famous phrase from Ovid’s Metamorphoses referring to how time consumes and destroys the physical works of man.</note> These are, namely, several booklets by Hermes Trismegistus, a most ancient writer, in which the principles of all human wisdom are contained, handed down not in the <term>Pythagorean manner<gloss>an esoteric teaching style using silence, symbols, and secrecy</gloss></term>, but in a <term>didactic manner<gloss>a direct, academic style intended for clear instruction</gloss></term>. These we shall soon issue, enlarged and corrected in countless places. 

Next, there are fourteen booklets titled *Mystical Philosophy according to the Egyptians*, first published long ago in Rome and later in Paris under the title *On the Secret Part of Divine Wisdom*, edited and explained by <term>Jacques Charpentier<gloss>a 16th-century French professor known for his controversial views on Aristotle and Plato</gloss></term>. Although these works are attributed to Aristotle, they contain neither Aristotelian principles, nor his methods, nor his objectives. Rather, they are by some student of Plato, whose name, through the injustice of time...

A 2 <note>This is a "signature mark," used by early modern printers to guide the bookbinder in folding and assembling the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>In this dedication, Francesco Patrizi introduces a collection of ancient philosophical texts, specifically highlighting the works of Hermes Trismegistus and a Platonic text that had been misattributed to Aristotle. He frames the recovery of these "small volumes" as a victory over the destructive nature of time.</summary>
<keywords>Francesco Patrizi, Antonio Maria Parolario, Hermes Trismegistus, Jacques Charpentier, Neoplatonism, Aristotelianism, ancient wisdom, book history</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Francesco Patrizi to Antonio Maria Parolario. Patrizi has just mentioned that many ancient works have been lost or damaged over time; he now introduces the specific works of Proclus that he has translated to remedy this loss.</meta>

...perished through neglect, though they had been written down. There are, furthermore, these *Theological Elements* of Proclus, which, having just been rendered into English <note>original: "latina facta," meaning translated into Latin. In this accessible version, the text is now in English.</note> by us, are given to you and to all those devoted to wisdom. I speak of Proclus the <term>Successor<gloss>From the Greek 'Diadochos,' a title for the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens, marking him as the spiritual heir to Plato's chair</gloss></term>, who, beyond his other admirable erudition, was a most great mathematician and a Platonic—that is to say, a divine—philosopher. 

His mathematical skill is shown by the commentaries he wrote on Euclid and Ptolemy; his philosophical depth is proven by his commentaries on the *Timaeus*, the *First Alcibiades*, the *Cratylus*, and the *Parmenides*, as well as his inquiries into the *Republic*, his six books on the *Theology of Plato*, and these very *Elements of Theology*. All of these indeed still survive, but the greater part of them has not yet been printed by the press, and none, until now, have been translated from the Greek. The rest of his works, including his commentaries on all the other Dialogues of Plato and writings of different kinds, have perished. 

There do survive, however, in this same genre of Platonic philosophy, the most elegant commentaries of Hermias (who was the father of Ammonius) on the *Phaedrus*, as well as certain excerpts from the commentaries of Olympiodorus—a man by far the most learned—on the *Phaedo* and *Philebus*, and his complete work on the *Gorgias*. But most eminent of all are the *Inquiries Concerning the Principles of Things* by Damascius. 

If all these works were made available to the public, they would excite the most ardent love for divine wisdom in those hearts which devote themselves to philosophy not for the sake of mere quibbling <note>Patrizi is likely critiquing the Scholastic philosophers of his day, whom he felt focused too much on logic games and Aristotelian definitions rather than spiritual truth.</note>, but for this one purpose: that they may truly be wise. If these works should eventually come into the light through the efforts—laborious though they may be, yet glorious—of some man who is truly a man, it will finally become apparent how great a part of wisdom lies buried in darkness while we follow and love only this common wisdom currently used in the schools. To lend a hand to this cause, as much as life and leisure allow, we do not lack the 
                                                                           spirit

<summary>Francesco Patrizi introduces his translation of Proclus' "Elements of Theology" and lists other surviving Neoplatonic works by Hermias, Olympiodorus, and Damascius that remain unprinted. He argues that recovering these "lost" Greek texts is essential to overcoming the limited "school wisdom" (Aristotelianism) of his time.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Elements of Theology, Francesco Patrizi, Hermias, Olympiodorus, Damascius, translation, history of philosophy, Scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page concludes Francesco Patrizi’s dedication to the physician Antonio Maria Parolario. Patrizi laments that his own difficult life circumstances prevented him from completing even more work, but presents these translations of Proclus as a model for how scientific and philosophical subjects should be treated with mathematical rigor.</meta>

...a vast spirit. Would that a more peaceful life and less adverse fortune had been granted to us; perhaps then the entire work would have been completed by now. 

But let us return to Proclus. Most learned Parolari, I give these *Theological Elements* of his to you with a willing heart, so that you may recognize with what keenness of mind—and in the mathematical style <note>original: *mathematico more*. Proclus composed his philosophy using a system of axioms and "geometrical" proofs.</note>—the entire Theology of Plato has been proven with a wonderful and compelling logic. Also included are his *Physical Elements* <note>original: *physica elementa*, referring to Proclus’s *Elements of Physics*, a summary of Aristotle's natural philosophy</note>, drawn from the teachings of Aristotle and presented in the same fashion—a method by which no subject other than mathematics has ever been treated by anyone else. By this example, Proclus might inspire the minds of sublime philosophers to imitate this rigor in the remaining branches of philosophy. 

This is especially true for you, Parolari, who even as a young man shone with extraordinary intellectual renown, and since then, through the loftiness of your talent in both teaching and practicing medicine, have been raised to such a peak of glory that hardly anyone else can keep pace with you. If you achieve this <note>i.e., applying Proclus's rigorous method to the study of medicine</note>, you will either equal or even surpass Hippocrates himself <note>The famous ancient Greek physician, often called the "Father of Medicine."</note>. In the meantime, continue to hold me in your affection.

->Given at Ferrara, the 10th day of May, 1583.<-

<note>The lower half of the page contains faint bleed-through text from the reverse side, which is mirrored and partially illegible.</note>

<summary>Patrizi presents his translations of Proclus’s theological and physical works to Parolario, praising Proclus's use of mathematical logic and encouraging the physician to apply similar rigor to medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Francesco Patrizi, Proclus, Antonio Maria Parolario, Elements of Theology, Elements of Physics, mathematical method, Aristotle, Hippocrates, Ferrara</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the actual text of Proclus's "Elements of Theology." Following the dedication by the translator Francesco Patrizi on the previous page, we move from the introductory front matter to the first two formal propositions of the work.</meta>

# THE THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS
## BY PROCLUS OF LYCIA, THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: *Diadochi*. This title refers to Proclus’s position as the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens, the "successor" to the lineage of Plato.</note>
### 211 PROPOSITIONS
### DEMONSTRATED IN THE MATHEMATICAL STYLE

->**CONCERNING THE ONE**<-

**PROPOSITION I.**
Every multitude participates in some way in the One.

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For if it did not participate in any way, then neither the whole multitude would be one, nor would each of the individuals from which the multitude is composed. Instead, each of those parts would itself be a multitude, and this would continue to infinity; and each of these infinite parts would again be an infinite multitude. For if a thing does not participate in the One in any part or manner, neither the whole itself nor the individual parts within it would be limited; it would be infinite altogether and in every respect. 

For whatever single part you might take from the many, it must be either one or not-one. But if it is not-one, it must be either many or nothing. Now, if each part is nothing, then that which is composed of them would also be nothing. But if it is many, it would be composed of "infinitely infinites" <note>original: *ex infinities infinitis*. Proclus argues that without the concept of 'one' to bound a thing, any part would be infinitely divisible into further multitudes, leading to a logical absurdity where the part is as "infinite" as the whole.</note>. These things, however, are impossible. For nothing among existing things is composed of infinitely infinites, as there is nothing greater than the infinite itself. Yet that which is composed of all its parts is necessarily greater than any single part. Nor can anything be composed of nothing. Therefore, every <term>multitude</term> <note>A collection of many things.</note> participates in some way in <term>the One</term> <note>The ultimate, simple source of all existence in Neoplatonism, from which all things flow.</note>.

**PROPOSITION II.**
Everything that participates in the One is both one and not-one.

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For if this were not so, it would be the One itself. To <term>participate</term> <note>The process by which a lower reality shares in the characteristics of a higher, more perfect reality.</note> in the One means to be something other than the One itself. Such a thing "undergoes" the One through participation <note>original: *patitur unum per participationem*. In this philosophical context, "to undergo" or "to suffer" a quality means to receive it from a higher source rather than possessing it as one's own essential nature.</note>, and it has sustained becoming one. 

However, if there is nothing besides the One, and it is the One alone, it will not participate in the One, but will be the One itself. If, however, there is...

<summary>Proclus begins his metaphysical system by proving that all groups or "multitudes" must possess a degree of unity (the One) to exist, and that anything possessing unity is distinct from the Absolute One itself.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, The One, Multitude, Participation, Neoplatonism, Mathematical Proof, Metaphysics, Infinity</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of the "One." The text picks up in the middle of the demonstration for Proposition II, having established in the previous page that everything in existence must relate to a single source of unity.</meta>

ELEMENTA THEOLOGICA. 1
<note>The Theological Elements, Page 1.</note>

...is something besides that which is not-one, a participant in the One, and is not-one itself; and the "one" we speak of is not the "One-itself," <note>original: *ipsunum*. This is a Latin philosophical term used to translate the Greek *auto-hen*, referring to the Absolute One or the pure principle of Unity itself, rather than a thing that is "one."</note> but a "one being," a participant in the One. This, therefore, is not-one, nor is it the One-itself; yet it is one, being simultaneously a being and a participant in the One. Because of this, it is not-one existing by itself, but is both one and not-one: existing as something other than the One, which in one sense surpasses the "not-one," but in another sense undergoes the "one." <note>Proclus is arguing that anything that "participates" in unity is a hybrid: it has its own nature (which is not-one) but is shaped or "affected" by the One.</note>
Every participant in the One, therefore, is both one and not-one.

PROPOSITION III.
Every thing that becomes one, becomes one by participation in the One; and insofar as it has undergone the participation in the One, it is one.

DEMONSTRATION.
For if things that are not one become one, they surely do so by coming together and communicating with each other; they become one and sustain the presence of the One without being the One-itself. They participate in the One, therefore, to the extent that they allow themselves to become one. For if they were already one, they would not "become" one. That which already is, does not become that which it already is. But if they become one from a state of "not-one" or from a privation <note>In Aristotelian and Platonic logic, "privation" is the absence of a quality that should naturally be present.</note> of unity, they will first possess the One by having some unity generated within them.

PROPOSITION IV.
Everything united is different from the One-itself.

DEMONSTRATION.
For if a thing is united, it will be a participant in the One, insofar as it is called "united." But a participant in the One is both one and not-one. The One-itself, however, is by no means both one and not-one. For if the One-itself were also both one and not-one, then that "one" which is within it would again have both these qualities, and this would continue to infinity, with no One-itself ever being found where the mind could stop. Instead, everything would be both one and not-one. Therefore, the "united" is something other than the One. For if the "united" and the One were the same thing, there would be an infinite multitude, and likewise for each of the components from which the united thing is made. <note>Proclus uses a "reductio ad absurdum" here: if there isn't a pure, simple "One" at the top of the chain, everything would dissolve into an endless, bottomless series of divisions.</note>

PROPOSITION V.
Every multitude is secondary to the One.

[bottom right catchword] DE
<note>Catchword for the next page: *DEMONSTRATIO* (Demonstration).</note>

<summary>This page presents Propositions III through V of Proclus's system, establishing that "becoming one" requires participation in a higher Unity and that the "One-itself" must be distinct from the things it unifies to avoid an infinite logical regress.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, participation, unity, multitude, One-itself, logic, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition III and begins the core logical proof for the primacy of the One over all multiplicity. It follows the previous page’s discussion on the nature of "being" as a participant in unity.</meta>

# THE DEMONSTRATION OF PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
<note>Proclus "the Successor" (Diadochus) was the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens during the 5th century AD. This text is his systematic "Elementa Theologica" or Elements of Theology, modeled after Euclid’s geometry to prove the nature of the divine.</note>

For if a multitude existed before the One, it would not participate in the One. Indeed, if that multitude exists before the One comes into being, it cannot be a participant of that which does not yet exist. This is because that which participates in the One is both "one" and "not-one"; however, if the primary state were a multitude, the One would not yet have attained subsistence. <note>Proclus argues that for something to "share" in unity, unity must already exist as a source. If you start with a crowd, you can't explain how the individuals in that crowd are "each one" without a prior concept of oneness.</note>

But it is impossible for any multitude to exist that does not participate in the One in any way. Therefore, multitude is not prior to the One.

If, however, multitude were simultaneous with the One by nature and of the same rank (for nothing prevents this in terms of time), the One could not be "many" in itself, nor could the multitude be "one," since they would be distinct from each other by their very natures. Since neither would be prior or posterior to the other, the multitude would not be "one" in itself; and every single thing contained within that multitude would not be "one," and this would continue to infinity. <note>This "infinite regress" is a classic Greek logical trap: if a thing isn't a single unit, it must be made of parts, and if those parts aren't units, they are made of more parts, forever, meaning the object could never exist.</note> This is impossible.

Therefore, multitude participates in the One by its very nature, and it is impossible to take any part of it that is not "one." For if it were not "one," it would be an infinity made of infinities, as has been demonstrated. It absolutely participates in the One.

If, however, the One—which is the **One-itself**—does not participate in multitude in any way, then multitude will be entirely posterior to the One; it participates in the One, but the One is not shared or divided by the multitude. <note>original: *ipsunum*. This is a Latin term for the Greek *auto-hen*, the Absolute One. Proclus uses this to distinguish between a thing that happens to be one (like a single apple) and the Principle of Unity itself.</note>

But if the One were also to participate in multitude, it would be "one" according to its existence (as a standing substance) but "not-one" according to its participation. In that case, the **One-itself** would be multiplied, just as a multitude is made "one" through unity. Therefore, both the One would communicate with multitude, and multitude with the One. 

If things that come together and communicate in this way are brought together by something else, then that "something else" exists before them. If, however, they bring themselves together, they are not truly opposed to one another. For opposites do not naturally hasten toward each other.

But if the One and multitude are divided against one another—and multitude, insofar as it is multitude, is not the One, and the One, insofar as it is the One, is not multitude—then neither can exist within the other; for if they did, they would be both "one" and "two" at the same time. <note>Proclus uses the analogy of logic: just as "rational" and "irrational" are distinct categories that define a group, Unity and Multiplicity are distinct definitions that cannot be the same thing.</note>

If there is something prior to them that brings them together, that thing must be either the One or not-one. If it is not-one, it must be either many or nothing. But it cannot be many, otherwise we would have a multitude existing before the One. Nor can it be nothing, for how could nothingness bring things together? 

Therefore, it is the One alone. For even this One cannot be many, lest we proceed to infinity. There is, therefore, the **One-itself**; and every multitude proceeds from the **One-itself**.

<summary>Proclus provides a logical proof that Unity (the One) must exist before Multiplicity (the Many). He argues that without a primary "One-itself," any group of things would be infinitely divisible and thus could never truly exist.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, Multitude, Ipsunum, Unity, Infinite Regress, Participation</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general nature of the One to how the One constitutes all many-ness (Proposition VI) and introduces the principle of hierarchy between causes and their effects (Proposition VII), establishing the "chain of being."</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 2

## PROPOSITION VI
Every multitude is composed either of **unified things** or of **unities**.
<note>original Latin: "OMnis multitudo, vel ex vnitis eſt, vel ex vnitatibus."</note>

### DEMONSTRATION
For it is clear that each part of a "many" is not merely a multitude itself; and likewise, it is clear that every part of that secondary multitude is not just a multitude. If a thing is not merely a multitude, it must be either a **unified thing** <note>A "unified thing" is a single object that participates in oneness but is made of parts, like a body made of limbs.</note> or **unities** <note>In Proclus’s philosophy, "unities" (or *henads*) are the most fundamental, indivisible units of existence that come directly from the "One."</note>. 

Indeed, if it participates in the One, it is a "unified thing." But if it is the source from which the unified thing is first composed, then it consists of "unities." For if there is the "One itself," there must also be that which first participates in it and is first unified. This primary unified thing is composed of unities. If it were instead composed of other unified things, those in turn would be composed of something else, and this would continue to infinity. However, it is necessary for the first unified thing to be composed of unities. And thus, we have found what we initially proposed.

->**ON PRODUCERS AND PRODUCTS**<-
<note>In this context, "producing" refers to the way a higher cause—like a sun or a mind—generates a lower effect. This is the foundation of the Neoplatonic hierarchy of reality.</note>

## PROPOSITION VII
Every producer of another thing is superior in nature to the thing it produces.
<note>original Latin: "OMne productiuum alterius, præſtantius eſt natura producti."</note>

### DEMONSTRATION
For the producer is either superior, inferior, or equal to the product. Let us first assume it is equal. In this case, that which is produced by this cause either has the power to produce something else itself, or it exists as something entirely sterile. 

But if it were sterile, it would be, by that very fact, inferior to its producer; it would be unequal to that which is fruitful and possesses the power to act, since the product itself would be ineffective. 

If, however, the product is also a producer of other things, it either produces something equal to itself (and this would be the same for all things, and all beings would be equal to one another, and nothing would be superior to anything else, since a producer would always establish a product equal to itself) or it produces something unequal. 

If it produces something unequal, it would no longer be equal to its own producer. For it is the nature of equal powers to produce equal effects. Yet the things resulting from these would be unequal to one another, granted that the producer is equal to what comes before it, but unequal to what comes after it.
B Not <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page, continuing the logical proof on the following leaf.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that all groups must be made of fundamental units and establishes the "Law of Causality," stating that a cause is always more powerful than its effect.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, The One, Unities, Henads, Causality, Neoplatonism, Logic, Hierarchy of Being</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page concludes the proof of Proposition VII regarding the hierarchy of causes and introduces Proposition VIII, which defines the "First Good" as the supreme principle above all existence.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as "The Successor" (Diadochus) because he headed the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens, following the lineage of Plato.</note>

Therefore, it is not necessary for the product to be equal to the producer. On the contrary, the producer will never be inferior to the product. For if the producer grants essence to the product, it also bestows power according to that essence. Now, if the producer is the source of all the power belonging to the thing that follows it, the producer would also be able to make itself such as that product is. If this were so, the producer would also make itself even more powerful. For "inability" does not prevent it, since a creative power is present; nor does a "lack of will" prevent it, for all things by nature desire the good.

Thus, if a cause can make another thing more perfect, it would also have made itself perfect before perfecting that which comes after it. Therefore, the product is neither equal to the producer, nor is it superior. In every case, the producer is superior to the nature of the product.

## ON THE FIRST GOOD, WHICH IS ALSO CALLED THE GOOD ITSELF.

### PROPOSITION VIII
**O**f all things that participate in the good in any way, the first good—which is nothing other than the good itself—takes precedence. <margin>O.</margin>
<note>original Latin: "OMnib. quomodocunque bonum participantibus, præcedit primo bonum, & quod nihil eſt aliud, quàm bonum."</note>

### DEMONSTRATION
**S**ince all beings <term>beings: things that exist in the realm of intellect or physical reality</term> desire the good, it is clear that the first good is above beings. For if the good were the same as any particular being, then that being would no longer be a desirer of the good, since it would itself exist as the Good itself. However, whatever desires something is in need of that which it desires; it is distinct from and foreign to the object of its desire. 

Either they are one and the same, or they are different. If they are different, then the being will participate in the good, and the Good itself will be the thing "participated in" within that being. In that case, there is a certain "good" found within a particular participant which only that specific participant desires. But that is not the "Good in the simple sense," <note>original Latin: "ſimpliciter bonum"</note> which all beings desire. For the universal good is the common object of desire for all beings, whereas a good that is generated within a specific thing belongs to that participant alone. 

Therefore, the First Good is nothing other than "The Good." For if you add any other quality to it, you will diminish the Good by that very addition; you would be making it a *specific* good instead of the Good which is simply and absolutely the Good.

<summary>This page concludes the argument that causes are naturally superior to their effects and argues that the supreme "Good" must exist as a pure principle above all individual beings.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The Good, causality, participation, desire, essence, perfection, hierarchy of being</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues from the conclusion of Proposition VIII, where Proclus argues that the First Good must be unmixed and superior to all things that merely participate in it. It transitions into Proposition IX and X, which define the nature of "self-sufficiency" in the hierarchy of being.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 3

For that which is added—since it is not the good itself, but something less than it—by its own essence diminishes the good itself. <note>original Latin: "Quod enim additur cum non sit bonum ipsum, sed minus illo, sua essentia bonum ipsum diminuit." Proclus argues that adding any specific quality to "The Good" actually limits it, making it "less" than the absolute, pure source.</note>

## ON THE SELF-SUFFICIENT.
<note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, "self-sufficiency" (autarkeia) refers to a being's ability to provide for its own existence or perfection without relying on a lower or external cause. However, it is still inferior to the absolute First Principle.</note>

### PROPOSITION IX.

**E**very <term>self-sufficient: a being that contains the source of its own completion or "good" within itself</term> thing, whether according to its essence or according to its action, is superior to the non-self-sufficient, which has the cause of its perfection depending on another cause.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**I**f all beings naturally desire the good, and one thing provides that good for itself while another stands in need of another for it, then the former surely possesses the cause of the good within itself, while the latter has that cause existing separately. The closer that which provides the object of desire is to the thing desiring it, the more superior that thing will be to the one which requires a separate cause and receives the perfection of its existence or action from an outside source. 

Furthermore, the self-sufficient is both similar to and a diminished version of the Good itself. It is "diminished" because it merely participates in the good rather than being the First Good itself. Yet, it is somehow related to the First Good insofar as it is able to possess the good through its own power. However, a being that participates in the good only through another is more distant from the First Good—which is nothing other than the good itself, etc.

### PROPOSITION X.

**E**very self-sufficient thing is inferior to the <term>Simply Good: the absolute, unmixed source of all goodness, which Proclus identifies as "The One"</term>.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or what else is the "self-sufficient" but that which possesses the good with itself and in itself? Such a thing is already full of the good and is a participant in it. But it is not the *Simply Good* itself. For the Simply Good is superior to both "participating in the good" and "being full of the good," as has been demonstrated. 

If, therefore, the self-sufficient has filled itself with the good, that source from which it filled itself must be more excellent than the self-sufficient thing and stand above self-sufficiency. Moreover, the Simply Good is not in need of anything. It does not desire anything else; for if it did, it would be deficient in the good according to the measure of its desire,

<summary>Proclus defines self-sufficiency as the ability of a being to possess its own "good" within itself, though he clarifies that even self-sufficient beings are inferior to the absolute "Simply Good."</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, self-sufficiency, The Good, hierarchy of being, Neoplatonism, participation, perfection</keywords>

PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
...neither sufficient; for it would be full of the good, and not the first good itself. <note>original Latin: "esset enim plenum bono, & non ipsum bonum primo." Proclus distinguishes between the absolute "Good" and a being that is merely "filled with good."</note>

## ON THE CAUSE.

### PROPOSITION XI.

**A**ll beings proceed from one first cause.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or either there is no cause for beings; or causes move in a circle (since all things are finite); or there is an ascent to infinity, where one thing is the cause of another, and the pre-existing essence will never come to a halt. 

But if there were no cause for beings, there would be no order of secondary and primary things; of those that perfect and those that are perfected; of those that adorn and those that are adorned; of those that generate and those that are generated; or of agents and patients. <note>Proclus is describing the Great Chain of Being, where every level of reality has a specific relationship to the levels above and below it.</note> Nor would there be any <term>science: in the classical sense, "scientia" refers to certain knowledge obtained by understanding the underlying causes of a thing</term> of beings. For the work of science is the knowledge of causes; we say that we know when we recognize the causes of beings. 

If, however, causes move in a circle, the same things will be both prior and posterior, and both more powerful and weaker. For every producing cause is superior to the nature of the thing produced. It makes no difference whether one joins the cause to the effect through many or few intermediate steps to make the effect subordinate to it. For a cause will be superior to all the intermediates of which it is the cause; and the more intermediates there are, the more it is a cause. 

If, on the other hand, the addition of causes goes to infinity, and one thing always follows from another, again there will be no science of anything. For there is no knowledge of the infinite. If the causes are unknown, there will be no science of the things that follow from them. 

If, therefore, there must be a cause of beings, and causes are distinct from their effects, and there is no ascent to infinity, there must be a first cause of beings, from which, as if from a root, <note>The "root" metaphor is a favorite of Neoplatonists to describe how the many (branches/leaves) depend on the One (the root) while remaining a single organism.</note> individual things proceed—some existing near it, and others further away. For it has already been demonstrated that there must be a single principle, because every multitude that follows it subsists from the One.

<note>Printer's signature mark: 2 u</note>

->DE<- <note>Catchword for the following page: "ON [THE UNITY...]"</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that all existence must originate from a single First Cause, refuting theories of infinite regress or circular causation to maintain the possibility of scientific knowledge.</summary>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 4

## ON THE GOOD.

### PROPOSITION XII.

**T**he principle and most primary cause of all beings is the Good itself.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if all things proceed from a single cause, we must call that cause either the Good itself, or something superior to the Good itself. 

But if that cause is superior to the Good, does anything pass from it into beings and into the nature of beings, or does nothing? If indeed nothing passes from it, the idea is absurd; for we would no longer be preserving that cause in the rank of a "cause," since something from the cause must be present everywhere in the effects—and especially from the most primary cause, upon which all things depend and through which each individual being exists. 

If, however, beings also participate in that cause just as they participate in the Good itself, there would be something superior to goodness penetrating into beings from that most primary cause. For since it is superior to and above the Good itself, it would not bestow something inferior upon its secondary effects than the gifts which the being following it <note>The "being following it" refers to the next level down in the divine hierarchy.</note> bestows. 

Yet what could possibly be superior to goodness itself? For we use the very word "SUPERIOR" to describe that which participates more greatly in the good. Therefore, if that which is not "good" cannot be called superior to the Good itself, then it is entirely necessary that the first cause be the Good. Furthermore, if all beings desire the Good, how can there be anything before this cause? For if that "superior" cause also desires the Good, how can it be the Good in the highest degree? But if it does not desire it, how can beings—which have proceeded from it—desire a cause that is not their own? <note>Proclus argues from the Platonic principle that all things naturally "strive" or "turn back" toward their source; if they strive for the Good, their source must be the Good.</note> If, therefore, the Good itself is that upon which all beings depend, then the principle and most primary cause of all is the Good itself.

### PROPOSITION XIII.

**E**very good is a unifying force for those things which participate in it, and every union is a good; and the Good itself is the same as the One itself.

**DEMON-**

<summary>Proclus argues that the ultimate source of all reality must be "the Good," as nothing can be superior to goodness, and concludes that goodness and unity (the One) are fundamentally the same.</summary>
<keywords>The Good, First Cause, Principle, Participation, Unity, The One, Neoplatonism, Causality</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the nature of the "First Cause." Having established that the First Cause is the Good (Proposition XII), Proclus now moves to Proposition XIII to prove that the Good and "The One" are identical, followed by Proposition XIV which begins the classification of motion.</meta>

PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI"</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if the good is that which preserves all beings, and for that reason is desired by all, then that which preserves and sustains the essence of individual things is "the one" itself. (For it is by being "one" that all things are preserved) and dispersion <note>The state of being scattered or divided.</note> moves individual things away from their essence. But the good itself makes those things in which it is present "one," and contains them through union. But if the one is that which gathers and contains, it will perfect each individual being through its presence. Therefore, for all things, it is a good to be unified. If, however, union is a good in itself, and the good is a thing that creates unity, then the "simply good" and the "simply one" are the same. This principle simultaneously unifies beings and makes them good. For this reason, those things which in some way fall away from the good itself are also deprived of participation in the one; and those things which become devoid of the one, being filled with separation, are likewise deprived of the good in the same way.

### COROLLARY.
**T**herefore, goodness is union and union is goodness; and the good is the one, and the one is the primary good.

## ON THE IMMOVABLE AND SELF-MOVING PRINCIPLE, OR CAUSE.

### PROPOSITION XIV.
**E**very being is either immovable or moved; and if it is moved, it is moved either by itself or by another.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**A**nd if it is moved by itself, it is self-moving; if however it is moved by another, it is (so to speak) "other-moved." Therefore, everything is either immovable, self-moving, or other-moved. For it is necessary, since there are things moved by others, that there be both an immovable thing and, between these, a self-moving thing. For if every other-moved thing is moved by something else that is itself moved, then motions proceed either in a circle or to infinity. But they move neither in a circle nor to infinity, since all beings are defined by a principle <note>Proclus argues that an infinite chain of causes is impossible; there must be a starting point.</note>, and the mover is superior to the thing moved. Therefore, there will be something immovable which is the first mover. But if this exists, it is necessary that there also be a self-moving thing. For if the universe were at a standstill, what then would be the first thing to be moved? It would not be the immovable itself, for it is not by nature suited to be moved. Nor would it be the other-moved, for that is moved by something else. It remains, therefore, that the self-moving is that which is first <note>The text ends mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>This page identifies "The Good" with "The One" as the preserving force of all existence and introduces the hierarchy of motion, arguing for a "First Mover" that is immovable.</summary>

...ther moved first, since this is what joins those things that are moved by another <note>original: "alimobilia"</note> to the immovable, existing as a kind of middle ground, both moving and being moved at once. For of those other two, one only moves [the immovable], while the other is only moved [the other-moved]. Therefore, every being is either immovable, self-moving, or moved by another.

### COROLLARY.
**F**rom these points it is surely clear that of those things which are moved, the self-moving is the primary; but of those things which move, the immovable is the primary.

## ON INCORPOREAL ESSENCE, AND WHAT IT IS <note>The Latin "ET QUID" likely stands for "ET QUID SIT," meaning "and what it is."</note>

### PROPOSITION XV.
**E**verything that turns back upon itself <note>This refers to the concept of *epistrophē* or "reversion," where a higher being or soul directs its consciousness and activity toward its own essence rather than toward external, physical things.</note> is incorporeal.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or no body is by its nature capable of turning back upon itself. If that which turns toward something is joined to that toward which it turns, it is surely clear that all parts of the body would have to be joined to all its other parts. This is what it means to "turn back upon itself": when both the thing that turns and the thing it turns toward become one and the same. However, this is impossible for a body, and generally for all things that can be divided into parts. In a divisible thing, the whole cannot be joined to its whole self because of the separation of its parts, some of which are located in different places. Therefore, no body is capable of turning back upon itself such that the whole is directed toward the whole. Thus, if anything is self-reverting, it must be incorporeal and indivisible.

### PROPOSITION XVI.
**E**verything that turns back upon itself has an essence <note>original: "essentiam," referring to its fundamental being or existence.</note> that is separate from every body.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if it were inseparable from any body, it would not possess any activity that is separable from the body. It is impossible for...

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens, succeeding the lineage of Plato.</note>

...[self], for if the essence were inseparable from bodies, it would be impossible for its essential activity to be separable. For in that case, the activity would be more excellent <note>original: "præſtantior"</note> than the essence. This would follow if the essence required bodies to exist, while the activity was self-sufficient; the activity would belong to the being itself and not to the bodies. Therefore, if something is inseparable according to its essence, it is likewise—or even more so—inseparable according to its activity. But if this is the case, it cannot turn back upon itself. For that which turns back upon itself, being something other than a body, possesses an activity separate from the body; its action occurs neither through the body nor in conjunction with the body. This is because the activity itself, and the object toward which that activity is directed, have no need for the body. Therefore, any being that turns back upon itself <note>This "turning back" or <term>reversion</term> (Greek: *epistrophē*) describes how a soul or intellect gains self-knowledge by looking inward at its own nature rather than outward at the physical world.</note> is entirely separable from bodies.

## PROPOSITION XVII.
**E**verything that is primarily self-moving is capable of turning back upon itself.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if it moves itself, its motive activity is also directed toward itself, and the mover and the moved are one and the same. For either it moves by one part and is moved by another part, or the whole moves and is moved, or the whole...

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs in Proclus’s Elements of Theology. # ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 6

...the producer <note>original: "conditor"</note> of anything is more excellent than the nature of the thing produced; therefore, that which pre-exists in the giver is more sublime than the gift itself. And it is indeed that which the gift is, but it is not identical to it. For the former exists primarily, while the latter exists secondarily. For it is necessary that either both are the same and there is one definition <note>original: "rationem," referring to the formal principle or 'account' of a thing.</note> for both, or there is nothing common between them, or the same thing exists in both, but in the one primarily and in the other secondarily. But if the definition were the same, then the one would no longer be the cause and the other the effect; nor would the one exist in itself and the other in the recipient; nor would one be the maker and the other the thing made. If, however, they have nothing in common, then the existence of the one would in no way depend on the other, for it would communicate nothing to the other’s being. It remains, therefore, that what is bestowed exists primarily in the giver, and secondarily in that which is bestowed; in these cases, that which constitutes the being of the one is given to the other.

## PROPOSITION XIX.
**E**verything that primarily exists within any nature of beings is present to all things ordered according to that nature, by one principle and in the same manner.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if it were not present to all in the same way, but to some and not to others, it is clear that it did not exist in that nature primarily. Rather, it would exist in some primarily, and in others secondarily, which participate in it only at certain times. For that which exists at one time but not at another does not exist primarily nor by its own nature; instead, it is adventitious <note>original: "aduenticium," meaning something coming from the outside rather than being inherent to the thing's essence.</note> and comes from elsewhere to those things in which it is found in such a way, etc.

**S**uperior to all bodies is the essence of the soul; and superior to all souls is the intellectual nature; and superior to all intellectual substances is the One itself.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or every body is moved by another, for it is not its nature to move itself. But it is moved by the participation of a soul,

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus’s systematic hierarchy of reality. It completes Proposition XX, which establishes the ladder of existence from Body to Soul, Soul to Intellect (Mind), and Intellect to the One. It then begins Proposition XXI, focusing on the relationship between a unified principle and the "multitude" or series that follows from it.</meta>

...[it proceeds] from itself, and lives through the soul; and indeed, while the soul is present, it is **self-moving** in a certain way, but when the soul is absent, it is **moved by another** <note>original: "alimobile"</note>. It is as if the body possesses this dependent nature, while the soul has been allotted a self-moving essence. For to whatever the soul joins, it imparts this self-mobility. Now, that which is imparted by a thing’s very being <note>The essence of the giver is the source of the gift.</note> must exist in the giver much earlier and more fundamentally. Therefore, the soul is above bodies, being self-moving by its very essence, while bodies are made self-moving only through participation in the soul. 

Again, since the soul is moved by itself, it occupies the second rank after the **immobile nature** <note>The "immobile nature" refers to the Intellect, which acts without changing itself.</note>, which is an immobile activity. This is because for all things that are moved, the self-moving must come first; but for all things that move others, the immobile must come first. If, therefore, the soul is moved by itself and moves other things, there must be an **immobile mover** existing before it. This is the **Mind** <note>term: Mind (Latin: *Mens* / Greek: *Nous*). In Neoplatonism, this is the realm of pure thought and the Platonic Forms.</note>, which moves while being immobile itself, always acting in the same way. 

Indeed, the soul participates in "always understanding" through the Mind, just as the body participates in "self-motion" through the soul. For if the capacity for eternal understanding resided primarily in the soul, it would be present in all souls, just as the capacity for self-motion is. Therefore, this capacity is not primary to the soul. It follows that there must be something prior to the soul that is primarily intellectual. Thus, the Mind exists before souls. 

But even before the Mind, there is the **One**. For the Mind, even though it is immobile, is not strictly "One." It understands itself and acts upon itself <note>Because the Mind is both the "thinker" and the "object of thought," it possesses a basic duality and cannot be the absolute One.</note>. Moreover, all things whatsoever participate in the One, but not all things participate in the Mind. For only those who possess a portion of Mind must necessarily participate in knowledge, because intellectual knowledge is the principle and first cause of knowing. Therefore, the One is superior to the Mind. Nor is there anything higher than the One. For the One and the **Good** itself are the same. And the Good itself is the principle of all things, as has been demonstrated.

## THAT MIND IS NOT THE FIRST CAUSE.

### PROPOSITION XXI.
**E**very order, beginning from a unity, proceeds into a certain multitude associated with that unity; and every multitude of an order is reduced back into one unity.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or unity, possessing the character of a principle <note>original: "principij habens rationem"</note>, brings forth a multitude proper to itself. Therefore, one chain, and one order...

<summary>Proclus establishes the ontological hierarchy: Body is moved by Soul, Soul is illuminated by Mind (Intellect), and Mind is unified by the One/the Good. He then begins to explain how every structured "order" of reality must originate from a single source.</summary>
<keywords>self-moving, immobile mover, Mind, Intellect, the One, the Good, participation, hierarchy of being, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*. Having established the hierarchy from Body to Soul to Mind (Intellect), the text now explores Proposition XXI, which focuses on how every "order" or "chain" of existence derives from a single, unified source. It transitions into Proposition XXII, which asserts the unique, "only-begotten" nature of the primary principle at the head of every level of reality.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 7

Every order, in its entirety, has its descent from unity into multiplicity. For there would be neither order nor a chain <note>term: chain (Latin: *catena* / Greek: *seira*). In Neoplatonism, this refers to a series of beings that "hang" from a single divine source, like links in a chain, sharing a common character.</note> if the unity were sterile in itself. Multitude, however, is traced back again to one common cause of all things coordinated within it. For that which is the same in every multitude did not proceed from one of those members within the multitude itself. For what comes from one among many is not common to all, but is peculiar only to the specific property of that one. 

Since, therefore, in every order there is a certain communion, coherence, and identity—because of which some things are said to be coordinated and others to belong to a different order—it is clear that for every order, the state of "being the same" proceeds from a single principle. There is, then, one unity before the multitude in every order, providing a single rationale <note>original: *rationem* / Greek: *logon*</note> and series to those arranged within it, both toward each other and toward the whole. While one thing may be the cause of another among those under the same chain, it is necessary that there be a cause for the chain being **one** before all things; and from this cause, all things are born as coordinated members—not insofar as each is this or that particular thing, but insofar as it exists as part of this specific order.

## COROLLARY.

From these points it is certainly clear that both unity and multitude exist within the nature of the body; one nature has many coherent parts, and many natures depend upon the one nature of the universe. This is also present in the order of souls: starting from one first soul, descending into a multitude of souls, and leading that multitude back to the one. And in intellectual essence, there is an intellectual unity; a multitude of minds proceeds from one Mind and is turned back <note>term: turned back (Latin: *conversa* / Greek: *epistrophe*). The Neoplatonic concept where a created thing "reverts" or looks back toward its source to find its purpose and perfection.</note> toward it. And for the One that exists before all things, there is a multitude of unities <note>term: unities (Latin: *unitates* / Greek: *henads*). These are the highest principles in Proclus's system, acting as bridges between the Absolute One and the rest of existence.</note>, and for those unities, a return into the One. Therefore, after the One which is first, come the Unities; after the first Mind, come minds; after the first Soul, come souls; and after the universal Nature, come natures.

## PROPOSITION. XXII.

Everything that exists primarily and principally within each order is one, not two, nor more than two. Rather, everything [of this kind] is only-begotten <note>original: *unigenitum*. In this context, it means a unique principle that has no equal at its own level of the hierarchy.</note>.

<summary>This page explains that every level of reality (Body, Soul, Mind) forms a "chain" that must originate from a single, unique unity. It establishes that multiplicity always flows from a primary, "only-begotten" principle that provides the entire group with its shared identity.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, unity, multitude, chain, Henads, Mind, Soul, only-begotten, Neoplatonism, hierarchy of being</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s Elements of Theology, moving from the necessity of a single source for every chain of existence (Proposition XXI) to the impossibility of multiple primary causes (Proposition XXII). It concludes with the introduction of Proposition XXIII regarding the relationship between unparticipated principles and their participants.</meta>

# BY PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

### DEMONSTRATION.

Suppose, for the sake of argument, that there are two primary causes—or indeed, even if we grant there to be many. This is impossible. For the same impossibility holds even if there are several. Either each of these is what it is from both of them together; but if it comes from both, then that combined source would be the one primary thing, and there would not be two first causes. If, however, they are one because one comes from the other, then both are not primary. Or, suppose both are equal. But if they are equal, neither will now be primary. For if one were primary, but was not the same as the other, what would be the nature of that order? That which is nothing other than what it is said to be, that is what is primary <note>The author argues that a "primary" thing must be pure and simple. If it has to "share" its primacy with another, it is no longer the absolute first.</note>. If these two differ from one another, they do not differ insofar as they are "primary." For if this "primary" quality is the same in both, then they are not both primary, but rather that single quality in which they both participate is the thing said to be primary.

### COROLLARY.

From these points, it is surely clear that the primary Being is one alone, and there are not two primary beings, or many. And the most primary Intellect <note>original: *mens* / Greek: *nous*. In Neoplatonism, the Intellect is the second hypostasis, the realm of pure thought and the Platonic Forms.</note> is one alone, and not two primary intellects. And the most primary Soul is one. The same holds for each individual species: such as the primary Beauty, the primary Equal, and similarly in all things. Thus, even the form of Animal <note>This refers to the "Idea" or "Form" of a Living Being, of which all physical animals are reflections.</note> is one and primary, as is the form of Man; for the demonstration is the same.

## ON THE UNPARTICIPATED, THE PARTICIPANT, AND THE PARTICIPATED.

### PROPOSITION XXIII.

Every unparticipated principle produces participants from itself; and all participating substances are traced back to unparticipated existences <note>term: unparticipated (*imparticipabile* / *amethekton*). This refers to a transcendental principle that remains "aloof" or pure, while "participated" principles are those that actually enter into and animate lower things.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For the unparticipated, possessing the character of unity as existing of itself and not of another, and as being separate from participants...

<summary>This page demonstrates that every level of reality (Being, Intellect, Soul) must have a single, unique source rather than multiple primary causes, and introduces the distinction between "unparticipated" transcendental principles and the beings that participate in them.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, primary cause, unity, Intellect, Soul, Forms, Unparticipated, Participant, Neoplatonism, logic</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proof of Proposition XXIII, which establishes that every "unparticipated" principle generates "participated" ones. The text then transitions into Proposition XXIV, detailing the ontological hierarchy between the unparticipated, the participated, and the participant.</meta>

...[from the previous page: ...And the most primary Soul is one alone, and not two primary souls. For it is from these primary sources that the Monad] generates those beings which are able to participate. For either a principle will remain sterile, persisting within itself and possessing nothing honorable, or it will give something of itself. And that which receives this gift has participated, and that which is given exists in a participating manner.

Truly, everything that participates in something is secondary to that which allows it to be a participant, for the latter is present to all things equally and fills all things from itself. For that which is present in only one individual is not in others; but that which is present to all equally, so as to illuminate all, is not in one, but exists before all. 

For a principle is either in all things, or in one among all, or before all. But that which is in all, being divided among all, would in turn require something else to unite that which is divided, and thus all things would no longer participate in the same thing. Rather, this individual would have one part, and that individual another, if the "one" were divided. If, however, the principle were in only one among all, it would no longer belong to all, but only to that one. If it is indeed common to those able to participate, and the same for all, it must be before all; and this is the "unparticipated" <term>unparticipated: *imparticipabile* / *amethekton*. In Proclus's system, this is a principle that remains pure and distinct from the things that depend on it, acting as a source without being "divided" among its effects.</term>.

->PROPOSITION XXIV.<-

**Everything that participates is inferior to that which is participated, and that which is participated is inferior to the unparticipated.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For the participant, being imperfect before its participation but made perfect by it, is entirely secondary to the participated thing, insofar as it is made perfect by it. In the respect that the participant was imperfect, it is inferior to the participated thing which makes it perfect. 

Furthermore, the participated thing—which belongs to a specific thing and not to all <note>original: *alicuius existens, & non omnium*</note>—possesses a more diminished existence than that which belongs to all and not to just one. For the latter [the unparticipated] is more closely akin to the Cause of all, while the former [the participated] is less akin. 

Therefore, the unparticipated precedes the participated things, and these in turn precede the participants. And to state it briefly: The unparticipated is the "one before the many"; the participated is "one and not-one" <note>It is 'one' as a distinct quality, but 'not-one' because it is shared and distributed among many participants.</note> at the same time; and every participant is "not-one and one" at the same time.

<summary>This page concludes the argument that universal principles must exist prior to the things that share in them, establishing a three-tiered hierarchy: the pure source (unparticipated), the shared quality (participated), and the individual thing (participant).</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, participation, unparticipated, Neoplatonism, hierarchy of being, The One and the Many, ontological perfection</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus's *Elements of Theology*. Having previously discussed the relationship between unparticipated and participated principles, the author now addresses Proposition XXV, focusing on how perfection necessitates productivity. This leads to the conclusion that the ultimate principle (The One/The Good) is the most productive, while its opposite (Matter) is sterile.</meta>

# 8 PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

->ON THE PERFECT.<-

## PROPOSITION XXV.

**Every perfect thing proceeds into offspring which it is capable of producing, imitating the one principle of the universe.** <note>original: "vnum vniuersorum principiū"</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For as that principle, on account of its own goodness, is the uniform creator of all beings <note>original: "vniformiter constitutiuum est." The "One" acts as a single source for the diversity of existence.</note>—for the Good itself and the One itself are the same, such that "good-form" and "one-form" are also the same—so also those things which exist after it, by virtue of their own perfection, hasten to generate other things, without diminishing their own essence. <note>In Neoplatonism, the act of creation or emanation does not "use up" the source; the higher remains full while the lower is born.</note> This perfection is a certain portion of the Good itself; and the perfect, insofar as it is perfect, imitates the Good. 

The Good was indeed the creator of all things. Therefore, the perfect is also productive of those things which it is capable of producing according to its nature. And certainly, the more perfect it is, the more things it is the cause of. For the more perfect a thing is, the more it participates in the Good itself. That which is closer to the Good is more kindred to the universal cause, and that cause is the cause of the most things. 

The more imperfect a thing is, the more it is the cause of fewer things. For being far removed from the producer of all things, it is capable of procreating only a few. For to procreate all things—or to adorn them, or to perfect them, or to contain them, or to bring them to life, or to effect them—and to do these individual acts across many things, is kindred to that First Principle. But if these things should happen in only a few instances, it is more "alien" or distant from it, etc.

### COROLLARY.

From these points it is surely clear that matter, which is the furthest removed from the principle of the universe, is sterile and the cause of nothing. <term>matter: *materia* / *hyle*. In this philosophical tradition, "matter" is the absolute bottom of the hierarchy, possessing no power to organize or create, only to receive form.</term> For if it were to beget anything, it would also have something following after it; and it is clear that it would then not be the furthest removed. Instead, that which it produced would be further than itself, and matter itself would be closer to the source because it produced something and imitated the productive cause of all beings.

<column-break/>

<summary>Proclus argues that perfection is naturally productive, imitating the Good by generating offspring, whereas matter, being the most distant from the Good, is entirely sterile.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, The One, The Good, perfection, emanation, productivity, matter, Neoplatonism, causality</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s Elements of Theology. Following Proposition XXV, which established that every perfect thing is naturally productive, Proposition XXVI explains the mechanism of this production: a cause does not exhaust or change itself when generating an effect, but rather "remains" within its own essence.</meta>

# 9 THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS

## ON THE PRODUCTIVE CAUSE.

### PROPOSITION XXVI.

**Every productive cause of other things, while remaining in itself, produces those things which follow it in order.** <note>original: "Omnis productiua aliorum causa manēs ipsa in se ipsa." This introduces the vital Neoplatonic concept of "remaining" (mone), where a higher principle generates a lower one without losing any of its own substance or power.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if a cause imitates the One itself, and the One produces the things that follow it without undergoing any change, then every producing cause must also possess its power of producing in that same manner. Indeed, the One itself creates immovably. <note>original: "immobiliter procreat." In this context, "immovably" means without internal change, effort, or loss of identity.</note> 

If it produced through motion, that motion would either be within the One or not within it. If there were motion within it, that which is moved would no longer be "The One," having changed from its unified state; furthermore, that motion itself would have to proceed from the One. This leads either to an infinite regression of causes or to the conclusion that the One produces immovably. 

Thus, every producer will imitate the One and the universal productive cause. <note>original: "vniuer forum" likely intended as "vniuersorum."</note> For from that which is "primary," that which is "not-primary" arises. Likewise, from that which is productive of all things comes that which is productive of only some things. 

Therefore, every producer, by remaining in itself, produces the things that follow in succession. While the producers remain within themselves without being diminished, secondary things are brought forth from them. <note>In the philosophy of Proclus, "diminishment" would imply that the cause becomes less than it was by giving birth to an effect. He argues here that true causes are inexhaustible.</note> For it is impossible for something that is diminished in any way to remain exactly as it is.

### PROPOSITION XXVII.

<summary>Proposition XXVI argues that all causes generate their effects without undergoing internal change or loss of essence, imitating the "immovable" creative nature of the One.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, emanation, The One, causality, remaining, procession, immovability</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page concludes Proposition XXVII and provides the full text of Proposition XXVIII of the Elements of Theology. These propositions explore the relationship between a cause and its effect, specifically focusing on how a producer remains unchanged while generating "secondary substances" and why the first things produced must resemble their source.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

...of the producer, is the product itself. For this quality of being produced does not belong to those things which have already been generated, nor to the causes that produce them. Nor is there a "transition," for the matter is not the result of a transformation. <note>original: "non enim materia fit progressi." Proclus is arguing that creation isn't like a physical change where one thing turns into another, but a procession where the source stays the same.</note> It remains exactly as it is. And the product is something distinct from it. The generator, therefore, remains unalterable and undiminished, multiplying itself through its overflowing power and providing secondary substances from its own being.

### PROPOSITION XXVIII.

**Every producer brings forth things similar to itself before it produces things that are dissimilar.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

Since every producer is necessarily superior to what it produces, the two can never be simply identical or equal in power. But if they are not identical and equal, but rather different and unequal, they must either be entirely separate from one another, or both united and separate.

However, if they were entirely separate, they would not fit together; in no way would that which comes from a cause be in "sympathy" with its cause. <note>The term "sympathy" (sympathes) here refers to a natural affinity or a cosmic "attunement" between different levels of reality.</note> Therefore, one would not participate in the other, since they would be entirely different. For a "participated" thing grants a share of itself to the "participant." <note><term>Participation</term> is the Neoplatonic idea that lower things derive their existence or qualities by "sharing" in the nature of a higher principle, like a shadow participating in the shape of the object that casts it.</note> Yet it is necessary that the effect participate in its cause, insofar as it derives its very essence from it.

If, on the other hand, the product is partly separate from and partly united to its producer: if it experiences both equally, it will both participate and not participate in its cause in equal measure. Thus, it would both have its essence from the cause and not have it, in the same manner—which is a contradiction. 

If it were more separate than united, the thing generated would be more alienated from its generator than akin to it; it would be more "out of tune" than harmonious, and more "unsympathetic" than sympathetic. 

Therefore, if the things that come from causes are related to them in their being, are in sympathy with them, and depend upon them by nature, they must "strive for contact" with those causes. <note>original: "appetunt contactum." This describes a natural, ontological gravity where effects "long" to return to their source.</note> In striving for the <term>Good</term> <note>The "Good" is the ultimate source of all reality in Proclus’s system; all things naturally seek to return to it.</note> and attaining what is desirable through their cause, it is clearly evident that products are united to their producers more than they are separated from them. 

Things that are more united are more similar than they are dissimilar to those things with which they are most united. Therefore, every productive cause brings forth similar things before it brings forth the dissimilar.

<summary>Proclus argues that because an effect must "participate" in its cause to exist, it must naturally resemble its cause more than it differs from it, establishing a hierarchy of similarity in the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, causality, participation, The Good, similarity, emanation, secondary substances</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus’s systematic deduction of the metaphysical laws governing the universe. Having established that causes remain undiminished while producing, he now defines the mechanics of this production: "likeness," "remaining," "proceeding," and the beginning of "reversion"—the fundamental triad of Neoplatonic thought.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 10

### PROPOSITION XXIX.

**Every procession is accomplished through a likeness of the secondary things to the primary things.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

If the producer brings forth things that are like itself before it produces things that are unlike, then it is through likeness that the producers generate the products. <note>original: "similitudo a producentibus procreat producta." In Proclus’s system, "likeness" acts as the bridge that allows a higher reality to share its nature with a lower one.</note> For like things are made like through a state of likeness, and not through unlikeness. If, therefore, the procession toward a diminished state <note>This refers to the "descending" levels of reality, where each subsequent step is slightly less "perfect" or "powerful" than the one above it.</note> preserves the identity of the generated to the generator, then that which is produced holds its substance through likeness to the primary thing that brought it forth.

### PROPOSITION XXX.

**Everything produced immediately by another both remains in its producer and proceeds from it.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

If every procession occurs while the primary causes remain still, and is accomplished through likeness—with like things established before unlike things—then the product must also remain, in some sense, within its producer. <note>This is the concept of *monē* (remaining). Even as an effect begins to exist as a separate thing, it never fully "leaves" its cause.</note> For if it proceeded entirely, it would have nothing in common with the thing that remains, but would be totally separate. If, however, it is to have something in common and be united to its cause, it must remain within that cause just as the cause itself remained in its own nature. 

If, on the other hand, the product only "remained" and did not "proceed," it would not differ from its cause, nor would there be a new effect while the cause remains. If it is something "other," it is separate and exists apart. If it is apart, but the cause still remains, then the product has proceeded from the remaining cause in order to be distinguished from it. Therefore, insofar as the product has something identical to its producer, it **remains** in it; but insofar as it is something different, it **proceeds** from it. Since it is "like" its cause, it is both identical and different at the same time. <note>The "likeness" mentioned in Prop. XXIX is the "glue" that allows a thing to be both distinct from its source and yet still anchored within it.</note> It therefore both remains and proceeds simultaneously, and neither state exists apart from the other.

### PROPOSITION XXXI.

**Everything proceeding from something according to its essence reverts to that from which it proceeds.**

D DEMON- <note>The text cuts off here, beginning the demonstration of the concept of *epistrophē* or "reversion"—the natural tendency of all things to turn back toward their source of being.</note>

<summary>Proclus explains that all creation happens through "likeness" and that every effect exists in a state of tension: it simultaneously stays "inside" its cause (remaining) and moves "away" from it (proceeding), eventually seeking to return (reverting).</summary>
<keywords>procession, likeness, remaining, proceeding, reversion, monē, proodos, epistrophē, Neoplatonism, metaphysics, causality</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus’s systematic deduction of the metaphysical laws governing the universe. Having previously discussed "remaining" (monē) and "proceeding" (proodos), he now moves to the third stage of the Neoplatonic triad: "reversion" (epistrophē)—the process by which a created thing turns back toward its source to find its completion.</meta>

### BY PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
### THE DEMONSTRATION.

<note>This text begins mid-argument, completing the demonstration for Proposition 31: "All that proceeds from any principle and returns to it has its goal in that principle."</note>

For if a thing were to proceed forth, but did not turn back toward the cause of this procession, it would not desire its cause. <note>original: "non appeteret causam." In Neoplatonic thought, "desire" is the gravitational pull that draws an effect back to its source.</note> For everything that desires something is turned toward that which is desirable. Furthermore, every being desires the Good, and the attainment of that Good is achieved by each thing through its own proximate cause. Therefore, each thing also desires its own cause. For that through which each thing has its existence is also that through which it has its well-being; <note>original: "bene esse." A distinction between simply existing and "living well" or reaching one's full potential.</note> and that toward which it first has a desire is that toward which its reversion is directed.

### PROPOSITION XXXII.

**Every reversion is accomplished through a likeness between the things returning and that to which they return.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For everything that turns back hastens to join itself to the whole, desiring communion and connection with it. It is likeness that binds all things together, just as unlikeness separates and divides them. Reversion, therefore, is a kind of communion and contact. But all communion and all contact occur through likeness. Every reversion, therefore, is accomplished through likeness. <note>Proclus argues that a soul or intellect can only return to its source if it makes itself "like" that source, essentially tuning itself to a higher frequency.</note>

### PROPOSITION XXXIII.

**Everything proceeding from something and returning to it has a circular activity.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

If a thing returns to that from which it proceeds, it joins the end to the beginning; and the movement is one and continuous, being made partly by "remaining" <note>original: "a manente." This refers to the cause staying still while it produces.</note> and partly by returning toward that which remains. From this, it is clear that all things proceed in a circle from causes back to causes. There are greater circles and smaller circles: some reversions are made to the things immediately above them, while others are to higher principles, extending even to the Principle of All things. <note>original: "omnium principium." The One, or the ultimate source of reality.</note> For from that Principle all things come, and toward it all things return.

### PROPOSITION XXXIV.

**Everything that returns according to its nature makes its reversion toward that very thing from which it received the procession of its own substance.**

### DEMON-
<note>The text cuts off mid-word "DEMONSTRATIO."</note>
