<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 32 and introduces the fundamental Neoplatonic triad of "remaining, proceeding, and reverting" in Proposition 35. The text establishes that all things in the universe maintain a sympathetic connection to their source through their inherent likeness to it.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 11

### THE DEMONSTRATION.
For if a thing reverts <note>original: "conuertitur." This refers to *epistrophē*, the process by which a created thing seeks its source.</note> according to its own nature, it possesses an essential appetite toward that to which it reverts, and it directs its entire being toward that thing to which it makes its essential reversion. It is also similar to that source according to its essence, which is why it is "sympathetic" to it by nature, as if they were of the same lineage of being. <note>The term "sympathetic" here implies a metaphysical resonance or "shared feeling" between two things that are naturally linked.</note>

If this is true, then either their essence is the same, or one comes from the other, or both have been allotted a similarity because they both come from some third thing. But if their essence is the same, how can one be naturally reverted toward the other? And if both come from one source, then the natural reversion of each would be toward that source. It remains, therefore, that one must have its being from the other. If this is so, then the "procession" <note>original: "progressio." The act of flowing out from a source.</note> comes from that same thing toward which the natural reversion is directed.

### COROLLARY.
From these points it is surely clear that **Mind** <note>original: "mēs" (mens). In this context, it refers to the Divine Intellect or *Nous*.</note> is the object of desire for all things; all things proceed from Mind, and the entire world has its essence from Mind. Even if the world is eternal, it does not mean it does not proceed from Mind simply because it is eternal. For it is not the case that it does not "revert" simply because it is always ordered. Rather, it is always proceeding, and yet it is eternal according to its essence; it is always reverted, and yet it is indissoluble according to its order.

### PROPOSITION XXXV.
**Every effect both remains in its cause, proceeds from it, and reverts to it.**

### THE DEMONSTRATION.
For if it only **remains**, it will differ in no way from its cause, existing as something indistinguishable from it. For procession <note>The act of moving out or becoming a distinct entity.</note> occurs simultaneously with distinction. 

However, if it should only **proceed**, it will be disconnected from its cause and "unsympathetic," having no communication with its cause whatsoever. 

If, on the other hand, it only **reverts**, how can that which does not have its essence from the cause make an essential reversion toward something foreign to it? 

If it **remains** and **proceeds** but does not **revert**, how can there be a natural appetite in each thing for "well-being" <note>original: "bene esse." A philosophical term for reaching one's perfection or goal.</note> and for the Good, and how can there be an upward striving toward that which generated it? 

But if it should **proceed** and **revert** but not **remain**, how—

<note>Two faint circular stains appear on the right side of the page, likely watermarks or signs of age on the paper.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the proof that things return to their sources because they are similar to them, and introduces the central Neoplatonic concept that every effect must simultaneously remain in, proceed from, and return to its cause.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Reversion (epistrophē), Procession (proodos), Mind (Nous), Causality, Sympathy, Essence</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page concludes the proof of Proposition 35 regarding the relationship between a cause and its effect, and introduces Propositions 36 and 37. These sections detail the hierarchy of "perfection" as things move away from or return toward their divine source.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" or head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...how, since it stands apart from its cause, does it hasten to be joined to it? It would have been "unjoinable" if it were so before its departure. For if it were joined, it would remain entirely within that cause. But if it remains and **reverts**, yet does not fully merge, how can it revert without being separated? For everything that reverts <note>original: "Conuertens." This refers to the Greek *epistrophē*, the third stage of existence where a thing turns back toward its origin.</note> is assimilated to that which it returns to, from which it was divided according to its essence. It is necessary, then, that it must either only remain, or only revert, or only proceed, or bind the extremes to one another, or be an intermediate between both extremes, or be all of these things at once. It remains, therefore, that **everything remains in its cause, proceeds from it, and reverts to it.** <note>This "triad" of remaining (*manē*), proceeding (*proodos*), and reverting (*epistrophē*) is the fundamental heartbeat of Neoplatonic metaphysics.</note>

### PROPOSITION 36.
Of all things that are multiplied in **procession**, the first are more perfect than the second, and the second are more perfect than those which follow them, and so on in like manner.

### THE DEMONSTRATION.
For if the "processions" <note>The "flow" of reality out from the One into the many.</note> of produced things distinguish them from their causes, then the "second" things are lower than the "first" things. Those that proceed first are joined more closely with their causes, having been born from those very things. The second things, however, are further from the causes, and so on. Those things which are closer and more akin to their causes are more perfect. For causes are always more perfect than the things they cause; thus, things further away are more imperfect, as they have already become dissimilar to their causes.

### PROPOSITION 37.
Of all things that subsist according to **reversion**, the first are more imperfect than the second, and the second than those that follow. The last things, however, are the most perfect.

### THE DEMONSTRATION.
For if reversions occur according to a circle—and the point to which the reversion is directed is the same as the point from which the procession began—and since the procession began from the **most perfect**, then the reversion is also toward the most perfect. Furthermore, if the goal toward which the procession moves is the "last" thing, then the first stage of reversion begins from this point. The procession moves into the "last" thing, which is the most imperfect; therefore, the reversion begins from the most imperfect. Consequently, the first stages in the process of reversion are the most imperfect, while the last stages are the most perfect.

<summary>Proclus establishes the fundamental Neoplatonic cycle: all things remain in their cause, flow out from it, and turn back toward it. He argues that as things "proceed" they lose perfection, but as they "revert" they gain perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, remaining, procession, reversion, hierarchy of being, perfection, causality</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical exposition of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology," specifically focusing on the symmetry between the "outward" flow of reality (procession) and the "inward" return to the source (reversion), and how different types of beings participate in this return according to their nature.</meta>

## THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 12

### PROPOSITION 38.
Everything that proceeds from multiple causes returns through as many steps as it progressed through; and every **reversion** <note>original: "conuersio." This translates the Greek *epistrophē*, the process by which a created thing turns back toward its divine origin to find fulfillment.</note> occurs through the same stages as the **procession**. <note>original: "progressio." The Greek *proodos*, the "stepping forth" or "flow" of existence from the One into multiplicity.</note>

### THE DEMONSTRATION.
Since both procession and reversion occur through **similarity**, <note>A core Neoplatonic principle: like is attracted to like. An effect is "like" its cause, and it returns to that cause by emphasizing that similarity.</note> that which has proceeded immediately from a source also returns immediately to it; for the similarity between them was immediate. However, that which requires an intermediary during its procession also requires an intermediary during its reversion. For it is necessary that both movements occur in the same way. Thus, it will return to the intermediary first, and then to that which is higher than the intermediary. Therefore, for each thing, there are as many stages of "well-being" <note>original: "bene esse." In this context, "well-being" refers to the perfection or completion of a thing when it successfully returns to its cause.</note> as there are stages of "being"—and vice versa.

### PROPOSITION 39.
Every being returns either **essentially** alone, or **vitally**, or also **cognitively**. <note>This refers to Proclus’s hierarchy of existence: Being, Life, and Mind/Intellect.</note>

### THE DEMONSTRATION.
For a thing possesses from its cause either its "being" alone, or its "living" along with its being, or it has also received from that source a "cognitive" power. <note>original: "cognoscentem... potentiam."</note> 
1. Insofar as it only *is*, it performs an **essential** return. 
2. Insofar as it also *lives*, it performs a **vital** return. 
3. Insofar as it *knows*, it performs a **cognitive** return. 

For in the same manner as it proceeded, so does it return; the measures of its return are defined by the measures of its procession. Thus, for some things, **desire** <note>original: "appetitus." The natural inclination or "pull" a thing feels toward its source.</note> exists only according to "being"—which is a simple fitness for participating in its causes. For others, desire exists according to "life," which is a movement toward higher things. For others still, it exists according to "knowledge," which is a conscious sense of the goodness of one’s causes.

### PROPOSITION 40.
Among all things that proceed from an external cause, those which are **self-constituted** <note>original: "a se ipsis subsistunt." These are beings that contain their own cause within themselves, rather than being entirely dependent on something else.</note> and possess a **self-subsisting essence** take precedence.

### THE DEMON- <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>Proclus explains that the path back to the Divine exactly mirrors the path of creation and identifies three levels of return (Being, Life, and Mind). He then introduces the concept of "self-subsisting" beings as the highest tier of created things.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, procession, reversion, similarity, Being, Life, Intellect, self-subsistence, theology</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus’s systematic derivation of reality, moving from how things "return" to their source (Proposition 39) to the definition of "self-subsistent" beings—those entities that contain their own cause and do not rely solely on a higher power for their existence.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if everything that is **self-sufficient** <note>original: "sufficiens." In Neoplatonic philosophy, this describes a being that possesses everything it needs within itself to exist or act.</note>, whether in its essence or in its action, is more excellent than that which depends on an external cause; and if that which produces itself is the cause of its own being and is thus self-sufficient in its essence; then it follows that whatever is produced by another alone is not self-sufficient. 

The self-sufficient is more closely related to the **Good** <note>original: "Bono." For Proclus, the "Good" or the "One" is the ultimate source of all reality.</note>. Those things that are more closely related and more similar to their causes exist prior to those things that are dissimilar. Therefore, those things that are produced by themselves and **subsist through themselves** <note>original: "per se subsistunt." These are "self-subsistent" beings, like Souls or Intellects, which occupy a middle ground between the ultimate One and physical matter.</note> are more ancient than those that proceed into being from an external cause alone. 

Either nothing is self-subsistent, or the **Good itself** is such, or the first things that subsist from the Good are self-subsistent. But if nothing were self-subsistent, nothing would be truly self-sufficient in any way—not even the Good. Yet the Good is superior to self-sufficiency, being the **One** <note>original: "vnum." The One is so simple and absolute that it doesn't even need "self-sufficiency"; it just is.</note> and the Good itself, without having to "possess" goodness. Nor could self-subsistence be found in those things that come after the Good, for all those things would then be in need of another—specifically, the one thing that precedes them. 

However, if the Good itself were self-subsistent (meaning it produces itself), it would no longer be the One. For that which proceeds from the One is not the One itself. If it subsists through itself, it "proceeds" from itself. Thus, the One would be simultaneously the One and not the One. Therefore, the **self-subsistent** must necessarily exist after the **First** <note>the One</note>, and it is clear that it must exist before those things that proceed from an external cause alone. It is more fundamental than those things and more closely related to the Good, as has been demonstrated.

->PROPOSITION 41.<-

Everything that exists in another is produced by another alone. Everything that exists in itself, however, is **self-subsistent**.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For that which exists in another <note>original: "in alio est." This refers to things like physical properties or forms that need "matter" or a "subject" to hold them.</note> and requires a **substrate** <note>original: "subiecto." A base or underlying material.</note> could never be the producer of itself. That which is naturally suited to beget itself does not require an external "seat," since it is contained by itself and preserved within itself, separate from what is above it. 

But that which can remain and stand in itself is productive of itself. It is that which proceeds into itself and contains itself; it exists in itself as an **effect exists in its cause** <note>original: "ut in causa causatum." A self-subsistent thing is both the cause and the result of that cause.</note>. It does not exist in itself as if in a physical place, nor as a property in a substrate. For a place is different from the thing in the place, and a substrate is different from the property within it. This, however, is to itself...

<summary>Proclus argues that "self-subsistent" beings—entities that are their own cause—occupy a vital middle rank between the ultimate One (the Good) and dependent physical realities. He defines self-subsistence as existing within oneself rather than needing an external substrate or location.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, self-sufficient, self-subsistent, the Good, the One, substrate, cause and effect, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Proposition 41, completing the argument that a self-subsistent being is both its own cause and its own effect, before moving into the relationship between self-subsistence and "reversion"—the process by which a being turns back toward its source to find its own perfection.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY, 13

...to itself, it is the same. For that which is **self-subsistent** <note>original: "per se subsistens." A being that contains its own cause and does not depend solely on something higher for its existence.</note> is both as if in a cause, and as the effect of that cause; thus it exists within itself.

### PROPOSITION 42.
Every self-subsistent being is capable of **turning back toward itself** <note>original: "ad seipsum est conuersiuum." In Neoplatonic thought, "reversion" (epistrophē) is the process where a being looks back to its source to achieve its full potential or "well-being."</note>.

#### DEMONSTRATION
For if it proceeds from itself, it will also make a turning back toward itself. For from whatever source the "progression" of each thing begins, the "reversion" coordinated with that progression also returns to that same source. 

If it were to proceed from itself alone, but did not turn back into itself, it would never seek its own **good** <note>original: "bonum." The ultimate goal or perfection of a being.</note> or that which it is able to provide for itself. Yet every cause is able to grant to that which follows from it both the **essence** <note>original: "essentia." The core identity or "what-it-is" of a thing.</note> which it gives, and also the **well-being** <note>original: "bene esse." Not just existing, but existing in a state of completion or happiness.</note> that is joined to that essence. 

Therefore, the self-subsistent being provides this to itself. This "good" is specifically proper to the self-subsistent being. But it will not seek this good unless it turns toward itself; for if it does not seek it, it will not attain it. If it does not attain its good, it would be imperfect and not **self-sufficient** <note>original: "non sufficiens."</note>. But if it is appropriate for any other thing to be self-sufficient and perfect, it must certainly be appropriate for the self-subsistent being. Therefore, it will attain its own good, it will desire it, and it will turn back toward itself.

### PROPOSITION 43.
Everything that turns back toward itself is self-subsistent.

#### DEMONSTRATION
For if it is turned toward itself by its own nature, and is perfected in that turning back, it must also have its **essence** from itself. For wherever a thing’s natural reversion is directed, its essential progression also begins from that same place. 

If, therefore, it provides "well-being" to itself, it will surely also provide "being" to itself, and it will be the master of its own existence. Therefore, that which is able to turn toward itself is self-subsistent.

### PROPOSITION 44.
Everything that turns toward itself in its **action** <note>original: "actionem." The activities or functions of a being, as opposed to its internal nature.</note> is also turned toward itself in its **essence**.

#### DEMONSTRATION

<summary>Proclus establishes the circular relationship between a being's origin and its purpose, arguing that any entity capable of "reverting" or looking inward to find its own perfection must also be the source of its own existence.</summary>
<keywords>self-subsistence, reversion, essence, well-being, progression, self-sufficiency, Neoplatonism, Proclus</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding self-subsistent beings, specifically focusing on why their internal activity must match their internal nature, and concluding that such beings are both without beginning and without end.</meta>

# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it were possible for a being to turn toward itself in its **activity** <note>original: "actione." The outward operation or power of a thing.</note>, but not be self-turning in its **essence** <note>original: "essentia." The core nature or fundamental "being" of the thing.</note>, it would be superior in its activity than in its essence. This is because the activity would be self-turning, while the essence would not. That which belongs to itself is superior to that which belongs only to another. Furthermore, that which is capable of preserving itself is more perfect than that which is only preserved by another.

If, therefore, a being turns toward itself through the activity that flows from its essence, it must also have been granted a self-turning essence. Thus, it does not merely act toward itself, but it also belongs to itself; it is contained by itself and is brought to **perfection** <note>original: "perficiatur." In this context, reaching a state of completeness or full potential.</note> by itself.

### PROPOSITION XLV.

Every **self-subsistent** <note>original: "per se subsistens." A being that contains its own cause and does not depend solely on something higher for its existence.</note> being is **unbegotten** <note>original: "ingenitum." Something that was not "born" or produced by an external creator at a specific moment in time.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it were begotten, it would be imperfect by its own nature because it was "born," and it would require that perfection which comes from another. However, because it produces itself, it is already perfect and self-sufficient. Every begotten thing is brought to perfection by another, which provides the process of "becoming" to that which did not yet exist. Indeed, the process of **becoming** <note>original: "genitura." The act of being produced or generated.</note> is a path leading from the imperfect to its opposite, the perfect.

But if a thing produces itself, it is always perfect. It is always connected to its own cause—or rather, it is inherently rooted in an essence that is inherently perfecting.

### PROPOSITION XLVI.

Every self-subsistent being is **incorruptible** <note>original: "incorruptibile." Incapable of being destroyed, decaying, or ceasing to be.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it were to be corrupted, it would leave itself and would exist apart from itself. But this is impossible. Since it is a unified "one," it is simultaneously the cause and the thing caused. Everything that is corrupted is destroyed by departing from its own cause. For as long as a thing remains within that which contains and preserves it, it is kept safe; however, a self-subsistent being never leaves its cause, because it does not leave itself. Its cause is itself. Therefore, every self-subsistent being is incorruptible.

PROPO-

<summary>Proclus argues that self-subsistent beings must have a nature that matches their actions, and because they are their own cause, they can neither be "born" from another nor "destroyed" by separation from a cause.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, self-subsistence, essence and activity, unbegotten, incorruptible, Neoplatonism, causality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical exploration of self-subsistent beings—those that contain their own cause of existence. Having previously established that these beings are "unbegotten" and "self-turning," Proclus now demonstrates their internal simplicity and their relationship to eternity.</meta>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 14

### PROPOSITION 47.

Everything that is **self-subsistent** <note>original: "per se subsistens." A being that contains its own cause and does not depend on an external creator for its existence.</note> is **indivisible** <note>original: "impartibile." Something that cannot be broken down into smaller parts.</note> and **simple** <note>original: "simplex." In philosophy, "simple" means not made of different components; it is pure and uniform.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if that which is self-subsistent were divisible, it would be composed of parts, and in turning toward itself, the whole would be turned toward itself, such that every part would be in every other part; but this is impossible. Therefore, that which is self-subsistent is indivisible. Furthermore, it is also simple. For if it were a composite, there would be within it one part that is "lesser" and another that is "better." In that case, the better would arise from the lesser, and the lesser from the better, assuming the whole proceeds from itself as a single unit. Additionally, it would not be self-sufficient, as it would depend upon the elements from which it is composed. Therefore, everything that is self-subsistent must be simple.

### CONCERNING THE ETERNAL.

### PROPOSITION 48.

Everything that is not **eternal** <note>original: "æternum." Having no beginning and no end; existing outside of linear time.</note> is either composite or exists within another.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For a thing is either capable of being dissolved into the parts from which it is made (and is entirely composed of those things into which it eventually dissolves), or it is in need of a **subject** <note>original: "subiecto." An underlying foundation or "host" that supports its existence.</note> and, upon leaving that subject, it vanishes into non-being. If, however, a being is simple and exists within itself, it will be impossible to dissolve or scatter.

### PROPOSITION 49.

Everything that is self-subsistent is eternal.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For there are two ways by which it is necessary for something to be non-eternal: the way of composition <note>being made of parts that can break apart</note>, and the way of existing within another <note>depending on something else to stay alive or exist</note>. However, that which is self-subsistent is not composite, but simple; nor is it in another, but in itself. Therefore, it is eternal.

FROM THE PROPO- <note>A "catchword" indicating the start of the next page's text.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that self-caused beings must be indivisible and "simple" (non-composite) because they cannot depend on underlying parts. He concludes that because these beings are simple and self-contained, they are necessarily eternal and cannot be destroyed.</summary>
<keywords>self-subsistence, simplicity, indivisibility, eternity, composition, Proclus, Neoplatonism, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus's "Elements of Theology." Following the discussion of eternal and composite beings in Proposition 48 and 49, the text now moves to Proposition 50, which defines the nature of things that exist within the flow of time rather than in eternity.</meta>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 15

### PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
### PROPOSITION 50.

**Everything** that is measured by **time** <note>original: "tempore." In Neoplatonism, time is a moving image of eternity, characterized by succession rather than simultaneous presence.</note>, whether according to its **essence** <note>original: "essentiam." The fundamental nature or substance of a thing.</note> or according to its **activity**, is **becoming** <note>original: "generatio." Also translated as "generation" or "genesis." It refers to a state of change or "coming-to-be," as opposed to "being," which is stable and eternal.</note>, insofar as it is measured according to time.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if a thing is measured by time, it belongs to it to exist or to act according to time. Consequently, "was" and "will be" differ from one another within its being. If "was" and "will be" were numerically the same, the thing would have suffered no change from the progression of time, which always possesses a "prior" and a "posterior" <note>original: "prius & posterius." The before and after that define temporal sequence.</note>. 

If, therefore, "was" is one thing and "will be" is another, the thing is in a state of **becoming** <note>original: "in fieri." Literally "in the making."</note>. It never truly *is* in a fixed sense, but proceeds along with the time by which it is measured, existing only in the act of becoming. It is not fixed in the same state of being, but is always receiving one existence after another; so that now, according to time, it is always becoming something different because of the progression of time. 

Therefore, it is not a "whole all at once" <note>original: "simul totum." A key concept of eternity where the beginning, middle, and end are present simultaneously.</note>. Instead, it exists within the dispersion of temporal extension and is stretched out along with it. This is, in fact, to have one's being in **non-being** <note>original: "non esse." Because a thing in time is always changing, it never "is" any one thing perfectly; it is always "not yet" what it will be.</note>. For that which is in the process of becoming is not yet that which it is becoming. Therefore, that which exists in this manner is "becoming."

### PROPOSITION 51.

<summary>This page defines the nature of temporal existence, arguing that anything measured by time is in a constant state of "becoming" rather than fixed "being."</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, time, becoming, generation, essence, temporal extension, non-being, eternity vs time</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 52, detailing the distinction between eternal being and temporal becoming, and introduces the relationship between participants and the transcendent principles of Eternity and Time.</meta>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 15

is, but as much as it is possible to be, it already possesses the whole undiminished and without extension. It holds its activity <note>original: "actionem." In Neoplatonic thought, "activity" or "energy" is the external expression of a thing's internal essence.</note> united to its essence <note>original: "essentiam."</note>, consisting in the same measure of perfection; it is, as it were, compacted according to one and the same limit, immovably and without progression. 

For if a thing is eternal <note>original: "sempiternum."</note>, as the name itself denotes, it is "always being" <note>original: "semper ens."</note>. However, being "at a certain time" <note>original: "esse aliquando"</note> is different from being "always." In the latter, it is not necessary for one thing to be "prior" and another "posterior." If there were such a sequence, there would be **becoming** <note>original: "generatio." The process of coming-to-be, which implies a beginning and an end.</note> and **non-being**. But where there is neither prior nor posterior, nor "was" and "will be," but only "is," that which exists is a **whole all at once** <note>original: "totum simul." This is the defining characteristic of eternity in the Platonic tradition: a state where all of time is present in a single, unchanging moment.</note>. And every thing that exists in this way is the same in its acting as it is in its being.

->**COROLLARY.**<-

From this it is clearly manifest that Eternity is the cause of things being "wholes." Since everything eternal—whether according to its essence or according to its activity—has its whole essence or activity present to itself all at once.

### PROPOSITION 53.

**Prior** to all eternal things, **Eternity** exists; and prior to all temporal things, **Time** exists.

### DEMONSTRATION.

If it is true that everywhere the "participated" things exist before the "participants," and the "unparticipated" <note>original: "imparticipabilia." This refers to a Monad or a pure principle that remains transcendent and does not "descend" into lower things, though lower things may derive their nature from it.</note> exist before the "participated," then it is clear that the **eternal thing** is one thing, the **eternity** within that thing is another, and **Eternity-itself** <note>original: "per se ipsam sempiternitas." The absolute, transcendent principle of Eternity.</note> is yet another. 

The first exists as a participant; the second as the thing participated; and the third as the unparticipated. Likewise, that which exists in **time** is one thing (the participant); the **time** which is within it is another (the thing participated); and the **Time** existing before this is yet another (the unparticipated). 

Each of these unparticipated principles is the same everywhere and in all things. However, the "participated" principle exists only in those things by which it is participated. For there are many eternal things and many temporal things, in all of which eternity exists through participation. Likewise, while "participated" time is divided, the "unparticipated" Time is indivisible. There is one Time before all these temporal things, and one Eternity before all eternal things; the former is the **Eternity of eternities**, and the latter is the **Time of times**, the creators <note>original: "conditores." The founding principles or producers.</note> of all things that are participated.

### PROPOSITION 54.

**Every eternity** is the measure of eternal things, and **every time** is the measure of those things which are in time; and these are the only **two measures** of life and motion in beings.

E 2 DEMON-

<summary>This page defines eternity as a "whole all at once" where essence and activity are unified, and establishes a hierarchy of Eternity and Time as transcendent "unparticipated" measures that precede all individual eternal and temporal beings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Eternity and Time from Proposition 53, moving into the specific measures of duration and the classification of temporal beings in Proposition 56.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

### DEMONSTRATION.

For every measuring principle measures either according to a part, or as a whole, being adapted to the thing measured. That which measures according to the whole is **Eternity** <note>original: "sempiternitas." In this context, it refers to the transcendent measure of unchanging things.</note>; that which measures by parts is **Time**. There are, therefore, only two measures: the one for eternal things, and the other for temporal things.

### PROPOSITION 56.

**Every thing subsisting according to time either exists for the whole of eternity, or it has its subsistence only for a certain duration in a part of time.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

If indeed all progressions <note>original: "progressus." The Neoplatonic "proodos," or the process by which lower things flow from higher principles.</note> occur through similarity, and before things that are entirely dissimilar, there must subsist things more similar to the first principles than those that are dissimilar. Now, it is impossible to join things that come into being in only a part of time to those things which are eternal. For just as things that "become" <note>original: "quae fiunt." Things in the state of generation or change.</note> differ from those that "subsist" <note>original: "subsistantibus." Things that have stable, true existence.</note>, so also do things that exist only "at some time" differ from those that "always" exist. 

The middle ground between these two extremes consists of those things that are in some respects similar to the former, and in some respects similar to the latter. Is it not true, then, that those things which "become at some point" are distant from those things which "always exist"? Therefore, must the middle ground be either that which "always becomes," or that which "exists at some point"? 

It cannot be that which is "at some point truly being" <note>original: "aliquando enter ens." A literal Latin rendering of the Greek "ontōs on," meaning that which exists in the full, unchanging sense of Being.</note>, for that is impossible. On the other hand, that which is "at some point not truly being" is the same as that which "becomes." Therefore, the middle ground is not that which "is at some point." 

It remains, therefore, that the "always becoming" is the middle between both. Insofar as it "becomes," it is joined to inferior things; but insofar as it is "always," it imitates the eternal nature.

### COROLLARY.

From these points it is clear that there are two forms of "everlastingness" <note>original: "æternitas."</note>. One is indeed **Eternal**, but the other is **according to time**. The former is a standing eternity, while the latter is in a state of becoming. The former possesses its being unified and **whole all at once** <note>original: "totum simul." A key Neoplatonic term for the eternal "now" where past, present, and future are one.</note>; the latter is poured out and unfolded according to temporal extension. The former is a whole through itself, while the latter is composed of parts, of which each is separated according to **before** and **after**.

### PROPO-
<note>The text cuts off mid-word, preparing for the next Proposition.</note>

<summary>This page distinguishes between Eternity (the measure of wholes) and Time (the measure of parts), arguing that there is a middle state of "always becoming" that bridges the gap between eternal being and temporary existence.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, Eternity, Time, Totum simul, Subsistence, Becoming, Neoplatonism, Measure, Progression</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology." Following the discussion of temporal and eternal beings on the previous page, it now moves into the fundamental laws of causality that govern the hierarchy of being—specifically how primary causes exert more power than their immediate successors.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 16

### PROPOSITION 57.

**Everything produced by secondary beings is also produced, and to a greater degree, by those prior and more causal beings from which the secondary beings were themselves produced.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if a secondary being <note>original: "secundum." In Neoplatonic thought, this refers to a lower level in the chain of existence, such as Soul being secondary to Intellect.</note> receives its entire essence from the being that precedes it, it likewise receives its productive power from that source. This is because productive powers are essential to those things that produce; they complete their very essence. 

If, then, these beings have obtained their power of producing from a higher cause, they also receive from that higher cause the reason for the existence of those things of which they are causes—their reach being measured by that creative power. If this is true, then even the things proceeding from the secondary cause are caused by the principle that stands before it. For that which makes the cause itself exist also makes the effect of that cause exist. 

If this is so, the effect is also made "such as it is" by that higher source. Furthermore, it is evident that the effect is produced "more" from the higher source. For if the higher source gave the secondary being the power to produce, it possessed this causal power first; it is because of this that the secondary being can generate, having received the power of generation from the higher source. If, then, one thing becomes productive through participation <note>original: "participationem." The concept where a lower thing "shares" in the qualities of a higher one without diminishing the source.</note>, while the other is productive through its own bestowal, then that which bestows the power of generation upon the thing immediately following it is, in a primary and greater sense, the cause.

### PROPOSITION 58.

**Every cause both acts before its effect, and remains productive across a wider range of things than its effect.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For in that it is a cause, it is more perfect and more powerful than that which follows it. And if this is true, it is the cause of more things. For it is the nature of a greater power to produce more; of an equal power, to produce equal things; and of a lesser power, fewer things. 

Furthermore, a power that can achieve greater things in similar contexts can also achieve lesser things. However, a power that can achieve lesser things will not necessarily be able to achieve greater ones. If, therefore, the cause is more powerful, it is also productive of more things. 

But indeed, whatever the effect is capable of, the cause is even more capable of. For everything produced by secondary things is produced to a greater degree by the prior and more fundamental causes. Coexist <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, transitioning to the next page.</note>

<summary>This page outlines two fundamental laws of Neoplatonic causality: first, that primary causes are more responsible for an effect than the immediate, secondary cause; and second, that higher causes have a broader reach and power than their subordinates.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, causality, secondary causes, primary causes, essence, productive power, participation, hierarchy of being</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration of Proposition 57, establishing that a primary cause acts more powerfully and extends its influence further than any secondary cause. It then applies this principle to the hierarchy of the Soul, Intellect, and The Good.</meta>

-># PROCLUS DIADOCHUS<-

exist, therefore, in that higher cause all things which the secondary cause is naturally suited to produce. If, moreover, the higher cause produces the effect even before the secondary cause does, it is clearly evident that it acts prior to the secondary cause in its productive action. Therefore, every cause acts prior to its effect, acts alongside it, and produces other things after it.

### COROLLARY.

From these points, it is surely clear that for whatever things the Soul <note>original: "anima." In Neoplatonic thought, the Soul is the principle of life and motion that sits below the Intellect.</note> is a cause, the Intellect <note>original: "mens." The divine mind or "Nous," which contains the archetypal forms of all things.</note> is also a cause; however, the Soul is not the cause of everything that the Intellect causes. Rather, the Intellect acts even before the Soul. Whatever the Soul gives to secondary beings, the Intellect also gives, and to a greater degree. Even when the Soul is no longer acting, the Intellect illuminates with its own gifts those things to which the Soul did not give itself. Indeed, even an inanimate thing, insofar as it participates in form, participates in the Intellect and the activity of the Intellect. Furthermore, whatever the Intellect is the cause of, The Good <note>original: "bonum." The ultimate principle or "The One," which is the source of all existence and exceeds even the Intellect in its reach.</note> is also the cause of, but the reverse is not true. For even the privations of forms <note>original: "privationes formarum." This refers to the absence of qualities or matter itself, which still falls under the domain of the ultimate source (The Good) even where Intellect (Form) does not reach.</note> come from that ultimate source, because all things come from it. However, the Intellect is not the creator of privation, since its nature is Form.

### PROPOSITION 58.

**Every product resulting from a greater number of causes is more composite than that which is produced by fewer causes.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if every cause gives something to that which proceeds from it, then a greater number of causes will provide a greater number of gifts, while fewer causes will provide fewer. Thus, among participating things, some will be composed of more elements and others of fewer, depending on what they have participated in. Some result from a progression from many causes, and others from a progression from fewer. Those that arise from more causes are more composite <note>original: "compositiora." This suggests a higher level of complexity or a greater number of "layers" of existence.</note>, while those from fewer are simpler. Therefore, everything produced by many causes is more composite, while that from fewer is simpler. For whatever participates in the secondary cause must also participate in the primary cause, but the reverse is not true.

### PROPOSITION 59.

**Everything that is simple in its essence is either superior to composite things or inferior to them.**

### DEMON-

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 59 from the previous page, further refining the relationship between simplicity, composition, and the hierarchy of causes. It establishes that the most universal causes are the most powerful.</meta>

### ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 117

### PROOF.

If the highest of beings are produced by fewer and simpler causes, while the intermediate beings are produced by more numerous causes, these intermediate ones will indeed be composite. Regarding the extremes: some are simpler in a superior way <note>original: "secundum præstantius." This refers to the simplicity of the One or the Intellect, which is "simple" because it is above all division.</note>, while others are simpler in an inferior way <note>original: "secundum deterius." This refers to the simplicity of prime matter, which is "simple" only because it lacks any form or quality.</note>. But since the highest things are produced by fewer causes, it is clearly evident that they belong to the higher ranks; they begin their existence before the inferior things and extend their influence beyond them—reaching where those others cannot go due to their diminished power. For this reason, the very last of beings is also most simple, just like the first, because it proceeds from the first cause alone. Yet one kind of simplicity is superior to all composition, while the other is inferior; and in all things, the same logic will apply.

# PROPOSITION 60.

Every cause of a greater number of effects is superior to that which has been allotted power over fewer effects, and it produces those parts of which the other is productive as wholes. <note>In Neoplatonic logic, the "more universal" a cause is, the more "powerful" it is, because it can affect a wider range of reality.</note>

### PROOF.

If one cause is the cause of fewer things and another is the cause of more, and the effects of the former are parts of the effects of the latter, then whatever the one produces, the other—which is productive of more—will also produce. But that which produces the former will not produce all that the latter produces. The latter is therefore more powerful and encompasses a greater number of things. For just as a product relates to another product, so do the producers relate to one another when compared. That which is more capable possesses a greater and more universal power. This is closer to the Cause of all things <note>The "Cause of all things" is a title for the One or the First Principle.</note>; and that which is closer to it is more like The Good, since the Cause itself is **The Good** itself. Therefore, a cause of more things is essentially superior to that which produces fewer things.

# PROPOSITION 61.

Every power, if it exists in an indivisible <note>original: "impartibilis." This describes a power that is unified and not scattered or diluted.</note> state, is greater; but if divided, it is lesser.

### PROOF.

<summary>Proclus explains that the highest and lowest points of reality are both "simple," though for opposite reasons, and demonstrates that a cause's power is measured by how many effects it can produce.</summary>
<keywords>simplicity, universal cause, hierarchy of being, The Good, power, indivisibility, Proclus</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration from Proposition 61, establishing the inverse relationship between quantity and power in the Neoplatonic hierarchy, moving toward the structure of participation in Propositions 62 and 63.</meta>

### THE SUCCESSOR PROCLUS 113

### PROOF.

For if a thing is divided, it departs into multiplicity. If this happens, it becomes further removed from the One. If this is so, its power is diminished; for in departing from the One which contains it, it loses its integrity and becomes imperfect. This is because the "good" or well-being of every single thing exists precisely through its union <note>original: "per unionem." In Neoplatonic thought, "The One" is synonymous with "The Good." To be unified is to be strong and healthy; to be divided is to be weak.</note>.

# PROPOSITION 62.

Every multitude that exists closer to the One is, in quantity, smaller than those further away, but in power, it is greater.

### PROOF.

For that which is closer to the One is more like it. The One itself was the constitutive source of all things without being multiplied itself. Therefore, that which is more like the One is the cause of a greater number of things. Indeed, such a thing will be more "one-like" <note>original: "uniformius." This describes a state of being unified and simple rather than complex.</note> and more indivisible if the One is its model. 

Just as that which is less multiplied is more closely related to the One, it is also more closely related to the cause of all things. Consequently, it is productive of a greater number of effects. This, in turn, makes it more powerful.

### COROLLARY.

From these points it is clearly evident that there are more corporeal natures than there are souls; and there are more souls than there are intellects <note>original: "mentibus." In this context, "Minds" or "Intellects" refers to the divine level of reality above the individual soul.</note>. Furthermore, there are more intellects than there are divine unities <note>original: "divinis unitatibus," also known as Henads. These are the highest "units" of reality just below the absolute One.</note>. In all things, the same ratio applies. <note>Proclus describes a pyramid: at the bottom (physical bodies), there is the greatest number of individual things but the least power; at the top (divine units), there are very few entities, but they possess the greatest power.</note>

# PROPOSITION 63.

Every <term>unparticipated thing</term> <note>An "unparticipated" (imparticipabile) principle is a source that remains pure and distinct from the things that depend on it, rather than being "divided" among them.</note> produces two orders of participated things: one in those things which only sometimes participate in it, and another in those things which participate in it always and by their very nature.

### PROOF.

That which is always participated is more similar to the unparticipated principle than that which is only sometimes participated. Therefore, before the existence of that which is participated only occasionally, there must exist that which is always participated. This is because it is participated in by that which follows after it,

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 18

...differing in this way: that which exists always is more akin to the **unparticipated** <term>unparticipated: a Platonic term for a primary principle that remains independent and pure, rather than being "divided" among the things that depend on it</term> and more similar to it. Nor are there only those things which are participated in at certain times; for prior to these are those things which are participated in always, through which these [temporal things] are also gathered, according to a well-ordered progression, toward the unparticipated. Nor are there only those which are participated in always; for since these possess an inextinguishable power (given that they exist always), they are the source of others—namely, those which are participated in only at certain times—and the decrease in power continues down to these.

### COROLLARY.

From these points it is clearly evident that even the unifications which illuminate beings from the One <note>original: "ab vno." In this system, the "One" is the ultimate source from which all reality radiates like light.</note> are of two kinds: some are always participated in, while others are only sometimes. The same holds true for intellectual participations, the "animations" or life-giving forces of souls, and other forms similarly. For indeed **beauty**, **similarity**, **stability**, and **identity** are unparticipated in their highest form; however, they are participated in by those things that participate always, and by those things that participate only sometimes, according to their rank in the same order.

# PROPOSITION 64.

**Every primary unity produces a twofold number: one of substances that are perfect in themselves, and the other of illuminations that have their existence within other things.**

### PROOF.

For if the progression of reality occurs through a gradual decrease, it proceeds in an orderly fashion from the absolutely perfect, through the perfect, and through these intermediate things toward the imperfect. Therefore, some will be substances perfect in themselves, while others will be imperfect; these latter become the "forms" of the things that participate in them. Because they are imperfect, they require subjects <note>original: "subiectis." In ancient philosophy, a subject is the underlying matter or entity that "carries" a property, like a body carrying the property of "whiteness."</note> for their existence. 

The others, however, make the participants their own. Because they are perfect, they fill those participants with themselves and sustain them, yet they require nothing from lower things for their own self-existence. Therefore, substances perfect in themselves—being diminished from their primary unity due to their distinction into multiplicity, and because they are perfect in themselves...

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "successor" or head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...attain a perfect existence and are in some measure assimilated to that primary principle. But imperfect things—both because they exist within others and because they are far removed from that which subsists by itself and that which perfects all things—are distant from it. This progression from the source proceeds through similar things until it reaches those that are completely dissimilar. Therefore, each of the primary unities produces a twofold number <note>meaning a group of beings: one set that is "perfect" (independent) and one set that is "imperfect" (dependent qualities).</note>.

### COROLLARY.

From these points it is surely clear that even the unities <note>original: "unitates." In Proclus’s system, these are the "Henads," the first radiations from the Absolute One that act as the gods.</note> which are perfect in themselves proceeded from the One. The radiations of these unities and of the Mind are of different kinds: some are essences perfect in themselves, while others are merely intellectual perfections [qualities]. Likewise, some souls are independent (belonging to themselves), while others are merely the life-forces of animated bodies—these latter being only images or reflections of souls. 

And so, not every unity is a God, but only the unity that is perfect in itself. Not every intellectual property is an Intellect <term>Intellect: "Nous" in Greek, the realm of pure forms and divine thought</term>, but only that which is essential. Nor is every radiation of a soul a Soul; for there are also mere images of souls.

# PROPOSITION 65.

**Every being, in whatever way it exists, does so either primarily according to its cause, or according to its existence, or according to participation (as an image).**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if the product is viewed within the producer, it is seen as pre-existing in its **cause**. This is because every cause contains its effect within itself beforehand; what exists in the effect secondarily, exists in the cause primarily. 

Alternatively, the producer may be viewed within the product. For the thing **participating** <term>participation: the process by which a lower thing shares in the properties of a higher principle (e.g., a beautiful flower "participates" in the Principle of Beauty)</term> in the producer shows within itself, in a secondary way, that which the producer is primarily. 

Finally, each thing may be considered according to its own rank, neither in its cause nor in its effect. For the cause has a more excellent mode of being, and the effect has a lesser mode. It is necessary, then, that there is also the thing as it is in itself. This is its mode according to its actual **existence** in its own order.

# PROPOSITION 66.

**All beings, in relation to one another, are either wholes, or parts, or the same, or different.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

<summary>Proclus distinguishes between primary divine principles and their lower reflections, then outlines the three modes of existence: as a cause, as a self-standing being, or as a participated image.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration of Proposition 66 regarding how beings relate to one another through encompassment or participation, then introduces the influential Neoplatonic doctrine of the "Three Wholes" in Propositions 67 and 68.</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 19<-

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if some things encompass others, while the others are encompassed, or if they neither encompass nor are encompassed, they must either have experienced something identical (as participants in the One) or they are distinct from one another. But if they encompass, they would be **wholes**. If they are encompassed, they are **parts**. If they are many participating in the One, they are the same according to that One. But if they are only many, they are different from one another insofar as they are many.

---

### PROPOSITION LXVII.

Every wholeness is either prior to its parts, or composed of its parts, or exists within a part.

### DEMONSTRATION.

We may view the form of each thing either in its cause—and we call that the **whole prior to the parts** <note>original: "ante partes." This refers to the archetypal idea of a thing existing in the divine mind before it takes physical shape.</note> which pre-exists in the cause—or we view it in the parts that participate in it. And this latter case occurs in two ways: either in all the parts taken together, which is the **whole composed of parts** <note>original: "ex partibus." This is the sum total of all components, where the loss of any single part diminishes the whole.</note>; or in each individual part, insofar as the part becomes a "whole" through participation. This makes the part a whole "partially" <note>original: "partialiter." A "whole-in-the-part" is the presence of the organizing principle within a single fragment, allowing it to reflect the nature of the entire system.</note>. 

According to existence, the whole is that which is composed of parts. According to cause, it is that which is prior to the parts. According to participation, it is that which is in the part. For this last type is a whole according to a final diminution, by which it imitates the whole composed of parts; this happens when a part is not just any random piece, but is capable of being assimilated to the whole, and whose own sub-parts are also wholes.

---

### PROPOSITION LXVIII.

Every whole that exists in a part is itself a part of that whole which is composed of parts.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it is a part, it must be a part of some whole. It is either a part of that "whole" which exists within itself—insofar as it is called a "whole-in-the-part"—but in that case, it would be a part of itself, the part would be equal to the whole, and both would be the same. Or, it is a part of some other whole. And if it is a part of another, it is either only a part of that other, and thus would again differ in no way from a whole, being one

### PROPOSITION LXIX.

[Initial O] Every whole composed of parts participates in that wholeness which is prior to the parts.

### DEMONSTRATION.

[Initial S] For if it is composed of parts, it is a whole that has been "affected." <note>original: "passum." In Neoplatonic logic, this indicates that the "whole" is a result or a product of a process (the assembly of parts), rather than being the original source itself.</note> For since the parts have been made into one, the whole has undergone an effect due to that union, and it is a whole existing within parts that are not themselves the whole. Yet before every participated thing, there pre-exists that which is "unparticipated." <note>term: "imparticipabile" — an unparticipated principle is a pure form or cause that exists independently, whereas a "participated" one is the version of that form that has "descended" into a physical or specific object.</note> Therefore, an unparticipated wholeness pre-exists the participated one. There is, then, a certain form of wholeness prior to that whole which is composed of parts. This prior wholeness is not "affected" in order to be a whole, but is wholeness by its very nature. 

From this prior wholeness comes that wholeness which is composed of parts. Furthermore, since that whole which is composed of many parts exists in many places—and in many other things which are themselves made of parts—it exists in some things in one way, and in others in another. However, there must be a unity of all wholenesses that exists by itself. For none of these individual wholes is pure, as each is dependent on those parts from which it is made, which are not themselves the whole. Nor, if a whole comes to be in one specific place, can it be the cause for all other things to be wholes. But that which is the cause for all other wholes to be wholes is "prior to the parts." For if this cause were also composed of parts, it would be a specific whole and not "wholeness" simply. And that in turn would depend on something else, and so on either to infinity, or there will be a first whole—not a whole composed of parts, but that which is Wholeness itself.

### PROPOSITION LXX.

[Initial O] Everything that is more universal, among primary causes and prior to particular things, shines forth in the participants; and even when the more specific causes no longer act, the more universal cause continues to be present in the participant.

### DEMONSTRATION.

[Initial E] For indeed, prior to that which follows it, the universal cause begins its action in secondary things; and it is present along with the presence of that secondary cause, and even when that secondary cause is no longer acting,

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology." It concludes the discussion on Proposition 70 regarding the persistence of primary causes and introduces Proposition 71, which explains how universal causes serve as the foundation for particular ones.</meta>

->THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 20<-

...while the [secondary] agent is active, that which is more causal is still present and continues to act. This occurs not only in different subjects, but also in every single thing that participates in these causes at different times. For example, it is necessary for *being* to come first, then *animal*, and finally *man*. <note>This illustrates the hierarchy of existence: one must exist (being) before one can have life (animal), and have life before one can have reason (man).</note> Even when the rational power <note>original: "rationali potentia"</note> leaves him, he is no longer a *man*, yet he is still an *animal*, breathing and sensing. Furthermore, when life in turn leaves him, *being* remains; for even when he does not live, "to be" is still present. This holds true for all things in a similar way. The reason for this is that the principle which is more of a cause, being more effective, acts upon the participant first. For the same subject is affected by the prior, more powerful cause, and when the second cause acts in turn, that prior cause continues to act alongside it. Because everything that the secondary cause produces is also co-generated by that which is more of a cause; and when the former leaves, the latter remains. For the communication of the more powerful cause, since it acts more intensely, is the last to leave the participant. Indeed, through the communication of the secondary cause, the primary cause has even strengthened its own illumination. <note>In Neoplatonic thought, a "higher" cause like Existence reaches further down into the depths of reality than a "lower" cause like Humanity.</note>

### PROPOSITION LXXI.

[Initial O] All things which, in the principal causes, possess a more universal and superior order, become—within the effects and according to the illuminations from themselves—a kind of substrate <note>term: "subiecta" (substrate) — in this context, it refers to the underlying foundation or "canvas" upon which further qualities are painted.</note> for the participations of more particular things. The illuminations from the superior causes receive the progressions from the secondary ones, and the latter are stabilized within the former. Thus, some participations precede others, and images <note>original: "simulachra"</note> are piled upon images, meeting from above into the same subject. The more universal causes act beforehand; then the particular ones, following the actions of the former, bestow their own communications upon the participants.

### DEMONSTRATION.

[Initial N] For if those things which are more causal act before the secondary ones due to their excellence of power, and are present to those things that have a less perfect aptitude and illuminate them, while the lower and secondary things in the order receive gifts from them: it is clear that the illuminations of the superior causes preoccupy that which participates in both, and they strengthen the communications of the lower ones. These [lower communications] use those images which are from the superior causes as if they were stays and foundations, acting upon a participant that has already been prepared by the higher powers.

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology." The previous section established that higher, more powerful causes extend their influence further than secondary causes, persisting even when the secondary effects have vanished.</meta>

### PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" (Diadochus) as the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. He was a central figure in late Neoplatonism.</note>

### PROPOSITION LXXII.

All things which, in their participants, hold the rank of a substrate <note>original: "subiecti habent rationem." In philosophy, a "substrate" or "subject" is the underlying material or foundation that receives qualities or forms, like how wood is the substrate for a chair.</note> proceed from more perfect and more universal causes.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For the causes of a greater number of effects are more powerful, more universal, and closer to the One than the causes of fewer effects. <note>In Proclus's system, the higher a cause is, the more things it can influence. The highest cause, the One, influences everything in existence.</note> Those things which produce the substrates for other qualities are the causes of more numerous effects, because they produce the very capacity or aptitude <note>original: "aptitudines"</note> for a thing to exist before the specific forms are even present. Therefore, these causes are more universal and more perfect.

### COROLLARY.

From this it is clear why matter, having been produced from the One, is by its own nature devoid of form. <note>Matter is the ultimate "substrate" or "subject" of everything, so it must come from the highest possible source.</note> Similarly, the body by itself—even though it participates in *being*—does not participate in *soul*. For matter, existing as the substrate for all things, proceeds from the Cause of all things. The body, however, existing as the substrate for life (animation), arose from a more universal cause than the soul, even if it participates in *being* in its own particular way.

### PROPOSITION LXXIII.

Every "whole" is certainly a certain "being" and participates in existence, but not every being is a "whole."

### DEMONSTRATION.

Either "being" and "whole" are the same thing, or one is prior and the other posterior. However, even a *part*, insofar as it is a part, is indeed a *being* (for the whole is composed of parts that have being), yet the part is not a *whole* in itself. Therefore, "being" and "whole" are not the same. If they were, then a part would not be a being. But if the part were not a being, then the whole could not be a being either. For every whole is a whole of parts, whether it exists before them <note>as a blueprint or idea</note> or within them. If the part did not exist, it would be impossible for the whole to exist.

On the other hand, if the "whole" were prior to "being," then every being would immediately be a "whole," and there would be no such thing as a "part." But this is impossible. For if a whole is a whole, it exists as the whole of a part; and a part will be the part of a whole, existing as a part. It remains, then, that every whole is a being, but not every being is a whole.

CORE- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the corollary on the next page.</note>

<summary>Proclus explains that the most fundamental parts of an object (its substrate or matter) come from the highest, most universal causes. He also distinguishes between "being" and "wholeness," arguing that while all wholes exist, not all existing things are wholes (since parts also exist).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology." The previous page established that more universal causes (like the One or Being) have a wider reach than specific causes, extending even to the basic substrates of reality.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 21

->COROLLARY.<-

From these points, it is clear that Being, in its primary sense, exists beyond Wholeness. This is because Being is present in a greater number of things; for it provides existence even to parts, simply insofar as they are parts. Wholeness, however, is present in fewer things. <note>In Proclus’s logic, "Being" is the most universal category because everything—even a tiny fragment—"is." But not everything is a "whole." Therefore, Being is a higher, more powerful cause than Wholeness.</note> As previously demonstrated, the cause of many things is superior, while the cause of fewer things is lower.

### PROPOSITION LXXIV.

Every Form <note>original: "species." In Neoplatonism, a "Form" or "Species" is a divine blueprint or essence that gives a thing its specific character.</note> is a kind of whole.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For a Form consists of many elements, each of which completes that Form. However, not every whole is a Form. For instance, a specific individual thing is a "whole" (as an individual), but it is not a "Form." <note>A "whole" is any collection of parts, like a specific pile of stones. A "Form" is a universal category, like "Stone-ness" or "Humanity," which organizes those parts into a specific type.</note> Every whole consists of parts; however, a Form is that which can be divided into several specific sub-types. Therefore, "whole" and "Form" are different. The former [the whole] is found in more things, and the latter [the Form] in fewer. Consequently, Wholeness stands above the Forms of beings.

->COROLLARY.<-

From this it is clear that Wholeness holds a middle rank between Being and the Forms. It follows that Being exists before the Forms, and while Forms are indeed beings, not every being is a Form. This is why, even among the results of creation, privations <note>original: "privationes." A privation is the absence of a quality, such as darkness being the absence of light or sickness being the absence of health.</note> are in some sense "beings," yet they are not "Forms." Because of the unifying power of Being, even these privations have received a certain faint reflection of existence. <note>Even "nothingness" or "evil" must participate in "Being" to even be discussed, though they lack the structured essence of a "Form."</note>

### PROPOSITION LXXV.

Everything that is called a "cause" in the proper sense is separate <note>original: "exemptum." This means the cause exists on a higher plane of reality than the thing it creates, rather than being "soaked" into the effect itself.</note> from its effect.

### DEMONSTRATION.

If a cause existed inside its effect, it would either be a part that completes the effect, or it would somehow depend on the effect for its own existence. In that case, the cause would be more imperfect than the thing it produced. However, that which exists *inside* an effect is a "co-cause" rather than a "cause" in the true sense; it is either a part of the thing being made, or an instrument used by the maker. For a part within a thing made... <note>The text cuts off here, but the argument suggests that a true "Maker" must stand outside and above their creation, whereas "parts" and "tools" are mere components of the process.</note>

<summary>Proclus establishes a hierarchy of metaphysical categories, placing Being at the top, followed by Wholeness, and then specific Forms. He also argues that a true Cause must remain distinct and superior to its effects.</summary>
<keywords>Being, Wholeness, Forms, species, privations, causality, Neoplatonism, Proclus</keywords>

# OF PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (c. 412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, continuing the lineage of Plato.</note>

...is made, and is more imperfect than the whole, and is an instrument serving the process of generation, being unable to determine for itself the measure of its own making. Therefore, every cause in the proper sense, if it is more perfect than that which proceeds from it, also provides the measure for that generation; it is exempt from being a mere instrument and from all the basic elements simply called "co-causes." <note>original: "concause." In Neoplatonic thought, a "true cause" is the primary source of a thing’s nature, while a "co-cause" is a secondary factor, like the hammer a carpenter uses or the wood itself.</note>

### PROPOSITION LXXVI.
Everything that is produced by an unmoving cause has an unchanging existence. Everything produced by a moving cause has a changing existence.

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if the agent <note>The "agent" is the cause or the being that acts to create something else.</note> is entirely unmoving, it does not produce the secondary thing through movement, but by its very existence. If this is the case, that which comes from the cause is linked to the cause's own existence. And if this is so, the cause produces as long as it exists. Since the cause always exists, it therefore always produces that which follows it. Thus, the effect is always being generated from that source, and always exists. The "always" of the cause’s action joins together with the "always" of the effect's progression. 

However, if the cause moves, then that which is produced by it will be changeable in its essence; because its existence depends on movement, and when that movement changes, the product's existence changes. If a product of movement were to remain unchanging, it would be superior to its own producing cause, which is impossible. Therefore, it will not be unchanging; rather, it will change and be moved in its essence, mimicking the movement that produced it.

### PROPOSITION LXXVII.
Every being that exists potentially proceeds into actuality through a being that is already in actuality. That which is partially potential proceeds from that which is partially actual, insofar as it is potential; while a being that is entirely potential proceeds from a being that is entirely in actuality. <note>Proclus is using the Aristotelian concepts of "Potentiality" (the capacity to become something) and "Actuality" (the state of being fully realized). A block of marble is potentially a statue; the sculptor is the actual force that makes it so.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.
That which is potential is not by nature capable of bringing itself into actuality, because it is imperfect. For if that which is imperfect were to become the cause of its own perfection—and were to be the cause that brings itself into actuality—then the cause would be more imperfect than the thing it produced. Therefore...

<summary>Proclus argues that unmoving causes produce eternal effects while moving causes produce changing ones, and that anything "potential" requires a pre-existing "actual" cause to reach its fulfillment.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, causation, immutability, potentiality, actuality, co-causes, metaphysics</keywords>
