<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology," specifically concluding the demonstration of Proposition 77 regarding the transition from potentiality to actuality, and moving into Propositions 78 and 79 concerning the nature of "power" (potentia/dynamis).</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 22<-

...therefore, that which is in potentiality <note>original: "potentia." In this context, it refers to the state of being able to become something, but not yet being it—like a seed that is a potential tree.</note>, insofar as it is potential to itself, would be the cause of its own existence in actuality. In that case, that which is imperfect would be the cause of the perfect; for indeed, everything which is potential, as potential, is imperfect. But everything which is in act <note>original: "actu." This refers to "actuality" or "activity"—the state of being fully realized or functioning.</note>, as act, is perfect.

If, therefore, that which is in potentiality is to become actual, it will receive its perfection from something else. And that source is either itself potential (but in this way, once again, the imperfect would be the generator of the perfect) or it is actual. And either something else, or this very thing which is potential, becomes actual. But if something else already existing in act performs the action—acting according to its own property—it will make that which is potential in the other become actual. Nor will it [the subject] then become actual through itself; since it does not become so insofar as it is potential. It remains, therefore, that what is potential is changed into being in act by that which is already in actuality.

### PROPOSITION LXXVIII.
**E**very power is either perfect or imperfect. <note>Proclus distinguishes between "power" as a capacity to act (perfect) and "potentiality" as a capacity to be acted upon (imperfect).</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or a power that bestows actuality is a perfect power. It makes other things perfect through its own activities, and as a finisher of other things, it is itself more perfect. But that which requires another thing which already exists in act—insofar as it is something in potentiality—is an imperfect power. For it requires the perfect which exists in another, so that by participating in that other, it may become perfect. For by itself, such a power is imperfect. Therefore, the power of that which is in act is perfect, since it is fertile in activity. But the power of that which is potential is imperfect, receiving its perfection from the former.

### PROPOSITION LXXIX.
**E**verything that comes into being does so from a twofold power.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or it is necessary that the thing itself <note>The "recipient" or the thing being created.</note> be suitable and possess an imperfect power; and that the agent <note>The "cause" or the thing doing the creating.</note>, existing in act as that which the other is only in potentiality, should have previously possessed a perfect power. For every activity proceeds from an inherent power. For if the agent does not have the power, how will it act and produce an effect in another? If, however, [the text continues...]

<summary>This page discusses the metaphysical relationship between potentiality and actuality, arguing that something imperfect (potential) cannot bring itself to perfection (actuality) without an external, already-actual cause. It defines the two types of power: the perfect power that acts and the imperfect power that is acted upon.</summary>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

...that what occurs does not have a capacity according to its suitability, in what way will it occur? For you will see: everything acts upon that which is capable of being affected by it, but not upon just anything, nor upon that which is not naturally suited to be affected by it.

### PROPOSITION LXXX.

**E**very body is by nature intended to be acted upon. <note>original: "pati." In this philosophical context, this does not mean "to suffer" in the modern sense, but rather "to be passive" or "to receive an action."</note> But every incorporeal thing is by nature intended to act. The former is of itself ineffective, while the latter is incapable of being acted upon <note>original: "impassibile." This describes a state of being where a thing cannot be changed, divided, or harmed by external forces.</note>. Yet, even the incorporeal is affected through its association with the body, in the same way that bodies are able to act through their participation in incorporeal things.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or a body, insofar as it is a body, is only divisible; thus, it is everywhere subject to being affected, existing as something that can be partitioned in every direction and into infinity. However, the incorporeal, being simple in its existence, is unaffected. For that which is indivisible cannot be divided, nor can that which is uncompounded <note>Something made of only one "ingredient" or essence, rather than a mixture.</note> be altered. Therefore, either nothing is an agent <note>A source of action or change.</note>, or the agent is incorporeal. Indeed, the body, as a body, does not act; it is merely set out to be divided and to be acted upon. 

Since every agent possesses an active power, it follows that a body does not act insofar as it is a body, but according to an active power that resides within it. The body, by its own nature, is ineffective and powerless; therefore, it acts only by participating in a power. Conversely, even incorporeal things participate in being affected when they are in bodies, being divided along with them and sharing in their divisible nature, even though they remain indivisible according to their own essence.

### PROPOSITION LXXXI.

**E**verything that is participated in separately <note>This refers to the Neoplatonic concept of "Participation," where a lower level of reality (like a physical object) shares in the qualities of a higher level (like a Form or Idea) without the higher level being diminished or physically present within the lower.</note> is present through a certain inseparable power, which it implants into the participant.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if the participated source itself exists separately from the participant and is not within it as if possessing its own substance there, it requires a certain middle ground <note>original: "medietate." A bridge or intermediary link that connects two different levels of reality.</note> to hold the one to the other—something more similar to the source being participated in than the participant itself. For if that source is separate, how could the participant share in it? Nor would the source itself [give of itself].

<summary>Proclus argues that physical bodies are inherently passive and divisible, while incorporeal realities are active and indivisible; he further explains that for a lower being to participate in a higher, separate reality, there must be an intermediary power linking them.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, incorporeal, body, passivity, active power, participation, divisibility, Neoplatonism</keywords>

### PROPOSITION LXXXII.
**E**very incorporeal thing that is self-reverting <note>original: "ad se conuersiuum." This describes a being that can reflect on itself or return to its own essence, rather than being "poured out" entirely into external things.</note>, when it is participated in by others, is participated in separately.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if it were participated inseparably, its activity would not be separable from the participant, just as its essence would not be. But if this were the case, it would not revert to itself. For if it has reverted, it must be distinct from the participant, existing as one thing while the participant exists as another. Therefore, if it is able to revert to itself, it is participated in separately when it is participated in by others.

### PROPOSITION LXXXIII.
**E**verything capable of knowing itself is entirely self-reverting.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or it is manifest that in the act of knowing itself, it is turned toward itself. In this case, the knower and the known are one, and the knowledge of itself is directed toward itself as the object of knowledge. This exists as a certain activity of the knower, but it is "of itself toward itself" because it is a knower of itself. Moreover, it has been demonstrated that if a thing reverts in its activity, it also reverts in its essence. For everything that is self-reverting in its acting also has an essence that returns to itself and exists within itself.

### PROPOSITION LXXXIIII.
**E**verything that always exists possesses infinite power.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if its substance <note>original: "substantia," referring to its underlying reality or "being."</note> is unfailing, then the power according to which it is what it is—and is able to exist—is also infinite. For if the power of its being were finite, it would eventually fail. And if that power failed, the existence of the thing which possesses that power would likewise fail,

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

...will cease, and it will no longer exist as an eternal being. It is necessary, therefore, that the power of that which always is, which contains it according to its essence, must be infinite.

### PROPOSITION LXXXV.
**E**verything that is always becoming <note>original: "semper fit." In Neoplatonic thought, "becoming" (generation) refers to the world of change and time, as opposed to the static realm of eternal Being.</note> possesses an infinite power of becoming.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if it is always becoming, the power of generation within it is unfailing. For if this power were finite, it would cease in infinite time. But if the power of becoming were to cease, that which is becoming would also cease—namely, that which exists according to that power—and it would no longer be always becoming. But it is assumed to be always becoming. Therefore, it possesses an infinite power of becoming.

### PROPOSITION LXXXVI.
**E**verything that truly is <note>original: "enter ens." A Latin rendering of the Greek *ontōs on*, referring to "Really Real" things—beings that have substantial, eternal existence rather than the fleeting existence of physical objects.</note> is infinite—not in terms of multitude or magnitude, but in terms of power alone.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or everything infinite exists either in multitude, in magnitude, or in power. That which truly is is indeed infinite, as it possesses an inextinguishable life, an unfailing existence, and an undiminished activity. Yet it is not infinite due to magnitude, since that which truly is—being self-subsistent <note>original: "per se subsistens." A being that exists in its own right and does not require a physical body or a subject to contain it.</note>—is without magnitude. For every truly self-subsistent being is indivisible and simple. Nor is it infinite due to multitude, for it is most uniform <note>original: "uniformissimum." In this context, it means possessing the highest degree of unity or "one-ness."</note>, being ordered very close to the One <note>The "One" is the supreme, absolute principle in Neoplatonism from which all reality emanates.</note> and most akin to the One.

Instead, it is infinite in terms of power. Therefore, it will be simultaneously indivisible and infinite. Indeed, the more it is one and the more it is indivisible, the more infinite it is. For a divided power becomes weak and finite. And altogether divisible powers are entirely finite. For the last things, which are furthest from the One, are in a way finite due to their division; but the first things, due to their indivisibility, are infinite. For division scatters and dissolves the power of each thing, whereas indivisibility, by binding and compressing...

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 24<-

binding it, it contains that life as unfailing and undiminished within itself. For infinity in terms of magnitude <note>original: "magnitudinem," referring to physical size or extension.</note> or multitude <note>original: "multitudinem," referring to a numerical count.</note> is a complete privation of impartibility <note>original: "impartibilitatis," the state of being indivisible or without parts.</note> and is a falling away from it. That which is closest to the impartible is the finite, while that which is furthest from it is the infinite, which has flowed out in every direction from the One <note>In Proclus's system, the "One" is the ultimate source of all reality; as things move "away" from this unity, they become more divided and less powerful.</note>. Therefore, infinite power does not reside in an infinity of multitude or magnitude. Indeed, infinite power coexists with impartibility. However, the infinite in multitude or magnitude is at the greatest distance from the impartible. If, then, that which truly is <note>original: "enter ens," the "really real" or substantial being.</note> were infinite in magnitude or multitude, it would not be infinitely powerful; but it is infinitely powerful, and therefore it is not infinite according to magnitude or multitude.

### PROPOSITION LXXXVII.

**E**very eternal thing is a being; but not every being is eternal.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or even things subject to generation <note>original: "generabilibus," referring to things in the physical world of change, which are born and die.</note> participate in some way in being, insofar as they are not "non-being" in the sense of that which in no way exists. If that which is subject to generation were in no way a being, then it could not be anything at all. Yet "eternity" <note>original: "sempiternum," referring to that which exists for all time or outside of time.</note> is in no way present in things subject to generation—especially those which do not even participate in the whole of time. Yet every eternal thing "always is"; for it participates in eternity, which grants the quality of "always being" to those things by which it is participated. Therefore, Being is participated in by more things than eternity is. Thus, Being stands above eternity in the hierarchy of reality. For those things which participate in eternity also participate in Being; but not all things that participate in Being also participate in eternity.

### PROPOSITION LXXXVIII.

**E**verything that truly is <note>original: "enter ens"</note> is either before eternity, in eternity, or a participant in eternity.

<meta>This page continues the "Elements of Theology" by Proclus. # PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: "Procli Diadochi." Proclus was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, centuries after Plato.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or it has been demonstrated that [Being] exists before eternity. But it is also within eternity. For eternity <note>original: "sempiternitas"</note> possesses the "always" itself along with "being." And that which participates in eternity—that is, every eternal thing—is called "eternal" <note>original: "sempiternum"</note> by its participation in "always" and in "being." For it possesses both of these, both "always" and "being," according to its participation. Eternity, however, possesses "always" primarily, but possesses "being" through participation. Yet Being itself <note>original: "ens ipsum"</note> is primarily "being."

### PROPOSITION LXXXIX.
**E**very primary being <note>original: "primo ens," the first or most fundamental level of existence.</note> consists of <term>Limit</term> <note>original: "termino." In Platonic thought, Limit (peras) provides form, definition, and stability.</note> and the <term>Infinite</term> <note>original: "interminato." The Infinite (apeiria) provides power, extension, and the capacity for growth or multiplication.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**I**f it possesses infinite power, it is clear that it is infinite, and it subsists from this infinity. If, however, it is impartible <note>indivisible or without parts</note> and uniform, it has participated in Limit. For that which participates in the One is limited <note>original: "terminatum"</note>. But [primary being] is simultaneously impartible and infinitely powerful. Therefore, every truly existing being <note>original: "enter ens"</note> consists of Limit and the Infinite.

### PROPOSITION XC.
**F**or all things that subsist from Limit and the Infinite, there pre-exist by themselves a first Limit and a first Infinity.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if the beings that exist by themselves <note>original: "a se sunt entia"</note> precede those that are parts of a mixture, as causes that are common and primary to all—and not just to some, but to all things simply—it is necessary that before that which is composed of both, there must exist the first Limit and the first Infinite. For the Limit within a mixture participates in the Infinite, and the Infinite participates in the Limit. But the "first" of any kind is nothing other than what it is. Therefore, the first Infinite must not be "limit-formed," nor should the first Limit be "infinite-formed." These two, therefore, exist primarily before the mixture.

PROPO-

<summary>This page establishes that all existence is composed of two fundamental principles: Limit and the Infinite. Proclus argues that these principles must exist in their pure, unmixed forms prior to combining to create the "Being" of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, Being, Eternity, Limit, Infinite, Metaphysics, Neoplatonism, Causality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Elements of Theology" by Proclus. Having established that Being is composed of Limit and the Infinite (Propositions 89-90), Proclus now explores the nature of Power (Dynamis) and the "First Infinity" that acts as the source for all infinite capacities in the universe.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 25

### PROPOSITION XCI.

**E**very <term>power</term> <note>original: "potentia," translating the Greek "dynamis." In this context, it refers to the capacity or internal force of a being to act or produce effects.</note> is either limited or infinite. But every limited power subsists <note>derives its existence</note> from an infinite power, and infinite power subsists from the first Infinity.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or powers that exist only for a certain time are limited, having fallen away from the infinity of eternal being. But the powers of eternal beings are infinite, because they never abandon their own existence <note>In Neoplatonic thought, things that exist forever must have an "infinite" power to sustain that existence without being exhausted.</note>.

### PROPOSITION XCII.

**E**very multitude of infinite powers depends upon one first Infinity, which is not like a participated power <note>a power that is "owned" by a specific object or being</note>, nor does it exist within powerful things, but exists by itself—not being the power of any participating thing, but the cause of all beings.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if <term>Being itself</term> <note>original: "ens ipsum," the first level of reality below the One.</note>, which is the first of beings, possesses power, it is nonetheless not "Power" itself; for it also possesses Limit. But the first Power is Infinity. For infinite powers are infinite through the participation in Infinity. Therefore, Infinity itself must exist before all powers. Because of this, even Being has infinite power, and all things have participated in Infinity. 

For the First <note>the One, or God</note> is not Infinity, for it is the measure of all things: existing as the Good and the One. Nor is Being [the First Infinity] itself, for Being is "infinite" <note>it possesses the quality of infinity</note> but it is not Infinity itself <note>the abstract principle or source</note>. Therefore, between the First and Being, there stands Infinity, the cause of all things possessing infinite power and the cause of all infinity found within beings.

### PROPOSITION XCIII.

**E**very infinite thing among beings is neither infinite to those things placed above it, nor to itself.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or that to which each thing is infinite, to that thing it also exists as uncircumscribed <note>limitless or undefinable</note>. But everything that exists among [beings] is limited both to itself and to all things that come before it. It only remains, therefore... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, transitioning to the next page.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that all "Power" in the universe derives from a single, primal "Infinity" located between the One and Being. He establishes that while beings can have infinite power, they are always limited relative to the higher principles that created them.</summary>

<keywords>Power, Dynamis, Infinity, The One, Being, Neoplatonism, Participation, Hierarchy of Being</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's "Elements of Theology," specifically concluding the discussion on how infinity is perceived by different levels of being and transitioning into the relationship between Infinity, Eternity, and Power.</meta>

### PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: "Procli Diadochi." Proclus was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, succeeding Plato's lineage.</note>

...to the inferior beings <note>beings lower in the cosmic hierarchy</note> it is infinite, because that which is infinite in them is that over which it is expanded to such an extent that it exists as imperceptible to all of them. For even if those things are stretched toward it as much as possible, it has something entirely secret from them. And even if all things enter into it, it nonetheless has something hidden from second-order beings, and something incomprehensible. And even if it expands its powers, it nonetheless possesses, through its union, something insuperable and enfolded; it is the explanation of their outward progression, yet it contains and terminates itself. It does not exist as infinite to itself, nor much less to superior beings, for it possesses a portion of the infinity that is within them. For the powers of more universal things are more infinite, since they are more universal and more closely ordered to the very first Infinity.

### PROPOSITION XCIIII.

**E**very eternity is a certain infinity, but not every infinity is eternity.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or many infinite things do not have the infinite because they always exist; examples include infinity according to multitude <note>numerical infinity</note>, infinity according to magnitude, and the infinity of matter, and any other such thing—whether it exists as infinite because it is untraversable, or because of the lack of termination <note>indeterminacy</note> in its essence. 

But it is clear that eternity is a form of infinity. For that which never fails <note>never ceases to be</note> is infinite. And this [eternity] is that which always possesses an unfailing substance. Infinity, therefore, exists before eternity. For that which is constitutive of more things and is more universal is more of a cause. Therefore, the first Infinity—Infinity-in-itself—is above sempiternity <note>everlastingness or perpetual duration</note> and exists before sempiternity.

### PROPOSITION XCV.

**E**very <term>power</term> <note>original: "potentia," or "dynamis" in Greek.</note> that exists as more unified is more infinite than a power that is multiplied <note>divided or fragmented</note>.

<summary>Proclus argues that infinity is relative to the observer's level in the hierarchy, defines eternity as a specific type of infinity, and posits that unity increases a power's infinite capacity.</summary>
<keywords>Infinity, Eternity, Power, Neoplatonism, Hierarchy of Being, Sempiternity, Unity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology." ### ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 26

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
**F**or if the first infinity is closest to the One, and belongs to the powers which are most akin to the One, then that which is further removed from the One is more infinite <note>original: "infinita est." In this context, Proclus means "more fragmented" or "less defined" rather than "greater in power."</note>. For once it is multiplied, it loses its uniform nature <note>original: "vniforme." The state of being one or singular.</note>, in which, by remaining within itself, it held superiority over others; it was contained because of its indivisibility. Indeed, in things that have parts, powers that are compressed <note>original: "contractæ." Concentrated or drawn together.</note> are multiplied in strength. But once they are divided, they are weakened.

->PROPOSITION. XCVI.<-
**E**very infinite power existing in a finite body is incorporeal.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
**F**or if it were corporeal, and this body were infinite, then an infinite thing would reside in a finite one <note>A logical impossibility in Aristotelian and Platonic physics: the container must be able to hold the thing contained.</note>. If, however, the body is finite, it is not a power insofar as it is a body. For if, insofar as it is a body, it is finite, and yet the power is infinite, then it will not be a power according to its bodily nature. Therefore, that power which exists as infinite within a finite body is incorporeal.

->PROPOSITION. XCVII.<-
**I**n every series, the primary cause communicates its own property to the entire series; and that which the cause is primarily, the series itself is through a process of decline <note>original: "remiſsionem." This term refers to the "slackening" or "diminishment" of a quality as it moves further from its source, like a sound fading as it travels.</note>.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
**F**or if it is the leader of the entire series, and all coordinated things are ordered toward that cause, it is surely clear that it imparts to all of them one single idea, through which they are ordered under the same series. For either they participate in a likeness to that cause without a cause, or the same property is in all of them because of it. But to say it is "without a cause" is impossible. For what is without a cause occurs by chance. However, chance will never exist in those things in which there is order, a connection to one another, and which always exist in the same way. Therefore, the entire series receives the property of its substance from that primary cause. But if it receives it from that cause, it is clear that it does so with a certain decline and a descent appropriate to secondary beings. For either it exists similarly in both the principle and in the secondary beings

<summary>This page argues that infinite power in a finite body must be incorporeal and establishes that every metaphysical series is defined by a primary cause whose traits are reflected, albeit more weakly, throughout the entire chain of being.</summary>

## THE WRITINGS OF PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: "Procli Diadochi." Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" (Diadochos) as head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...the property exists in all subsequent things; the first cause takes the lead, while the others receive their substance after it, whether similarly or dissimilarly. If this is so, it is clear that identity passes from the One to the multitude, and not the reverse; and that property which exists primarily in the One, excelling the series, pre-exists there, while it exists in the multitude in a secondary degree.

### PROPOSITION 98.

->**E**very separate cause is at once everywhere and nowhere.<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or it is everywhere through the communication <note>original: "communicatione." This refers to the way a higher principle shares its influence or power with lower things without being depleted or losing its own nature.</note> of its power. Indeed, this cause fulfills <note>original: "completiva." In Neoplatonic thought, a cause "completes" an effect by giving it the form or power it needs to exist.</note> all those things that are naturally suited to participate in it; it is the ruler of all secondary things and is present to all through the fertile processions <note>original: "progressibus." The "procession" (proodos) is the flow of energy or being from the divine source down into the material world.</note> of its illuminations. However, by its unmixed essence <note>original: "immixta vero essentia." Its core being remains distinct and uncontaminated by the physical or local world.</note> relative to those things that exist in a specific place, and by its surpassing purity, it is nowhere. For if it is separate from its effects, it stands above all things. Similarly, it is not contained by anything inferior to itself. For if it were only everywhere, it would not be prevented from being a cause... <note>The text here ends abruptly mid-sentence; the full argument usually concludes that if it were only "everywhere," it would be merged with its effects and lose its causal transcendence.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the argument that a series derives its identity from its primary cause and introduces the paradox that a divine or primary cause is simultaneously present to all things (everywhere) yet remains distinct and transcendent (nowhere).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology" (Elementatio Theologica), following the discussion of how causes relate to their respective series. ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 27

### PROPOSITION 99.

->**E**very unparticipated <term>unparticipated: Greek *amethekton*. In Neoplatonism, this refers to a primary principle that remains transcendent and independent, not divided or "shared out" among the things that depend on it.</term> thing, insofar as it is unparticipated, does not derive its existence from another cause. Rather, it is itself the principle and cause of all participated things; thus, every principle in every series <term>series: Greek *seira*. A vertical chain or hierarchy of beings (like a "string" of pearls) where each level derives its character from the one above it.</term> is unbegotten.<-

<note>A circular library stamp is impressed upon the page here, marking it as part of a formal collection.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if it is unparticipated, it has obtained the primary rank in its own series, and it does not proceed from others; for if it did, it would no longer be the first. Furthermore, it would receive this very property—according to which it is unparticipated—from something else. But if it were inferior to others and proceeded from them, it would not proceed insofar as it is unparticipated, but insofar as it is a participant. For it participates in those things from which it descends, and those things which participate are not primary. 

However, that which is unparticipated is primary. Therefore, it does not exist from a cause insofar as it is unparticipated. For if it were from a cause, it would be a participant and not unparticipated. Insofar as it is unparticipated, it is the cause of participating things, and is not itself a participant in others.

### PROPOSITION 100.

->**E**very series of universals is referred back to an unparticipated cause and principle. Furthermore, all unparticipated things depend on the One, which is the principle of all things.<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if every series undergoes the same thing <note>original: "idem aliquid patitur." This refers to the shared identity or characteristic that defines all members of a specific hierarchy.</note>, there is something presiding in each series as the cause of this identity. Just as all beings come from the One, so too does every series come from a single source. Now, all unparticipated unities <note>original: "vnitates." These are the *Henads*, or divine unities, that serve as the highest principles of various chains of being.</note> are reduced to the One itself, since they are all analogous to the One. 

Therefore, insofar as they undergo the same thing and possess an analogy to the One, their return <note>original: "reditus." Greek *epistrophe*. The Neoplatonic movement where an effect "turns back" toward its cause to find its fulfillment.</note> to the One itself is accomplished. And insofar as they all originate from the One, none of them is the ultimate principle; rather, they exist from it as from a principle. Yet, insofar as each is unparticipated, each is a principle. Existing therefore as the principles of certain things, they nevertheless depend upon the Principle of all. For it is the Principle of all, in which all things participate. Indeed, all things participate in the first alone...

<summary>This page argues that the primary member of any metaphysical hierarchy (the "unparticipated") must be uncaused within that hierarchy and that all such primary principles ultimately depend on the "One."</summary>

->**PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR**<-

...indeed not all, but some. Therefore, that principle is also simply primary. Others, however, are primary relative to a certain order, but are not simply primary.

### PROPOSITION 101.

->**F**or all things that participate in Intellect <term>Intellect: Latin *mens*, Greek *nous*. In Neoplatonism, this represents the level of divine thought and the Forms.</term>, the leader is unparticipated Intellect; for those participating in Life, it is Life; and for those participating in Being, it is Being. Of these three, Being exists before Life, and Life exists before Intellect.<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or since in every order of beings <term>beings: Latin *entia*. This refers to anything that exists at any level of reality.</term> the unparticipated principles exist before the participants, it is necessary that prior to intellectual things, there is Intellect; prior to living things, there is Life; and prior to beings, there is Being.

Since, indeed, that which is the cause of more things precedes those which are the causes of fewer, Being will be the most primary among them. For Being is present to all things to which Life and Intellect are present. Everything that lives and partakes in intellection necessarily exists, but the reverse is not true: not all beings live and understand <note>original: "non enim entia omnia, viuunt, & intelligunt." Proclus is arguing that Being is a broader category than Life or Mind. For example, a stone has Being but not Life; a plant has Life but not Intellect.</note>. 

Second, however, is Life. For all things to which Intellect is present also participate in Life, but not the reverse. For many things live, yet are left without the experience of knowledge. 

Third is Intellect. For everything that knows in any way both lives and exists. If, therefore, Being is the cause of more things, Life of fewer, and Intellect of even fewer, then Being is the most primary, followed by Life, and then Intellect.

### PROPOSITION 102.

->**A**ll things that are beings in any way whatsoever consist of Limit and the Infinite, through the primary Being. All living things are self-moving through primary Life. All knowing things participate in knowledge through the primary Intellect.<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if that which is unparticipated in each series <term>series: a chain of related things descending from a single source.</term> communicates its own property to all those things falling under the same series, it is surely clear that even the most primary Being communicates to all things both Limit <note>original: "finem"</note> and Infinity <note>original: "infinitatem"</note>... 

<summary>Proclus establishes the ontological hierarchy where Being is the most universal cause, followed by Life and then Intellect, while also introducing the foundational principles of Limit and the Infinite.</summary>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 28

...existing primarily from these. And Life is the movement which is within itself. For Life is the first progression and motion from the stable substance <term>substance: Latin *substantia*, Greek *ousia*. The underlying reality or essence of a thing.</term> of Being; and Intellect is knowledge, for the summit of all knowledge is in Intellect, and Intellect is the primary cognitive power <note>original: "primum cognoscitiuum". Proclus concludes the demonstration of Proposition 102 by identifying the characteristics of the triad: Being is stability, Life is motion, and Intellect is knowledge.</note>.

### PROPOSITION 103.

->**A**ll things are in all things, but in each appropriately.<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or indeed, in Being there is both Life and Intellect; and in Life, there is Being and Intellect; and in Intellect, there is Being and Life. Yet all things exist in one place intellectually, in another vitally, and in another according to the manner of Being <note>original: "enter". This adverb describes things existing in the mode of Being or Essence.</note>. 

For since every thing exists either according to cause, or according to existence <note>original: "existentiam". In this context, it refers to the specific level where the thing is "at home" or fully itself.</note>, or according to participation; in the first <note>i.e., Being</note>, the remaining two <note>Life and Intellect</note> exist according to cause. In the middle <note>Life</note>, the first exists by participation and the third <note>Intellect</note> according to cause. And in the third <note>Intellect</note>, those that come before it exist by participation. 

Therefore, in Being, Life and Intellect pre-exist. Since each thing takes its character according to its actual existence—neither according to cause (for it is the cause of the others) nor according to participation (for it receives what it participates in from elsewhere)—Life and Intellect exist there in the manner of Being; they are "essential life" and "essential intellect." 

And in Life, Being is present according to participation, while Intellect is present according to cause. Yet both exist there vitally <note>original: "vitaliter". In the level of Life, even the "Intellect" that is to come exists as a form of living energy.</note>. For its existence is according to this level. 

Finally, in Intellect, both Life and Essence are present by participation, but both exist intellectually. For the "being" of Intellect is cognitive, and its "life" is knowledge.

### PROPOSITION 104.

->**E**verything that is primarily eternal has both its essence and its activity eternal.<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if it has primarily obtained the property of eternity <term>eternity: Latin *sempiternitas*, Greek *aiōn*. In Neoplatonism, eternity is the "now" of the divine realm, distinct from the "duration" of time.</term>, it has not obtained it in one part while not in another. But everywhere...

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s "Elements of Theology." It completes the proof for Proposition 104 regarding the nature of eternal beings and introduces Propositions 105 and 106, which distinguish between immortality and eternity and identify the "middle ground" between eternity and time.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI". Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, centuries after Plato.</note>

...that participates. For if it participates according to action <term>action: Latin *actionem*, Greek *energeia*. This refers to the outward activity or operation of a being.</term>, but does not participate according to essence <term>essence: Latin *essentiam*, Greek *ousia*. The core being or "whatness" of a thing.</term>, this is impossible; for the action would then be more excellent than the essence. Alternatively, if it participates according to essence but not according to action, the "primarily eternal" would be the same as that which participates in time at the primary level. In that case, time would measure the essence of certain primary things, but eternity would measure nothing, even though eternity is more excellent than all time. This is because the primarily eternal would not be contained, according to its essence, by eternity itself. Therefore, every primarily eternal being possesses both an eternal essence and an eternal action.

### PROPOSITION 105.

**E**verything immortal is eternal, but not everything eternal is immortal.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if a thing is immortal, it is that which always participates in life. That which is a participant in life always is also a participant in being; and that which is always living, always exists. Therefore, everything immortal is eternal. For the "immortal" is that which cannot receive death and is always living; whereas the "eternal" is that which cannot receive non-being and is always existing. 

However, if there are many beings that are either more excellent than life or inferior to it, they would not be receptive of immortality, yet they would still be "always beings." Therefore, not every eternal thing is immortal. It is clear that many "always beings" are not immortal; for there are certain beings devoid of life <note>In the Neoplatonic hierarchy, some levels of reality—like the Forms of inanimate objects—have eternal "Being" but do not possess the self-moving quality of "Life."</note> which are nonetheless always existing and incorruptible. For as "being" relates to "life," so "eternal" relates to "immortal." Life, which cannot be taken away, is the immortal itself; and being, which cannot be diminished, is the eternal. Being, however, is more capacious <note>original: "capacius". Meaning more comprehensive or broader in scope.</note> than life; therefore, the eternal is more capacious than the immortal.

### PROPOSITION 106.

**B**etween that which is entirely eternal—both according to essence and according to action—and that which has its essence in time, there is a medium: that which is in one respect eternal, but in another respect measured by time.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

<summary>This page argues that while all immortal things are eternal because they possess life and being, not all eternal things are immortal, as some exist beyond the level of life. It also introduces the concept of a transitional state between pure eternity and pure time.</summary>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 29

#### DEMONSTRATION.
<note>This text concludes the proof for Proposition 106: "Everything which is entirely eternal is eternal both in its essence and its activity."</note>

**F**or that which has an essence <term>essence: Latin *essentiam*, Greek *ousia*. The core nature or fundamental reality of a being.</term> encompassed by time is in all respects temporal. For much earlier than this, it must have also received a temporal action <term>action: Latin *actionem*, Greek *energeia*. The active expression or operation of a being’s nature.</term>. But that which is in all respects temporal is entirely dissimilar to the eternal. All progressions <note>original: "Progresſsiones". In Proclus's system, reality "progresses" from the One down through levels of decreasing perfection. For this to happen, there must be bridge-entities between the levels.</note>, however, occur through similar things. Therefore, there must be something intermediate between these. Thus, either that which is eternal in essence but temporal in action is the medium, or vice versa <note>original: "èconuerfo".</note>. But this [the latter] is impossible; for the action would then be more excellent than the essence. But this is impossible <note>In ancient philosophy, the "what" of a thing (essence) is always superior to "what it does" (action); a cause must be greater than its effect.</note>. It remains, therefore, that the other [the former] is the medium.

### PROPOSITION 107.

**E**verything which is in a certain respect eternal and in a certain respect temporal, is simultaneously both being and generation.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or everything eternal is "being" <term>being: Latin *ens*, Greek *on*. Real, unchanging existence.</term>, and that which is measured by time is "generation" <term>generation: Latin *generatio*, Greek *genesis*. The realm of change, becoming, and physical reality.</term>. Therefore, if the same thing participates in time and eternity, though not in the same respect, the same thing will be both being and generation, yet not both according to the same aspect of itself.

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

...is dissimilar, and as the particular is to the universal, and as one order <term>order: Latin *ordinis*, Greek *taxis*. A specific rank or level in the hierarchy of reality, such as the level of Intellects or the level of Souls.</term> is to another. But toward the universal of the same series, it is similar, because of the communion of property <note>In Neoplatonism, things in the same "series" share a specific "property" or characteristic (like "goodness" or "life") that flows from a single source.</note>. And it is coordinated most closely to the superior property because of their analogous subsistence. It is surely clear that through these media, a conversion <term>conversion: Latin *conversio*, Greek *epistrophē*. The process by which a created thing "turns back" to look toward and unite with its cause.</term> is naturally suited to occur toward that higher principle, just as through similar things one reaches a similar thing. For a particular is similar to another particular, and that which is of the same series is its own. However, that universal of the superior series is dissimilar in both respects.

### PROPOSITION 109.

**E**very particular intellect participates in that unity which is above intellect—the most primary unity—both through the universal <term>universal: Latin *vniuerſale*, Greek *katholou*. The general principle or "whole" that contains the particulars within it.</term> and through the coordinate particular unity. And every particular soul participates in the universal intellect both through the universal soul and through the particular intellect. And every particular nature of a body participates in the universal soul both through the universal nature and through the particular soul.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or every particular participates in the unity that is in the superior order, either through its own universal or through the particular which is in that higher order and is coordinated to it.

### PROPOSITION 110.

**O**f all things ordered according to each series, the first things—those joined to their own unity—are able to participate by analogy <note>original: "per analogiam". Analogy is the mathematical and logical tool Proclus uses to bridge the gap between different levels of reality.</note> in those things which are established most closely in the superior order. But the more imperfect things, and those remote from their own principle, are not naturally suited to enjoy those higher things.

**B**ecause some things are kindred to those higher levels, having obtained a more excellent and divine nature in their own order, while others have progressed further <note>original: "progreſſa ſunt". Meaning a supportive or servant-like role in the cosmic hierarchy, rather than a leading or causal one.</note> role, rather than a primary and principal role in the series, having progressed...

<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*. ->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 30<-

...progression, of necessity, some things are naturally linked to those that belong to a superior order <term>order: Latin *ordinis*, Greek *taxis*. A specific rank or level in the hierarchy of reality, such as the level of Intellects or the level of Souls.</term>, while others are not joined to it. For not all things possess equal dignity, even if they arise from the same arrangement. Indeed, there is not just one principle <note>original: *ratio*. Here meaning a governing logic, proportion, or foundational reason.</note> for all; rather, all things proceed from their own unity as if from one source and toward one end. Therefore, they do not all obtain the same power. While some can continuously receive the participations <term>participation: Greek *methexis*. The Neoplatonic concept where a lower entity "shares in" or derives its qualities from a higher, more perfect source.</term> of the higher beings, others—having become dissimilar through the longest possible progressions from their primary principles—are deprived of such power.

### PROPOSITION 111.

**O**f every intellectual series <term>series: Latin *series*, Greek *seira*. A "chain" or lineage of beings that share a common characteristic, descending from a single source.</term>, some are divine intellects receiving the participations of the gods, while others are merely intellects. And of every psychic <note>original: *animasticæ*, from Greek *psychikos*. Pertaining to the Soul (Psyche).</note> series, some are intellectual souls, bound to their own intellects, while others are merely souls. And of every corporeal nature, some have souls placed over them from above, while others are merely natures, lacking the presence of souls. <note>Proclus is describing a "trickle-down" effect. At every level of reality (Mind, Soul, Body), the highest members of that level are "perfected" by the level above them, while the lowest members possess only the bare traits of their own level.</note>

<summary>This page explains why not all beings have the same capacity to connect with the divine, and establishes a three-tiered hierarchy (Intellect, Soul, Body) where each level contains both "superior" members linked to the level above and "ordinary" members.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s Elements of Theology, moving from the general principles of series and progression into the specific nature of the Divine. It concludes the demonstration of Proposition 112 regarding the relationship between causes and their related effects, and presents Propositions 113 and 114 concerning the unity and self-perfection of the Gods.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

...of the universes. Therefore, just as those primary things are, so do these also obtain a related form, according to their nature. And they appear to be such, through the property of their substance, as those things which precede them.

### PROPOSITION 113.

**E**very divine number <term>number: Latin *numerus*, Greek *arithmos*. In Neoplatonism, a "number" is not just a digit but a structured group or "multitude" of entities belonging to a specific rank of reality.</term> has the form of the One.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if the divine number has as its preceding cause the One itself—just as the intellectual series has the Intellect as its source, and the psychic series has the Soul—and if the multitude is everywhere analogous to its cause, it is certainly clear that the divine number is also uniform <note>original: *uniformis*. Having the character or "form" of the One.</note>. This is because the One itself is God. This is true insofar as the Good itself and the One are the same. Indeed, the Good itself and God are the same. For that above which nothing exists, and which all things desire <note>A reference to the Aristotelian and Platonic definition of the Good as the ultimate object of desire for all existence.</note>, is God; he is the one from whom all things proceed and toward whom all things tend. And this is the Good. If, therefore, there is a multitude of Gods, that multitude is uniform. That this is clear is evident: for every principal cause is the leader of its own multitude, and is similar and related to it.

### PROPOSITION 114.

**E**very God is a self-perfect unity. And every self-perfect unity <term>self-perfect unity: Latin *vnitas per se perfecta*, Greek *autoteles henas*. A "Henad" or divine unit that is complete in itself and serves as a fountain of existence for lower things.</term> is a God.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if the number of unities is twofold—as has been shown previously—and some are indeed self-perfect, while others are merely illuminations <note>original: *lumina*. Lower unities that are "participated" or "shined" into souls or bodies, rather than existing independently.</note> from them; and if the divine number is related and annexed to the One itself and the Good itself, then the divine unities are self-perfect. And conversely, if there is a self-perfect unity, it is a God. For just as a unity is the One itself, and as a self-perfect thing it is most excellently related to the Good itself, and through both it participates in the divine property, so also that which is self-perfect is a God.

I <column-break/> CORE-

<summary>Proclus argues that all divine beings (Gods) share the nature of 'The One' and 'The Good,' and establishes that every God is a self-sufficient, primary Unity known as a Henad.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, The One, The Good, Henads, Divine Number, Self-perfection, Neoplatonism, Participation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*. Having established that the Gods are self-perfect and uniform in the previous propositions, Proclus now moves to define their ontological status as being "beyond" the traditional categories of Being, Life, and Mind. This page covers the end of a corollary and the full text of Propositions 115 and 116.</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 31

## COROLLARY.

But if there were a truth that was not yet perfect in itself, or even a substance that was perfect in itself but was not a "unity," it would be categorized into a different rank because of the change in its essential property.

### PROPOSITION 115.

**E**very God is beyond being <term>beyond being: Latin *supereſſentialis*, Greek *hyperousios*. This indicates a state of reality that is "above" or "prior to" existence itself. In this system, to "be" something is already a limitation that the Gods transcend.</term>, and beyond life, and beyond intellect.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if every God is a unity that is perfect in itself, but each of these—being, life, and intellect—is not a unity but a "unified thing" <note>original: *vnitum*. This refers to something that possesses unity as a gift or attribute, rather than being Oneness itself.</note>, it is surely clear that every God is above all the aforementioned categories: being, life, and intellect. 

For if these categories differ from one another, yet all things are present in all things—each one existing as all things—then the God would no longer be just "one." <note>Proclus argues that if a God were defined as "Being" or "Intellect," he would be composed of parts or definitions, losing the absolute simplicity required of a divine Unity.</note> Furthermore, if the First <note>The "First" refers to the One, or the Good, the absolute source of all reality.</note> is beyond being, and every God belongs to the first series insofar as he is a God, then each individual God will be beyond being. 

That the First is beyond being is clear. For "to be" for the One is not the same thing as the "being" of an essence. Nor is it the same thing as being "unified." If they were not different, then either both would be the First—and there would be something else besides the One, a participant in the One rather than the One itself—or it would be one of the two. 

But if the First were indeed an "essence," it would stand in need of the One. It is impossible, however, for the Good and the First to be in need. Therefore, the One is that alone. Consequently, it is beyond being. Now, if that which each thing is primarily imparts its own property to its entire series, then the entire divine number is beyond being. 

Indeed, every principal cause produces things similar to itself before it produces things dissimilar. If, therefore, the first God is beyond being, then all the Gods are beyond being. In this way, they will be similar to their source. If they were mere essences, they would be produced by a "first Essence," appearing as mere unities of essences <note>This would mean they were subordinated to the category of "Being" rather than being the supreme rulers of it.</note>.

### PROPOSITION 116.

**E**very God is participable, except for the One. <note>The "One" refers to the absolute, unparticipated First Principle. All other Gods (henads) can be "participated" in, meaning they can irradiate their power into lower levels of reality like souls or bodies.</note>

I 2 DE-

<summary>Proclus demonstrates that because the Gods are absolute Unities, they must exist above the levels of Being, Life, and Mind. He also distinguishes between the "One" (the unparticipated source) and the "Gods" (who are participable unities).</summary>
<keywords>beyond being, supereſſentialis, hyperousios, unity, First Principle, participable, Neoplatonism, divine number</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*, specifically the section detailing the nature of the divine "Henads" (individual Gods). The previous page established that the Gods are "beyond being." This page concludes the demonstration of that principle and moves into Propositions 117 and 118, which describe the Gods as the "measures" of all things and explain how all reality pre-exists within them.</meta>

# THE SUCCESSOR PROCLUS

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or it is clear that the First <note>The "First" refers to the absolute One, the source of all existence.</note> is unparticipated <term>unparticipated: original Latin *imparticipabilis*. This means the source is not "used up" or "divided" by the things it creates; it remains independent and separate.</term>; otherwise, if it were participated, it would belong to a specific thing. Consequently, it would no longer be the cause of all things alike—both those that are present and those that are yet to be—in the same way. 

However, we shall now demonstrate that the other unities <note>In Proclus’s system, these "unities" or "henads" are the individual Gods who follow the absolute One.</note> are indeed participated. For if there were another unparticipated unity after the First, how would it differ from the One? Either it exists in the same way as the First—but then how is one "primary" and the other "secondary"?—or it exists in a different way. In that case, one is the "One itself," while the other is both "one and not-one." But this second unity, if it has no substance, will be only "one." If, however, there is some other substance besides the "one," then that "one" will be participated in by the "not-one." And while the One is perfect in itself, it is that through which the substance is joined to the divine. Therefore, this unity is again a God. Because the "not-one" exists, it subsists through the participation of the One. Every unity that exists after the One is therefore participated, and every God is participated.

## PROPOSITION 117.

**E**very God is a measure of beings.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if every God possesses the form of the One, he defines and measures all the multitudes of beings. Since all multitudes are by their own nature indefinite <note>The Greek philosophical concept of "the indefinite" suggests a chaotic state of "too muchness" that requires a boundary or limit to become a specific thing.</note>, they are defined through the One. The One, which is the "measurer" and the "limiter," seeks to bring anything it encounters into a state of limitation according to its own power. Thus, even that which was indefinite becomes uniform <note>original: *uniforme*. Meaning "brought into a single form" or "made consistent."</note> through participation. This process causes a departure from infinity and lack of boundary. And the more "uniform" a thing becomes, the less it is unmeasured and indefinite. Therefore, every multitude of beings is measured by the divine unities.

## PROPOSITION 118.

**E**verything that exists in the Gods pre-exists in them according to their own character. And their character is uniform and super-essential <term>super-essential: existing "above" or "prior to" the level of basic existence or substance.</term>. Therefore, all things exist within the Gods in a uniform and super-essential manner.

### DEMON-
<note>The text breaks here mid-word as the demonstration continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that while the absolute One is independent, the individual Gods are "participated" in by reality, serving as the divine measures that give limit and form to the otherwise chaotic multitude of beings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*, specifically the demonstration following Proposition 118, which argues that the Gods contain the causes of all things. It transitions into Proposition 119, defining the Gods as "Goodness itself," and begins Proposition 120 regarding Divine Providence.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 32

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or everything exists in one of three ways: either by cause, by essence, or by participation. Since the divine number <note>original Latin: *divinus numerus*. This refers to the collection of "Henads" or individual Gods that proceed from the absolute One.</note> is the first of all numbers, nothing within the Gods exists by participation. Instead, all things exist there either by essence or by cause. 

Furthermore, whatever things have pre-received the status of causes of all things have done so in accordance with their own union. For everything that rules over secondary things by way of being their cause possesses the cause of those inferior things according to its own nature. Therefore, all things exist in the Gods uniformly and superessentially <term>superessentially: original Latin *superessentialiter*. In Neoplatonic thought, this describes a state "beyond being." The Gods exist at a level higher than the "essence" or "substance" found in the physical or even intellectual worlds.</term>.

## PROPOSITION 119.

**Every God subsists according to superessential goodness, and is good neither by habit nor by essence.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For both habits <note>original Latin: *habitus*. In this philosophical context, a "habit" is a quality that something "possesses" or "acquires," rather than what it fundamentally "is."</note> and essences are assigned a secondary and more distant order from the Gods. The Gods exist superessentially. For if the First <note>The absolute One, the source of all.</note> is the One and is Goodness itself—and insofar as it is the One, it is the Good, and insofar as it is the Good, it is the One—then the entire series of Gods is both "uniform" <note>original Latin: *uniformis*; having the nature of the One.</note> and "good-form" <note>original Latin: *boniformis*; having the nature of the Good.</note>. 

Insofar as there is unity, there is goodness; and where there is goodness, there is also unity. Those who have proceeded from the First and follow after it are likewise good-form and uniform. Since the First is the One and is Goodness itself, it follows that all Gods are unities and goodnesses. Just as the "One" is superessential to the Gods, so too is the "Good" superessential to them; it is not something else existing apart from the One. For each God is not one thing and then, as a result, good; rather, each is simply Good. Likewise, they are not one thing and then, as a result, one; but they are simply One.

->**ON PROVIDENCE.**<-

## PROPOSITION 120.

**Every God, in their own essence, possesses the power to provide for all things; and primary providence resides in the Gods.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

<summary>This page explains that the Gods contain the causes of all things "superessentially" and defines the nature of the Gods as pure goodness and unity. It concludes by establishing that Divine Providence is an inherent part of the divine essence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 120 from Proclus’s Elements of Theology, which explores the nature of Divine Providence. It then introduces Proposition 121, defining the three core characteristics of any divine being: goodness, power, and knowledge.</meta>

# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS’S
### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or all other things that exist after the Gods exercise providence <note>original Latin: *provident*. In this context, "providence" refers to the caring or governing activity of a higher being over a lower one.</note> only through their communion with the Gods. However, for the Gods, providence is innate. If it is the specific nature of the "good" to communicate benefits to those things for which it provides, then this is a property of a provider. Since all Gods are "goodnesses" itself, they must either communicate themselves to others, or not at all. If they did not communicate themselves, there would be no good found in secondary things <note>By "secondary things," Proclus means everything in the universe that is not a God, such as souls, nature, and matter.</note>—for where else could that which is called "good by participation" come from, if not from those who possess these properties primarily? 

But if they do communicate themselves, they communicate good things, and in this way, they provide for all things. Therefore, providence exists primarily in the Gods. This is even indicated by the name itself: for "Providence" <note>original Latin: *prouidentia*; Greek: *pronoia*</note> is an action that is "before the intellect" <note>original Latin: *pro nu*. Proclus is using an etymological argument: *Pro-noia* (Providence) means being *pro* (before) the *nous* (intellect). This suggests divine care comes from a level higher than rational thought.</note>. Therefore, by the very fact of their being Gods and being "goodnesses," they provide for all things, filling all things with that goodness which exists even before the intellect.

## PROPOSITION 121.

**Every divine being possesses goodness as its very essence, a power that is uniform, and a knowledge that is secret and incomprehensible to all secondary beings at once.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

**I**f a divine being provides for all things, there must be within it a power that contains those things for which it provides. Through this power—which is unsurpassable and cannot be described—it has filled all things with itself and subjected all things to itself. For every primary cause that rules over others dominates and contains them according to its own nature because of its abundance of power. 

And truly, this most primary power in the Gods does not rule over some things while failing to rule over others; rather, it commands the powers of all beings equally within itself. This is not a power that exists as an "essence" <note>original Latin: *essentialis*. Here, Proclus means it is not a limited, defined substance.</note>, nor even less is it "without essence." Instead, it is innate to the very existence of the Gods and is superessential <term>superessential: original Latin *supereſſentialis*. This describes a state of being that is "above" or "beyond" the standard categories of existence or substance.</term>. 

Furthermore, the limits of all forms of knowledge pre-exist uniformly within the Gods. Because of this divine knowledge—which is set apart from the universe—all other forms of knowledge have come to exist. This divine knowledge is not "intellectual," and even less is it like those forms of knowledge that exist after the intellect <note>Proclus distinguishes between "Divine Knowledge" (which is unified and total) and "Intellectual Knowledge" (which involves thinking and distinction).</note>. But according to its own property...

<summary>This page concludes the argument that Providence is an innate quality of the Gods (Prop 120) and begins defining the nature of divine power and knowledge as "superessential" (Prop 121).</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, Providence, Pronoea, Goodness, Superessential, Divine Power, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the demonstration of Proposition 121 and introduces Proposition 122 of Proclus’s Elements of Theology. It focuses on how the divine nature remains transcendent and "unmixed" even while actively providing for and sustaining all lower levels of reality.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 33

...divine property, which is established above the mind <note>original Latin: *supra mētem*. Proclus argues that the nature of the Gods is "supra-intellectual," meaning it exists at a level higher than even the purest rational thought.</note>. If, therefore, this knowledge is divine, it is both secret <note>original Latin: *arcana*. In Neoplatonism, divine knowledge is "secret" not because it is hidden by choice, but because it is of a different, higher order than human logic.</note> and uniform. If it is power, it is unbounded by all and encompasses all in the same way. If it is goodness, it defines their essence. For even if all things are in the Gods—knowledge, power, and goodness—their essence is nevertheless characterized by the Best, and their existence is according to the Best; and this, truly, is Goodness itself.

## PROPOSITION CXXII.

**E**very divine being both exercises providence for secondary things and is separate from the things provided for; neither does providence diminish its unmixed and uniform excellence, nor does its separate union obscure its providence.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or while remaining in their own uniformity and in their essence, they fill all things with their power; and all things capable of participating in them enjoy the goods which they are able to receive according to the measure of their own substance. For the goods shine forth upon all existing things by the very fact of the Gods' being—or rather, by their "pre-being" <note>original Latin: *praeesse*. This refers to the idea that the Gods exist "before" or "above" the level of mere Being.</note>. For since they are nothing other than "goodnesses," they grant goods abundantly to all things by their very being. They do not make this distribution according to human reasoning or calculation, but because while some things receive these goods according to their own worthiness, the Gods grant them according to their own essence.

Therefore, they do not receive any relational attachment <note>original Latin: *respectus*. Proclus is emphasizing that even though the Gods "care" for the world, they do not become emotionally or physically entangled with it; they remain unaffected by what they govern.</note> to the things for which they provide; for by the very fact of being what they are, they make all things good, and everything that acts by its very being acts without such attachment. For a relation is an addition to one's being, and therefore contrary to nature. Nor does the fact that they are separate take away their providence; for if they did so, they would (and it is a sin to even say it) take away their own essence, the property of which is goodness. For that which is "good-form" <note>original Latin: *boniforme*. A technical term meaning "having the form or character of the Good."</note> does not simply descend into everything capable of participating in it; rather, the "Best" is that which is "beneficent" <note>original Latin: *benificum*. Proclus distinguishes between simply *being* good and actively *doing* good/conferring benefits.</note>. Therefore, either no existing things will have this quality, or else the Gods must have it, since they exist before all things. For a greater good cannot exist in those things that are good only by participation, while a lesser good exists in the primary Goods themselves.

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

## PROPOSITION 123.

**E**very divine being, in itself, is ineffable and unknown to all secondary things due to its super-essential <note>original Latin: *supereffentialem*. This is a translation of the Greek *hyperousios*, describing a state that is "above" or "beyond" Being or Essence itself.</note> union; but it is comprehensible and knowable through its participants. Therefore, only the First <note>The "First" refers to the One, the absolute pinnacle of Proclus’s hierarchy, which is entirely "unparticipated" and therefore totally beyond the reach of knowledge.</note> is entirely unknown, since it is unparticipated.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or all knowledge through reason is of beings, and finds its grasp of truth among beings; for it reaches toward intellections and exists within them. The Gods, however, are above all beings. Therefore, the divine is not graspable by opinion, nor by discursive reason <note>original Latin: *rationis discursione*. This refers to the human process of thinking through things step-by-step.</note>, nor by intellection. For every being is either sensible—and thus a matter of opinion—or "truly being" <note>original Latin: *enter ens*. A literal rendering of the Greek *ontos on*, referring to the highest form of reality accessible to the mind.</note>—and thus a matter of the intellect. Or, it is between these: a being that is simultaneously generated, and thus a matter of reason. If, therefore, the Gods are super-essential and exist before beings, there is no opinion of them, nor science, nor discursive thought <note>original Latin: *dianoea*, from the Greek *dianoia*.</note>, nor intellection. 

But they are known from the properties of those things which depend on them, and this is by necessity. For according to the properties of the things participated, the differences of the participants are also divided. Not everything participates in everything, for there is no composition of things that are entirely dissimilar, nor can just anything be a participant in anything else. Instead, that which is kindred is joined to each thing, and proceeds from it.

## PROPOSITION 124.

**E**very God knows divisible things indivisibly, and things in time timelessly; he knows things that are not necessary with necessity, and things that are mutable immutably; and in general, he knows all things in a manner more excellent than the order of the things themselves.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if everything whatever that exists among the Gods exists according to their own property, it is surely clear that they do not know according to... <note>The text breaks off here, as the demonstration continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the inherent unknowability of the Gods (who exist beyond Being) except through their effects, and argues that divine knowledge is superior to its objects, knowing temporal things timelessly and divisible things indivisibly.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, super-essential, ineffable, divine knowledge, the One, intellection, participation</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues Proposition 124, which concludes Proclus’s argument on how the Gods possess knowledge. It then introduces Proposition 125, which explains how a divine being "proceeds" or flows down through the different levels of reality while still remaining essentially itself.</meta>

ELEMENTA THEOLOGICA. 34

...[the knowledge of the Gods] will not be according to the nature of inferior things, but according to their own distinguished excellence. Therefore, their knowledge of things that are many and capable of suffering <note>original Latin: *passibilium*, referring to things that can be acted upon or changed by external forces.</note> will be uniform and without passion <note>original Latin: *absque passione*. In Neoplatonic philosophy, "passion" refers to undergoing change or being affected by something else; the Gods know changing things without being changed themselves.</note>. Furthermore, even if the object of knowledge is divisible, divine knowledge remains indivisible; if it is of things that have parts, it is partless; if of things that change, it is immutable; if of things that are contingent <note>original Latin: *contingentium*, referring to things that may or may not happen.</note>, it is necessary; and if of things that are indefinite, it is definite. For the divine does not receive knowledge into itself from inferior things, such that the knowledge takes on the nature of the thing known; rather, inferior things are bounded around the definiteness of the Gods, they change around the immutable, they receive the "impassible" in a way that involves suffering, and that which is beyond time they receive in a temporal way. For it is possible for inferior things to be transcended by the superior, but for the Gods to receive anything from the inferior into themselves is forbidden <note>original Latin: *nephas*, a term implying a violation of cosmic or divine law.</note>.

## PROPOSITION 125.

**Every God, from whichever order they began, proceeds through all following things to reveal themselves. They are always multiplying their communications and distributing them, yet they preserve the distinct character of their own substance.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

**S**ince "progressions" <note>original Latin: *progressiones*. In this system, reality flows out from the One like light from a sun, becoming "dimmer" or less intense as it moves further away.</note> occur through a "remission" <note>original Latin: *remissionem*, a technical term for the lessening of power or intensity as one moves down the hierarchy of being.</note>, primary things everywhere multiply as they descend into secondary things. Things that "proceed" do so according to their likeness to the things that produced them, and they receive their own proper order. Thus, the whole is in a way both the same and other: the thing proceeding remains with its source, appearing as something else due to the "remission," yet not departing from identity with the source due to its continuity with it. Whatever character the source has among the first things, it establishes that same character among the secondary things, preserving an unbreakable communion within the series. Therefore, each of the Gods is revealed appropriately to the orders in which they manifest themselves; indeed, each proceeds down to the very last things through the generative power of the first things. The God always multiplies the progression, which moves from the one into the many. Yet, in this progression, the God preserves the identity of each series' principal and original cause, because of the likeness that the produced things bear to it.
