<meta>This page continues the sequence of Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*. Having previously discussed how the Gods possess knowledge and how they "proceed" into reality, this section moves to the hierarchy of the Gods (universality vs. particularity), their absolute simplicity, and how they are "participated" in by lower beings.</meta>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY BY PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>Proclus was known as "The Diadochus" or "The Successor," meaning the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

->### PROPOSITION 126.<-

**E**very God is more universal the closer he is to the One, and more particular the further he is from it.

->**DEMONSTRATION**<-

**F**or he who is the cause of more things is closer to that which produces all things <note>The "One," the ultimate source of all existence in Neoplatonic thought.</note>; while he who is the cause of fewer things is further from it. He who is the cause of more things is "more universal," and he who is the cause of fewer things is "more particular." Both are indeed unities <note>original Latin: *unitas*, referring to the divine nature as a single, indivisible entity</note>, but one is greater in power, while the other is lesser in power. These more particular gods are produced from the more universal ones, yet without the universal gods being divided (for they are unities) or changed (for they are immovable), and without being multiplied through any external relationship (for they are unmixed). Instead, they generate secondary progressions from themselves, which are diminished in power compared to the first, through their own abundance of power.

->### PROPOSITION 127.<-

**E**verything divine is primarily and supremely simple, and because of this, it is most self-sufficient.

->**DEMONSTRATION**<-

**T**hat it is simple is clear from its union, for everything divine is supremely one. That which is of such a nature is excellently simple. Furthermore, one may learn that it is most self-sufficient if one considers that anything composite is "needy"—even if it does not need things outside of itself, it nonetheless needs those parts from which it is composed. However, that which is most simple, uniform, and one, is identical to the Good; and in this Good, by establishing itself, it is most self-sufficient. Every divine being is of this nature. Therefore, it does not need others, being goodness itself, nor does it need those things from which it is made, since it is uniform <note>original Latin: *uniforme*, meaning it has a single, consistent form rather than being made of different parts.</note>.

->### PROPOSITION 128.<-

**E**very God is participated in <note>original Latin: *participatus*. In this philosophy, lower things "participate" in higher things by receiving a reflection or share of the higher being’s qualities.</note> immediately by those beings close to him, but is participated in by those further away through intermediaries, whether they be few or many.

[small decorative mark]
**DE-**
<note>The catchword "DE-" indicates the start of the next word on the following page: *DEMONSTRATIO*.</note>

<summary>Proclus establishes that the divine hierarchy is organized by "power" and "universality" relative to the One, and argues that the Gods must be absolutely simple and self-sufficient.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, divine hierarchy, simplicity, self-sufficiency, participation, causality</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*. Having previously discussed the self-sufficiency of the divine and the nature of the "One," this section concludes a demonstration on the nature of "unified plurality" before moving to Proposition 129, which explains the hierarchy of how divinity descends from the ultimate Unity through the Intellect and the Soul down to the physical body.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 35

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or those things which are of a single form <note>original Latin: *vniformia*, referring to things that share the simple, unified nature of the source.</note> through their kinship are also able to participate in the divine unities from that source. However, those things which—through their lessening and their stretching out into a multitude—lack other things, require those that are more unified. They participate in those things which are unities in themselves, rather than things that have merely been made one. For between Unity-in-itself and a divided multitude, there exists a "unified multitude." This is something capable of growing together into unity through a process of union, yet it remains kin to the divided multitude because of its emphasis on plurality.

->### PROPOSITION 129.<-

**E**very divine body is divine through a divine soul. Every divine soul is divine through a divine intellect. Every divine intellect is divine through the participation of a divine unity. And while Unity itself is God, the intellect is the most divine thing, the soul is divine, and the body is god-like in form <note>original Latin: *diuiniforme*, meaning it reflects the shape or character of the divine without being a god itself.</note>.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**I**f the whole number of the Gods is above the level of the intellect, and participation is achieved through things that are kin and similar, then an indivisible essence <note>In this system, "Intellect" or "Mind" is the first level of being below the Gods.</note> will participate first in the super-essential unities. Second, that which is joined to the world of "becoming" <note>original Latin: *generationi*, referring to the physical world of birth, change, and decay.</note> will participate. Third, the state of "becoming" itself, and each individual thing through that which is placed immediately above it. Thus, the property of the Gods proceeds even to the very last of participants, yet it does so through these intermediate steps that are kin to one another. For Unity first gives its extraordinary divine power to the intellect, and makes that intellect similar to itself according to its "one-form plurality." Through the intellect, this power is also present to the soul, drawing the soul toward the intellect and setting it aflame <note>original Latin: *coinflammans*, a metaphor for the soul being "kindled" or inspired by the divine light of the intellect.</note>. If this intellect is capable of being participated in, then through the soul, it bestows its ultimate property even upon the body. If a body participates in such a soul, it becomes not only animated and intellectual, but also divine; receiving life and motion from the soul, but an unbreakable permanence from the intellect. Union, indeed...

<summary>This page explains the hierarchy of divine participation, describing how divinity flows from the "One" (Unity) through the Intellect and the Soul to the physical Body. It establishes that a body only becomes "divine-form" by being connected to this descending chain of higher principles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology." The previous page (Proposition 129) established that divinity flows from Unity through the Intellect and Soul down to the physical body. This page explores the specific mechanics of this hierarchy: Proposition 130 discusses the degree of dependence between different levels of existence, while Proposition 131 introduces the concept of divine "super-abundance," where a God’s action begins within himself before overflowing to lower beings.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

...the divine essence from a participated unity. For each thing communicates its own essence to those things that follow it.

### PROPOSITION 130.

**I**n every divine order, the first members depend more upon those things which are ordered immediately above them than they [the superior things] depend on those that follow. Furthermore, the second members depend more on those placed immediately above them than the things that follow after them depend on those second members.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or the more uniform <note>original Latin: *uniformius*, describing an entity that is simpler, more integrated, and closer to the nature of the "One."</note> and more universal something is, the greater the superiority it holds over the things that follow it. Conversely, the more a thing is diminished in its power, the more closely it is tied <note>original Latin: *connatum*, literally "born together" or kindred in nature.</note> to the things that follow it. The higher things are more closely united to the causes of their own being, while the lower things are less so. For it is a sign of greater power to be more distinct from those things that are weaker <note>original Latin: *remissioribus*, referring to things further down the chain of being with "slackened" or diminished intensity.</note>, and to be more united with those that are superior. Just as, conversely, to move away from the superior and to be more in sympathy with the inferior is a sign of diminished power. This is indeed what happens to the secondary members in every order, but not to the first members.

### PROPOSITION 131.

**E**very God begins his own characteristic action from himself.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or a God shows the characteristic of his presence to secondary things first within himself, because he communicates himself to others out of his super-abundance <note>original Latin: *superplenitudinem*. In Neoplatonic thought, the divine does not give because it lacks something, but because it is so "more-than-full" that it naturally overflows.</note>. For "deficiency" is not a characteristic of the Gods, nor is "fullness" alone. For anything that is deficient is imperfect, and it is impossible for something to make another thing perfect when it is not perfect itself. However, that which is merely "full" is only self-sufficient, and is not yet ready to share or communicate. Therefore, that which fills other things and extends its gifts into others must be "super-full." If, then, the divine fills all things with goods by its very existence, those things which are within the divine are each super-full. If this is true, the God establishes his own characteristic first within himself...

<summary>Proclus argues that higher divine beings are more closely linked to their superior causes than to their subordinates, and explains that divine action is an overflow of "super-abundance" that begins within the deity itself.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, hierarchy of being, divine order, super-abundance, causality, participation, Elements of Theology</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression from the previous page, where it was established that every divine being is self-sufficient and possesses its own characteristic before sharing it. Proposition 132 deals with how these divine orders are linked through intermediaries to ensure a smooth transition from the highest to the lowest, while Proposition 133 defines the essential nature of a "God" as a "Unity" or "Goodness."</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 36

...possesses the property which he bestows upon others. In this way, he indeed extends to them the communications of his overflowing goodness. <note>original Latin: *superplenę bonitatis*, describing the Neoplatonic concept of "emanation," where reality overflows from the source like water from a fountain without diminishing the source itself.</note>

### PROPOSITION 132.
**A**ll orders of the Gods are bound together by intermediaries.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or as all the processions <term>processions: the way in which lower levels of reality flow out from higher levels</term> of beings are made to be very similar to one another, so much more certainly do the orders of the Gods possess an unbreakable continuity. This is because they exist in a unified way and are defined according to the one primary cause of themselves. Therefore, these transitions <note>original Latin: *remisfiones*, literally a "slackening" or "lowering of tension" as one moves down the chain of being.</note> occur in a unified manner, and only through that similarity of the second things to the first which exists among beings. Indeed, the essence of the Gods is established in union far more than the essence of ordinary beings. 

Therefore, all the divine kinds are bound together by their own intermediaries <term>intermediaries: "middle" entities that bridge the gap between two very different levels of reality</term>. The first things do not proceed immediately into entirely different levels of progression; rather, they proceed through kinds that are common to both—those from which they proceed and those of which they are the immediate causes. For these intermediaries lead the extremes into a single union; they are naturally linked to those below them, while they depend closely on those above them, and thus they preserve the well-ordered generation of divine things.

### PROPOSITION 133.
**E**very God is a beneficent unity, or a unifying goodness. And they possess this essence insofar as each one is a God. But the "Most Primary" <note>The "First" or the "One," which sits at the very top of Proclus's hierarchy.</note> is simply "the Good" itself and simply "the One." However, each of those who come after the First is a *certain* goodness and a *certain* unity.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or the divine property has distinguished the unities and the goodnesses of the Gods. Thus, each one, according to a specific character <note>original Latin: *idioma*, meaning a distinctive property or specific nature.</note> of goodness, makes all things good—such as by perfecting, or containing, or guarding them. For each of these actions is a certain "good," but it is not "all good." The First Principle, however, has established itself as the unified cause. Wherefore, that "Good itself" is the constituent of every goodness. For neither are all the Gods...

<summary>Proclus explains that the divine hierarchy is connected through bridges (intermediaries) to maintain continuity, and defines every individual God as a specific type of "Goodness" or "Unity" flowing from the ultimate Source.</summary>
<keywords>divine orders, intermediaries, processions, the One, the Good, Neoplatonic hierarchy, emanation, divine essence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Proclus's systematic exploration of the divine hierarchy. Having previously established that the Gods are "Unities" or "Henads" that exist above the level of being, he now moves to Proposition 134 and 135 to distinguish the functions of a divine Intellect from its nature as a God, and to explain how beings participate in these divine Unities.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

->The essences of the Gods are all at once equaled to the One, so great is the pre-eminence that the One has obtained over the multitude of the Gods. <note>original Latin: *Deorum essentiæ simul adæquantur uni...* Proclus is arguing that while there are many Gods, they are all unified by their proximity to "The One," the ultimate source of reality in Neoplatonic thought.</note><-

### PROPOSITION 134.

**E**very divine intellect understands as an intellect, but exercises providence as a God. <term>providence: (Greek: *pronoia*) the caring and ordering activity of a higher being toward lower ones.</term>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or it is the specific nature of an intellect to know existing things and to reach perfection through the act of thinking. However, it is the characteristic of a God to exercise providence and to fill all things with goodness. This communication and fulfillment occurs through union—specifically, the union of those who fill with those things that exist before them. <note>original Latin: *vunionem... implentium ad ea quæ ante ipsa sunt*. This refers to the flow of power from the higher "filler" to the lower "filled" being.</note> 

Even the intellect, by imitating this process, achieves the same state as the objects of its thought. Therefore, insofar as it provides for others, it acts as a God; this is because providence exists even before the activity of the intellect begins. For this reason, it communicates itself to all things as a God, but it is not present to all things as a mind. Divinity reaches even those things to which intellectual properties do not extend. Indeed, even those things that do not possess the power of thought still desire to be provided for and to participate in some form of the Good. This is because not all things desire intellect—nor are all things even capable of participating in it—but all things desire the Good and hasten to acquire it. <note>Proclus explains that "Godliness" (the Good) is more fundamental than "Intellect" because everything wants to be "good" (to exist and flourish), but not everything is capable of "thinking."</note>

### PROPOSITION 135.

**E**very divine unity is participated in immediately by some one of the beings; and everything that is deified is directed toward one divine unity; and there are as many kinds of participating beings as there are participating unities.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or it is not possible for two or more unities to be participated in by a single being. In what way, if the properties within those unities were distinct, would the nature of the being joined to them not also be confused? Connection between levels of reality occurs through similarity. Furthermore, a single unity cannot be participated in by many different things in a divided way. For "the many" are disconnected from unity—both as beings compared to that which exists before being, and as many... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the argument that there is a precise, structured relationship between each divine "Unit" (Henad) and the level of reality that follows it.</note>

<meta>This page concludes the argument of Proposition 135 regarding how beings relate to the "Unities" (the Gods) and proceeds to Propositions 136 and 137, which describe the hierarchical structure of these divine Unities and how they bring beings into existence.</meta>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY 37

...to unity. It is necessary, however, that the participant be in some respects similar to the participated, but in other respects different and dissimilar. <note>original Latin: *Oportet autem, participans aliquatenus simile esse participato...* Proclus is explaining a fundamental law of Neoplatonism: for one thing to be affected by another, they must share some common ground, yet remain distinct.</note> Since the participant is an existing thing, while the Unity is **beyond-essential**, they are dissimilar in this regard. Therefore, the participant must also be "one" so that, in this respect, it may be similar to the Unity it participates in. Furthermore, one is "one" as a Unity itself, while the other is "one" as a recipient—it is "unified" through its participation in that higher Unity.

### PROPOSITION 136.

**E**very God who is more universal and ranked closer to the First Principle is participated in by a more universal class of beings. Conversely, a God who is more particular and more distant is participated in by a more particular class. As being is related to being, so is a Unity related to a divine Unity. <term>First Principle: The "One," the absolute source from which all reality flows.</term>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**I**f there are as many unities as there are beings, and vice versa—one unity being participated in by one being—it is quite clear that the order of beings follows the order of unities, modeled after the level that precedes it. More universal beings coalesce with more universal unities, while more particular beings join with more particular unities. If this were not the case, then dissimilar things would be joined to dissimilar things, and the distribution of power would not be according to merit or rank; however, these scenarios are impossible. <note>Proclus argues for a "Great Chain of Being" where every level matches perfectly with the one above it; chaos or "mismatched" layers would violate the rational order of the universe.</note> Indeed, in all other things, the "One" and its proper measure shine forth from that source and descend from it. Much more so, then, will there be an order of participation among the Unities themselves, arranging similar things according to the power of those things they resemble.

### PROPOSITION 137.

**E**very unity, together with the "One," establishes the being that participates in it.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or just as the "One" is the constituent of all things, so too is it the cause of the participated unities and of the beings that are drawn toward those unities. <note>original Latin: *entium in unitates intensorum*. This suggests a "stretching" or "tension" of a lower being as it reaches up toward its divine source.</note> That which depends upon each principle produces the unity that shines forth within it. Simply "to be" is the work of the "One," but "to be of a shared nature" is the work of the Unity... <note>Proclus is distinguishing between the raw fact of existence (given by the "One") and the specific, unified nature of a thing (given by its specific participating "Unity" or God).</note>

<summary>Proclus explains the hierarchical "alignment" between the Gods (Unities) and the beings that follow them, asserting that more powerful Gods govern broader classes of reality. He also distinguishes between existence itself and the unified nature of a being.</summary>
<keywords>Unities, Henads, participation, hierarchy of being, The One, divine order, providence, Neoplatonism</keywords>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

...to which it is connatural; it is this, therefore, which through itself defines the being that participates in it, and manifests its **super-essential** property essentially. <term>Super-essential: (Greek: hyperousios) That which exists beyond or above the level of "essence" or "substance," specifically the nature of the Gods/Henads.</term> For because it exists in a primary way, it follows everywhere in a secondary way that this thing is what it is. If, then, there is any super-essential property of the Deity, it belongs also to the being that participates in it essentially.

### PROPOSITION 138.

**O**f all those things that participate in the divine property and have been **Deified**, the very first and highest is Being itself. <term>Deified: Things that have been made god-like through their direct participation in the divine Unities.</term>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**I**f Being is above intellect and above life, as has been demonstrated—for if Being is the cause of the greatest number of things after the One, it must be the highest. For it is more uniform than these others, and for that reason altogether more venerable. <note>In Neoplatonic thought, the more "universal" a cause is, the higher it sits in the hierarchy. Being is more universal than Life or Intellect because everything that lives or thinks "is," but not everything that "is" lives or thinks.</note> Nothing else exists before it except the One. For what else is before a uniform multitude but the One? Now, a uniform multitude is Being itself, existing from the **Limit** and the **Infinite**, and Being is altogether a super-essential "being" before essence. <note>original Latin: *...ex termino existens, & ieterminato...* Proclus is referring to the "Limit" (*peras*) and the "Infinite/Indeterminate" (*apeiron*), the two principles that rank immediately below the One and combine to produce Being.</note> Since also, in the secondary rank of illustrations, the One alone reaches beyond Being. But Being follows immediately after the One. For that which is Being in potentiality, yet not yet Being in actuality, is "one" according to its nature; and that which follows this is already Being in actuality. Therefore, among the principles of Being, that which is immediately above Being is "non-being"—not as something inferior, but as something more excellent than Being—and that is the One.

### PROPOSITION 139.

**A**ll things that participate in the divine unities, beginning from Being, end in corporeal nature.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or the first of the participants is Being, and the last is the body. For we even say that there are "divine bodies." <note>Proclus refers here to the celestial bodies (stars and planets) which were considered divine living beings in ancient cosmology.</note> For the summits of all genera are dedicated to the Gods—whether they be the summits of bodies, of souls, or of intellects—so that in every order, there are things analogous to the Gods, which contain and preserve the secondary things. Thus, every whole number becomes a "whole in the part," having all things within itself, and before all other things, it possesses the divine property. Therefore, the divine exists even **corporeally**,

<summary>Proclus argues that Being is the primary participant of the Gods, sitting between the "One" and the rest of creation, and explains that the divine influence extends through a hierarchy all the way down to physical bodies.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Being, The One, Henads, Super-essential, Limit, Infinite, Divine Bodies, Elements of Theology</keywords>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 38

...essentially, psychically, and intellectually, the divine genus. It is clear that all these things are divine by participation. For the divine, in the primary sense, consists in the **Unities**. <term>Unities: Also called "Henads" (from the Greek *henas*), these are the highest principles of reality, identified by Proclus as the Gods themselves, existing just below the Absolute One.</term> Therefore, those things which participate in the divine Unities begin from Being and end with the incorporeal nature. <note>Proclus is arguing that the "divine" quality flows down from the Gods into Being, Life, and Intellect, stopping just before it reaches physical, corporeal matter.</note>

### PROPOSITION 140.

**A**ll the powers of the divine, beginning from above and proceeding through their own intermediaries, reach as far as the lowest things and the regions around the earth.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or nothing hinders them or forbids their presence in all things. They lack nothing in terms of locations or distances because of their unsurpassable excellence over all things and their unmixed presence everywhere. Nor is that which is fit to participate in them prohibited from doing so; rather, as soon as something becomes ready for participation, they are present—neither arriving then, nor having been absent before, but always remaining in the same state. 

If, therefore, any of the things around the earth is fit to participate, they are present to it also, and fill all things with themselves. They are present to higher things more fully, to middle things according to their order, and to the lowest things last. Thus they extend themselves from above to the very end, which is why the images of the first things exist even in these lowest realms, and all things "sympathize" with all others. <note>original Latin: *compatiuntur omnia omnibus*. In Neoplatonism, "sympathy" is a cosmic law where parts of the universe respond to one another because they are all parts of a single living whole.</note> In the first things, the secondary things pre-exist; while in the secondary things, the first things appear. For everything exists in three ways: either by **cause**, or by **existence**, or by **participation**. <note>This is a fundamental Procline rule: a thing exists as a seed in its cause, as itself in its own level, and as a reflected image in the things below it.</note>

### PROPOSITION 141.

**E**very providence of the Gods is of two kinds: one is distinct from the things for which it provides, and the other is coordinated with them.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or one kind of providence, according to its existence and the property of its order, is entirely spread above the things it illuminates; while the other, being of the same...

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

...of the same order and reason, they provide for those subjects of the same arrangement; and they imitate the providential action of the separate Gods, and being themselves secondary, they desire to fill [all things] with such goods as they are able. <note>Proclus refers to the "secondary gods" or divine souls who act as intermediaries between the absolute Gods and the physical world.</note>

### PROPOSITION 142.

**A**ll the Gods are present to all things in the same way, but not all things are present to the Gods in the same way. Rather, each thing participates in their presence according to its own rank and power—some uniformly, others manifoldly; some eternally, others according to time; some incorporeally, and others corporally.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**I**t is necessary that any difference in the participation of the same things must arise either from the participant or from the thing participated in. But every divine thing always holds the same rank, and is detached from all relative connections, and is unmixed. <note>In Neoplatonism, the "unmixed" (*amiktos*) nature of a God means they remain pure and unaffected by the lower things they create or sustain.</note> Therefore, it must be that the variation arises from the participant; it is the participant that does not exist in the same way [as the divine], but exists now in one way, now in another, and is present to the Gods differently. Thus, while the Gods are present to all things in the same way, all things are not present to them in the same way. Rather, as each is able, so are they present, and as they are, so they enjoy them. For the participation of these things is according to the measure of their capacity for that presence.

### PROPOSITION 143.

**A**ll inferior things fall away from the presence of the Gods, even if the participant is suitable. Everything alien to the divine light departs, though all things are suddenly illuminated by the Gods.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or the divine always comprehends more and is more powerful than those things which proceed from it; the lack of divine light is caused by the unsuitability of the participants. For the participant even weakens that light by its own infirmity. When the participant is thus weakened, some other dominion seems to take over—not because of a change in the divine power, but according to the impotence of the participant, so that it seems to depart from the illumination of the divine form. <note>original: "divinae formae" - In this context, "form" refers to the specific essence or character a God imparts to a thing to make it what it is.</note>

<summary>This page discusses how the Gods remain constant and unmixed while the material world experiences their presence differently based on its own capacity and "infirmity." It explains that distance from the divine is an illusion caused by the participant's weakness rather than the Gods' withdrawal.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, Divine Presence, Participation, Neoplatonism, Providence, Divine Light, Infirmity of Matter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 38, following the discussion in Proposition 142 regarding the relationship between the Gods and the various levels of being that participate in their presence. This page contains Propositions 144 and 145 of Proclus's *Elements of Theology*.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 39

### PROPOSITION 144.
**A**ll beings, and all the arrangements of beings, have proceeded as far as the ordinations of the Gods. <note>Proclus is arguing that the physical and metaphysical world's structure is a direct mirror of the divine hierarchy; nothing exists that is not reached by divine order.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or the Gods produced beings along with themselves, nor could anything subsist, or receive its measure and order, outside of the Gods. For all things are perfected according to their power, and are ordered and measured by the Gods. Therefore, even before the lowest kinds of beings, there pre-exist the Gods who arrange them, granting to these also life, formation, and perfection, and even converting these back toward the Good itself. <note>In Neoplatonism, "conversion" (*epistrophe*) is the process by which a created thing turns back toward its divine source to find its purpose.</note> Similarly, they exist before the middle and before the first ranks. All things are bound to and rooted in the Gods, and are preserved for this reason. But if anything were to withdraw from the Gods and become solitary, it would depart entirely into non-being and perish, being completely deprived of the divine influences that held it together.

### PROPOSITION 145.
**E**very property of a divine order permeates through all secondary things and gives itself to all inferior kinds. <note>This explains the "divine chains" (*seirai*) where a specific quality—like "purity" or "life"—starts in a God and echoes down through every level of reality, from angels down to rocks.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or if beings proceed as far as the arrangements of the Gods have proceeded, then in each kind of being there is the property of the divine powers illuminated from above. For each thing carries from its own immediate cause a property according to which that cause obtained its substance. I say, for example, if there is a **purifying Deity**, then there is also a purification found in souls, in animals, in plants, and even in stones. Similarly, if there is a **guarding** power, or a **converting** power, or a **perfecting** or **vivifying** power, they all descend through the ranks.

A stone, indeed, participates in the purifying power only in a bodily way; a plant participates more clearly according to the nature of life; an animal possesses this form according to its capacity for motion; a rational soul possesses it rationally; and an intellect possesses it intellectually. However, the Gods possess it **super-essentially** <note>original: "superessentialiter" — meaning "above the level of being or substance." The Gods are the source of the property, not just participants in it.</note> and uniformly. Thus, the entire series possesses the same power.

L 2

<summary>Proclus argues that the reach of divine order is co-extensive with existence itself and that specific divine attributes (like purification or life) echo down through every level of reality, from the Gods to inanimate rocks.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, divine chains, conversion, purification, hierarchy of being, participation, super-essential</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 39, completing the demonstration of Proposition 145 and proceeding through Propositions 146, 147, and the beginning of 148. The text remains centered on Proclus’s systematic Neoplatonic theology, specifically how divine orders are structured and linked.</meta>

# OF PROCLUS DIADOCHUS

...essence from a single divine cause. And the same logic applies to the rest. For all things depend upon the Gods. Some are illuminated by some Gods, and others by others, and these series descend all the way to the lowest of things. Some are connected to the Gods immediately, while others are joined through more or fewer intermediaries. But all things are full of Gods <note>original: "Plena autem omnia sunt Dijs." This is a famous ancient maxim, often attributed to Thales, which Proclus uses here to argue that divine influence permeates every level of physical reality.</note>, and whatever each individual thing possesses according to its nature, it receives from that source.

### PROPOSITION 146.
**T**he ends of all divine progressions are made like unto their own principles, maintaining a circle without beginning or end through a conversion back to those principles. <note>In Neoplatonism, reality is a cycle: Procession (*proodos*) is the flow away from the divine source, and Conversion (*epistrophe*) is the return to it. This creates a "divine circle."</note>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or even if every individual thing that has proceeded turns back toward the specific principle from which it originated, it is surely much more true that entire orders, having proceeded from their summit, turn back toward that same summit. The conversion of the end back into the beginning makes the whole into a single, finished unity, inclined toward itself and displaying a uniformity within its multitude through this mutual inclination.

### PROPOSITION 147.
**T**he summits of all divine orders are made like the ends of the orders that are above them.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or if it is necessary for the divine progression to be continuous, and for each individual order to be bound together by its own middle terms, it is necessary for the summits of the second-rank orders to be connected to the conclusions of the first-rank orders. This union occurs through likeness. Therefore, there must be a likeness between the principles of a lower order and the ends of the order placed above it. <note>Proclus is describing a "telescoping" effect where the highest point of a lower world (like the world of Souls) is nearly identical to the lowest point of the world above it (the world of Intellects), ensuring there are no gaps in reality.</note>

### PROPOSITION 148.
**E**very divine order is united to itself in a threefold way: by its own summit, by its middle, and by its end.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or that summit, possessing a most unifying—if I may say so—power <note>original: "vnicissimam... potentiam." Proclus uses a superlative form of "unique" to describe the intense power of the "One" as it manifests at the top of a hierarchy.</note>, transmits that union into the whole order and unites every part...

<summary>Proclus explains the "divine circle" where all things return to their source, and describes how different levels of reality are seamlessly linked by ensuring the top of one level resembles the bottom of the level above it.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, divine progression, conversion, divine circle, hierarchy of being, unity, theological principles</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 39, which was discussing the continuity and structure of divine orders (Proposition 148). The text concludes the proof that divine orders are linked through their summits and ends, then moves to Propositions 149 and 150 regarding the number and power of the Gods.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 40

...remaining in itself from above. But the middle, by reaching to each of the extremes, connects the whole around itself: conveying the gifts of the first things, while extending the powers of the last things, and instilling in all a communion and a mutual connection. For in this way, a single coordination <term>coordination: translated from the Latin "coordinatio," referring to the "taxis" or systematic arrangement of divine beings</term> arises from those who fulfill and those who are fulfilled, as if inclining toward a certain center in the middle. The end, moreover, turns back again into the beginning, and by leading back the proceeding powers, it provides a likeness and an inclination toward the whole order. And so the whole order together belongs to the One, because of the unifying power of the first things, because of the containment that is in the middle, and because of the conversion of the end of the progressions back to the beginning. <note>original: "conuersionem finis progressionum, in principium." This refers to "epistrophe," the Neoplatonic concept where all things strive to return to their divine source, completing a cosmic circle.</note>

### PROPOSITION 149.

**E**very multitude of divine unities is bounded by number. <note>In Proclus's system, these unities are the "Henads"—the highest Gods existing just below the Absolute One. He argues here that there is not an infinite number of Gods; they are a definite, finite group.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or if it is near to the One, it is not infinite; for the infinite is not innate to the One, but foreign to it. For if multitude by itself departs from the One, it is clear that an absolutely infinite multitude is entirely deserted by the One. Wherefore it is also powerless and ineffective. Therefore, the multitude of the Gods is not infinite. It is therefore uniform and bounded, and more bounded than every other multitude. For it is more akin to the One than any other multitude. If, therefore, the multitude is the principle <note>original: "principium," referring to the starting point of a series</note>, that which is closer to the principle ought to be more like the principle than that which is further away. For that which is closer is more similar. But since the first itself is the One, the multitude touching it is "less" of a multitude than that which is further away. But the infinite is not "less" of a multitude, but rather the greatest possible multitude.

### PROPOSITION 150.

**E**verything that proceeds in the divine orders is not by nature capable of receiving all the powers of its producer, nor, in general, are secondary things capable of all the powers of those things that come before them. But those [higher things] possess certain powers that are set apart from inferior things and are incomprehensible to those things that come after them. <note>This principle explains the hierarchy of reality: as divinity descends, the "vessels" (lower beings) cannot contain the full intensity or variety of the "light" (higher powers) from above.</note>

->DEMON-<-

<summary>This page concludes Proposition 148 on the circularity of divine orders and presents Proposition 149, arguing that the number of Gods (Henads) must be finite and bounded because they are closest to the One. Proposition 150 begins to explain why lower divine beings cannot fully grasp the powers of their superiors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus's "Elements of Theology." The previous page (40) concluded Proposition 149, which argued that the number of Gods is finite. PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR 113
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens, succeeding in the line of Plato.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or if the properties of the gods differ, the properties of the diminished <note>original: "diminutorum," referring to lower-ranking divine beings who possess less power than those above them.</note> beings certainly pre-exist in the higher ones, but the properties of the higher are more universal and are not found in the diminished. Yet the more excellent beings bestow some qualities upon those produced by them, while other qualities they have pre-occupied in themselves in an eminent way. <note>original: "eminenter." In Neoplatonic thought, a cause possesses the qualities of its effect in a "higher" or "more perfect" way than the effect itself does.</note> For it has been demonstrated that those things closer to the One are more universal, while those further away are more particular. But if the more universal beings possess powers that are more comprehensive than the particular ones, then those who occupy the second and more particular rank will not comprehend <note>In this context, "comprehend" means to fully contain or match the capacity of the higher power.</note> the power of the higher. There is, therefore, in the higher beings something incomprehensible and uncircumscribable to those who are subordinate. For indeed, each of the divine beings is truly infinite; it has been shown to be an entity not merely of itself, nor much less of those above it, but to all those things that come after it. Moreover, infinity according to power exists in them. But the infinite is incomprehensible to those for whom it is infinite. Therefore, the lower beings do not participate in all the powers which the more excellent beings have pre-occupied in themselves. For those powers were incomprehensible to the secondary beings—just as, indeed, the secondary beings are to themselves. <note>Proclus argues that a lower being cannot fully "grasp" the vast power of the being that created it, because the higher power is "infinite" relative to the lower capacity.</note> Therefore, the lower do not possess all the powers of the higher because they are more particular, nor do they possess the powers they *do* have in the same manner as the higher, because of the infinity which causes the higher to soar above the inferior.

### PROPOSITION 151.

**E**very paternal element in the Gods is of the nature of a first principle, <note>original: "primificum," meaning it acts as a primary cause or origin.</note> and exists beforehand in the order of the Good, extending through all divine arrangements.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or the paternal element itself produces the essences, the powers, and the entire substances of the secondary beings according to a single ineffable eminence. For this reason, it is named "paternal," as it displays the united and good-form power of the One, acting as the procreative cause of secondary things. Within every order of the Gods, the paternal genus acts as the leader, producing all things from itself and providing them with order. This is because it is arranged as an analogue to the Good itself. <note>Just as "The Good" (the Absolute One) produces all reality, the "Father" within any specific level of the divine hierarchy acts as the source and creator for that specific level.</note> And among these fathers, some are certainly more universal, and others more particular, just as the orders of the Gods themselves are more universal and particular...

<summary>Proclus explains that higher divine beings possess infinite power that lower beings cannot fully contain, and defines the "paternal" role among the gods as the primary, creative source of order and essence.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, hierarchy, paternal cause, divine power, universal vs particular, the Good, eminence</keywords>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 41

...differ from the former, according to the principle of the cause. Therefore, as many as there are universal progressions of the Gods, there are just as many differences among the "fathers." <note>In Neoplatonic theology, a "Father" refers to a primary, stabilizing cause within a divine rank that acts as a representative of the "One" or the "Good" for that level.</note> For if in every order there is something analogous to the Good itself, it is necessary for the paternal nature to exist in all orders, and for all individual things to proceed from a paternal union.

### PROPOSITION CLII.
**E**very generative nature of the Gods proceeds according to the infinity of divine power, multiplying itself, permeating through all things, and excellently demonstrating its inexhaustibility in the progressions of secondary beings.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or to multiply those things which proceed, and to lead them forth from their occult concealment within their causes into emerging offshoots—to whose else is this characteristic, if not to the infinite power of the Gods? It is through this power that all divine things are filled with fertile goods. Therefore, let the deity produce other things from itself through its overflowing power. <note>The concept of "overflowing" (superplenam) is central to Neoplatonism: the higher reality is so full of being and power that it naturally "pours over" to create the lower levels of reality.</note> It is, therefore, a property of the generative Deity to have dominion over power, multiplying the powers of those things generated, making them fertile, and arousing them to generate and produce other things. 

For if individual beings first communicate the specific property they possess to others, then surely everything fertile bestows a fertile progression upon those things that come after it. This models that primordial infinity which belongs to the universe, from which every generative power has proceeded, excellently gushing forth the perennial progressions of the divine.

### PROPOSITION CLIII.
**E**verything perfect among the Gods is the cause of divine perfection.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or just as some substances belong to beings and others to super-essential <term>super-essential: referring to the Gods or Henads who exist "above being" (hyperousios) and are the source of all existence.</term> entities, so surely some perfections belong to the Gods themselves according to their essence, while other perfections belong to the beings that are secondary to them. The former perfections are perfect through themselves and are the primary makers <note>original Latin: "primopifices," translating the Greek "protopourgoi," referring to the highest creative principles.</note>, because the Good is also in them primarily; however, the latter possess their perfection only through participation. Therefore, the perfection of the Gods is one thing, and the perfection of those things which are deified <note>Those entities, like souls or intellects, that are not gods by nature but are made "god-like" by their connection to the divine.</note> is another. That which is...

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "Proclus Diadochus." Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, centuries after Plato.</note>

In the Gods, the perfected state is primary; it is the cause of perfection not only for those beings who have been "deified" <note>This refers to lower souls or beings that have attained a god-like state by participation.</note> but also for the Gods themselves. For if every being, insofar as it is perfected, is "converted" <note>In Neoplatonism, "conversion" or "reversion" (epistrophe) is the process by which a created thing turns back toward its source to find its completion or "perfection."</note> toward its own principle, then the perfection-granting class of the Gods is the cause of all divine return.

### PROPOSITION CLIV.

**E**very guardian property in the Gods preserves each thing in its proper rank, uniformly separated from secondary things and established in those that are primary.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or if the guardian nature preserves the measure of each order immutably, and keeps all preserved things within their own perfection, it bestows upon all things an eminence over what is inferior. It establishes each thing permanently within itself, remaining unmixed; and by being the cause of "immaculate purity" <note>original: "puritatis immaculatę." In this context, purity means remaining untainted by the limitations or "grossness" of the lower, material worlds.</note> for those things it guards, it anchors them in the superior realms. For everything that is immediately close to the primary causes is "perfect"; it remains within itself, yet extends its power over those things that are inferior.

### PROPOSITION CLV.

**E**verything that is life-giving in the divine ranks is a generative cause; however, not every generative order is life-giving.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or the "generative" nature <note>The power that produces "being" or existence itself.</note> is more universal than the "life-giving" nature, and is closer to the First Principle. Generation indicates the cause that produces beings into a multitude. "Vivification" <note>The act of giving "Life" (zoe), which in the Neoplatonic hierarchy is a specific stage of development after "Being" but before "Intellect."</note> however, represents the Deity as the bestower of all life. Therefore, if the former multiplies the substances of beings, while the latter establishes the progressions of life, then just as "Being" relates to "Life," so the generative order will relate to the life-giving series. The generative will therefore be more universal, the cause of more things, and for that reason, closer to the Principle.

### PROPOSITION CLVI.

**E**very cause of purity is contained within the guardian order. However, the reverse is not true: the preserving nature is not identical to the purifying class.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or purity bestows upon all the Gods an unmixed state regarding inferior things, and keeps that which is purified focused on the providence of fertile things...

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 42

Guardianship <note>original: "Custodia." In Proclus’s system, this refers to the power that keeps every level of reality from collapsing into the one below it.</note> also achieves this: it contains all things within themselves and firmly establishes them upon the higher powers. Therefore, the guardian property is more universal than the purifying property. For it is the specific nature of guardianship to preserve the order of each thing, both in relation to itself and to those things that come before and after it. Purity, however, is to separate the superior from the inferior. These properties exist first in the Gods. For one cause must precede all others. Everything that exists in all things, and indeed all uniform measures of all goods, are pre-accepted by the Gods; there is no good in secondary beings that does not pre-exist in the Gods as a cause. Where else would it come from, and what cause would it have? In the Gods, therefore, purity is a primary good, as is guardianship, and every such property.

<note>A faint circular stamp, likely a library mark or ownership seal, is visible on the right side of the page.</note>

### PROPOSITION CLVII.

**E**very paternal cause is the giver of being to all things and establishes the essences <note>original: "essentias." The fundamental "whatness" or substance of a thing.</note> of beings. But every demiurgic cause <note>original: "opifex." This refers to the "Craftsman" or Demiurge who shapes the world, a concept central to Plato's *Timaeus*.</note> precedes the formation of composite things, their order, and their division according to number; it belongs to the same order as the paternal, but in more specific classes.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or both belong to the order of the "Limit" <note>original: "termini." In Neoplatonic thought, the Limit (Peras) and the Unlimited (Apeiron) are the two primary principles directly below the "One."</note>, because essence, number, and form are all characteristic of the Limit. Thus, these causes are linked to one another. But that which is demiurgic leads the creation into a multitude, while the paternal provides the uniform progression of beings. One is form-giving, the other is essence-giving. Therefore, just as form and being differ from each other, so the paternal and demiurgic causes differ. Form is a kind of unity. Thus, the paternal is more universal and causal than the demiurgic, standing above the demiurgic class just as being is more universal than form.

### PROPOSITION CLVIII.

**E**very elevating cause <note>original: "anagogica." From the Greek *anagoge*, meaning to lead or lift the soul upward to the divine.</note> among the Gods differs both from the purifying cause and from the classes that "convert" beings.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**T**hat this cause must also exist primarily among the Gods is clear, since all causes of universal goods pre-exist there. But the elevating cause pre-exists the purifying cause, because the latter separates from lower things, while the former joins to the superior—
                                        M <note>This "M" is a "catchword," a printer's tool used to help the binder put pages in the correct order; it is the first letter of the first word on the following page.</note>  superior beings.

<summary>Proclus distinguishes between the guardian and purifying roles of the Gods, then defines the paternal cause as the source of being and the demiurgic cause as the source of form and order.</summary>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens, succeeding in the line of Plato.</note>

...higher beings. But the **reverting** <note>original: "conuersivi." Reversion is the process by which a created thing turns back toward its source to achieve its own perfection.</note> property has a more specific order. For everything that reverts, reverts either toward itself or toward what is higher. The effect of the **elevating** <note>original: "anagogici"</note> power is defined by a reversion toward what is higher, leading the thing that is being reverted toward a superior and more divine state.

## PROPOSITION CLIX.

**E**very order of the Gods is derived from the primary principles: the **Limit** <note>original: "termino." In Proclus’s philosophy, the "Limit" provides form, boundary, and stability.</note> and the **Infinite** <note>original: "intermino." This represents the power of expansion, multiplication, and potential.</note>; but some incline more toward the cause of Limit, others toward the Infinite.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or every order proceeds from both, because the gifts of the primary causes permeate through all secondary things. But in some cases, Limit predominates in the mixture; in others, the Infinite. Thus, one genus is formed as **limit-like** in which Limit rules, and another as **infinite-like**, in which Infinitude <note>original: "interminitas"</note> rules.

## PROPOSITION CLX.
### CONCERNING THE INTELLECT.

**E**very divine **Intellect** <note>original: "intellectus." Not merely human reason, but a cosmic level of reality that perceives truth instantly and whole.</note> is uniform and perfect; it is an Intellect primarily through itself, and it produces other minds.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

**F**or if it is a God, it is full of divine **unities** <note>original: "vnitatum." These are the "Henads," the highest principles of individuality and godhood.</note> and is uniform. If this is so, it is also perfect, being full of divine goodness. If this is so, it is also an Intellect in a primary sense, as it is united to the Gods. For a deified intellect is superior to every other intellect. Being an Intellect in the primary sense, it also gives substance to others. For from the primary beings, all secondary beings have acquired their **essence** <note>original: "essentiam."</note>.

## PROPOSITION CLXI.

**E**very **truly existing being** <note>original: "enter ens." This is a Latin rendering of the Greek *ontōs on*, referring to the highest level of reality that does not change.</note>, which depends upon the Gods, is a divine **Intelligible** <note>original: "intelligibile." That which can be grasped by the Intellect.</note> and is **unparticipated** <note>original: "imparticipabile." This means it remains pure and independent in itself, rather than being "divided" or fully absorbed into the things that depend on it.</note>.

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 43
->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or since the first of those things participating in divine union is **true being** <note>original: "enter ens." In Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to "True Being" or "Being that truly is" (*ontōs on*), which resides at a higher level of reality than physical existence.</note>, as has been shown, and it fills the intellect from itself: for indeed the intellect is a being; as filled with being, it is divine indeed, and **intelligible** <note>original: "intelligibile." The "intelligible" refers to the object of thought—that which is purely knowable—which exists prior to the "intellectual" act of thinking.</note>. Insofar as it is deified, it is divine; but insofar as it fills the intellect and is participated by it, it is intelligible; and the intellect is indeed a being because of the **primary being** <note>original: "primo ens"</note>; but primary being itself is separate from the intellect, since the intellect comes after being. **Unparticipated** <note>original: "imparticipabilia." These are pure principles or "monads" that exist in their own right before they are shared or "participated in" by lower levels of reality.</note> things exist before participated things. Therefore, even beyond the being joined to the intellect, there pre-exists a being that is through itself and unparticipated; for it is intelligible, not as coordinated with the intellect, but as perfecting the intellect in a transcendent way, since it also communicates existence to it, and fills it with a truly existing essence. <margin><note>A circular stamp mark appears on the right margin of the original manuscript.</note></margin>

## PROPOSITION CLXII.

**E**very multitude of **henads** <note>original: "vnitatum." Proclus uses the term "henads" (unities) to describe the divine units that proceed directly from the One, acting as the sources of all properties in the universe.</note> illuminating true being is hidden and intelligible. Hidden indeed insofar as it is joined to the One; intelligible, however, insofar as it is participated by being.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or all the Gods are named from those things that depend on them, since it is possible to know their different substances, which are otherwise unknown to these participants. For everything divine is ineffable and unknown in itself, insofar as it is innate to the ineffable One. However, from the variation among the participants, it happens that their properties are known. Those who illuminate true being are surely intelligible. Because indeed, true being is intelligible, divine, and unparticipated, and it pre-exists the intellect. For this would not depend on the most primary Gods unless they also possessed a primary-making essence and a perfecting power over the other Gods. For just as the participants relate to one another, so also do the essences of the things participated.

## PROPOSITION CLXIII.

**E**very multitude of henads participated by the unparticipated **Mind** <note>original: "mente." Often used interchangeably with "Intellect" (*Nous*) in this context.</note> is intellectual.

# THE SUCCESSOR PROCLUS
->DEMONSTRATION.<-

Just as the **Intellect** <note>original: "intellectus." In Neoplatonism, the Intellect (Nous) is the realm of pure, eternal thought.</note> stands in relation to **True Being** <note>original: "ente ens." This refers to the highest level of reality that possesses existence.</note>, so the **Unities** <note>original: "vnitates." These are also known as "Henads," the first emanations from the Absolute One; they are the "Gods" of Proclus’s system.</note> themselves stand in relation to the **Intelligible Unities**. Therefore, those Unities that illuminate the divine and **unparticipated** <note>original: "imparticipabilem." A principle is unparticipated when it remains pure and transcendent, rather than being shared out among lower things.</note> intellect are themselves **intellectual**; however, they are not intellectual in the sense that they exist *within* an intellect. Instead, they exist prior to the intellect as its causes, and they are the generative source of the intellect itself.

### PROPOSITION 164.
Every multitude of Unities participated by the **unparticipated Soul** is **supermundane**. <note>original: "supermundana." Meaning "above the world" or "celestial," these gods exist outside the physical constraints of the cosmos.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
Since the unparticipated Soul is the primary supermundane entity, the Gods participated by it are also supermundane. They hold the same relationship to the intellectual and intelligible Gods that the Soul holds toward the Intellect, and the Intellect holds toward True Being. Therefore, just as every soul is directed toward the Intellect, and the Intellect is turned back toward the Intelligible, so too do the supermundane Gods excel the intellectual Gods, just as the intellectual Gods excel the intelligible ones.

### PROPOSITION 165.
Every multitude of Unities participated by any **sensible body** <note>original: "sensibili corpore." A body that can be perceived by the five senses; i.e., the physical planets or the material world.</note> is **mundane**. <note>original: "mundana." Meaning "within the world" or "worldly." These are the gods or divine powers that inhabit and govern the physical universe.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
For the divine illuminates the parts of the world through the mediation of the Intellect and the Soul. Indeed, the Intellect is not present to any mundane body without the Soul, nor is the **Deity** <note>original: "Deitas."</note> joined immediately even to the Soul. Participations occur through similar things. The Intellect itself, according to its highest and most intelligible part, is a participant in Unity. Therefore, the mundane Unities are those that complete the whole world and act as the "deifiers" of visible bodies. For even each of these bodies is divine, not because of the soul—for the soul is not primarily God—nor even through the intellect, for the intellect is not identical to the One. Rather, the body is **animated** <note>original: "animatum." Literally "given a soul" or "made alive."</note> and self-moving through the soul. But always in the same... <note>The text ends mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>Proclus distinguishes between "supermundane" gods, who associate with the transcendent Soul, and "mundane" gods, who inhabit and give divinity to physical bodies through the hierarchy of Intellect and Soul.</summary>

ELEMENTA THEOLOGICA. 44

...maintaining itself in the same manner, and is carried along in the most perfect order through the **intellect**; whereas that which is divine acts through **union** <note>original: "unionem." And if it possesses a **providential power** <note>original: "potentiam... prouidentem." The capacity of higher beings to care for and govern lower levels of reality.</note>, it is of such a nature for this very reason.

### PROPOSITION 166.

Every intellect is either **unparticipated** <note>original: "imparticipabilis." A primary source that remains pure and distinct from the things it influences.</note> or **participated**. And if it is participated, it is participated either by **supermundane souls** <note>original: "supermundanis animabus." Souls that exist above the physical cosmos.</note> or by **mundane souls**.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For an unparticipated intellect presides over every multitude of intellects, possessing the most primary existence. Among the participated intellects, some illuminate the supermundane and unparticipated soul, while others illuminate the mundane soul. For the mundane multitude does not proceed immediately from the unparticipated, since **progressions** <note>original: "progres-siones." The "emanations" or steps by which reality unfolds from the One down to matter.</note> occur through similar things. Indeed, that which is separated from the world is more similar to the unparticipated than that which is distributed throughout the world. Nor does only a supermundane multitude exist, but there are also **mundane intellects**. Since there is also a mundane multitude of Gods, and the world itself is both ensouled and endowed with mind, the participation of mundane souls in the supermundane gods occurs through the mediation of mundane intellects.

### PROPOSITION 167.

Every intellect knows itself. But the most primary intellect knows itself alone; and being numerically one, in this respect it is both the **intellect and the intelligible**. <note>original: "intellectus & intelligibile." In this state, the "Thinker" and the "Object of Thought" are the exact same thing.</note> Each of those following, however, knows itself and at the same time those things prior to it; and to such an intellect, the "intelligible" is partly that which it is itself, and partly that from which it originates.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For every intellect knows either itself, or that which is above it, or that which is after it. But if it should know that which is after it, it would turn toward the inferior; although it is an intellect, it would not truly know that to which it has turned, as it would not be within itself, but outside itself. It would only possess an **image** <note>original: "typum." A reflection or secondary impression of a thing rather than its essence.</note> of the object, formed within itself by that object. For it knows what it possesses and what it experiences, and not that which it does not possess and from which it does not experience anything. If, however, it knows that which is above it through its own self-knowledge, it knows itself and that thing simultaneously... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence: "cogno-" likely for "cognoscet" (will know).</note>

<summary>Proclus distinguishes between transcendent and worldly intellects, and argues that every intellect possesses self-knowledge, though the highest intellect is perfectly identical with its own object of thought.</summary>
<keywords>Intellect, Unparticipated, Supermundane, Mundane, Self-knowledge, Intelligible, Proclus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Intellect’s cognitive relationship with its cause and itself. It completes Proposition 167 and provides the full text for Propositions 168 and 169, which define the Intellect’s self-awareness and its existence within eternity.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, centuries after Plato.</note>

...[the intellect] will know [the cause], but if it knows that alone, it will be ignorant of itself, since it is an **intellect** <note>original: "intellectus." In this philosophy, Intellect is a divine level of reality that perceives truth directly and intuitively.</note>. On the whole, however, by knowing that which is before it, it also knows that that thing is its cause, and of what things it is the cause. For if it is ignorant of these facts, it will also be ignorant of the fact that it produces those things by its very being—producing things while not knowing what it produces. However, that which one procreates, and the things of which one is the cause, it will also know itself as being procreated from that cause, because it knows the things that exist before itself. Therefore, altogether, by knowing the things before it, it will also know itself. If, then, there is an **intelligible intellect** <note>original: "intellectus intelligibilis." An intellect that serves as an object of thought for itself or for others.</note>, that intellect, knowing itself, also knows the **intelligible** <note>original: "intelligibile." The realm of pure forms or ideas that can only be grasped by the mind, not the senses.</note>, since it is itself intelligible. But each individual intellect among those that come after that primary one understands the intelligible within itself and, at the same time, that which is before itself. There is, therefore, also an intelligible in the intellect, and an intellect in the intelligible. But one is identical to the intelligible itself, while the other is identical to that which is in itself. It is not, however, identical to that which is before it; for the "simply intelligible" is one thing, and the "intelligible within the intelligent" is another.

### PROPOSITION 168.

Every intellect knows in **act** <note>original: "actu." This refers to *energeia*—being fully operational or realized, rather than just having the potential to know.</note> that which it understands, and also knows that it understands; it is not the property of one intellect to understand, and of another to understand that it understands.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For if it is an intellect in act and understands itself, not being anything different from the object understood, it knows itself and contemplates itself. Moreover, in beholding the one who understands, and in knowing the one who beholds, it knows that it is an intellect in act. Knowing this, it knows that it understands, and not only what it understands. Therefore, it knows both at once: the intelligible object, and the fact that it understands that object, and it is itself understood by itself as the one understanding, etc.

### PROPOSITION 169.

Every intellect has its **essence** <note>original: "essentiam." The fundamental "whatness" or being of a thing.</note>, its power, and its act in **eternity** <note>original: "sempiternitate." This refers to *Aion*, a state of existence where all things are present at once, without past or future.</note>.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For if it understands itself, and the intellect and the intelligible are the same, then **intellection** <note>original: "intellectio." The actual process or event of intellectual thought.</note> is the same as the intellect and the intelligible. For since intellection stands in the middle between the one understanding and the thing understood, and since those two are identical...

<summary>Proclus argues that the Intellect achieves self-knowledge by knowing its cause and that its act of understanding is inseparable from its awareness of that act. He further establishes that the Intellect exists entirely within the realm of eternity, rather than time.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Intellect, Intelligible, Self-knowledge, Actuality, Eternity, Essence, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of the nature of the Intellect. The previous page established that the Intellect is self-aware and eternal; this page focuses on how the Intellect grasps all knowledge simultaneously rather than sequentially.</meta>

**ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 45**

...since they are co-substantial, there will surely be the same intellection <note>original: "intellectio." In Neoplatonism, this refers to the immediate, intuitive grasp of truth, distinct from human "reasoning" which moves step-by-step.</note> for both. But because the essence of this is eternal (for it is all together at once), the intellection is similarly eternal, since it is indeed the same as the essence. For if the intellect is immobile, it will certainly not be measured by time, neither in its being nor in its action. These things being so, its power is also eternal, etc.

**PROPOSITION 170.**

Every intellect understands all things at once. However, the **unparticipated** <note>original: "imparticipabilis." This refers to the primary, transcendent Intellect that remains pure and does not "mingle" with lower levels of reality.</note> intellect understands all things simply. But each of those intellects that follow it understands all things according to its own specific nature.

**DEMONSTRATION.**

For if every intellect has established its essence in eternity, and its action together with its essence, it will understand all things at once. If it understood all things partially—now one thing and then another of the things that follow—it would not be in eternity. For that which exists in succession exists entirely in time. Those things that exist in succession have a "before" and an "after," and do not possess the whole all at once. 

If, therefore, all intellects understood in the same way, they would not differ from one another. For if they understand all things in the same way, they are all things in the same way, since they are the very things they understand. But if they existed as all things in the same way, one would not be unparticipated while another is not. For those whose intellections are the same, their essences are also the same. Since the intellection of each is the same as the **being** <note>original: "esse." In this context, an intellect's thinking and its actual existence are identical.</note> of each, and each thing is both: both intellection and being. 

It remains, therefore, either that each intellect does not understand all things in the same way—understanding perhaps one thing, or several, but not all—or else they understand all things specifically. But to say that an intellect does not understand all things is to make it ignorant of some of the things that exist. Furthermore, it would undergo a transition if it understood things not at once, but through a "before" and "after"; yet it is immobile. It would even be inferior to the **Soul** <note>In the hierarchy of reality, the Soul is usually below the Intellect because it thinks in time and sequences.</note>, which in its motion understands all things, if the intellect only understood one thing due to its permanence. 

Therefore, it will understand all things specifically. It is either all things, or one. Or rather, it understands all things specifically; the intellection of all things always exists in all intellects, but all things are defined by one thing out of the whole. Thus, one thing will become predominant in the intellection and in the things understood. While all things are understood at once through one thing, that one thing shapes all others to itself.

**PRO-**

<summary>Proclus argues that all intellects possess all knowledge simultaneously (in eternity) rather than sequentially (in time), though individual intellects focus on specific aspects of that total knowledge.</summary>
