<meta>This page continues Proclus's systematic proof of the nature of the Intellect (Nous). Having previously established the Intellect's relationship with time and eternity, these propositions now define its internal structure as an indivisible unit and its role as a creator of stable, eternal realities.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

**PROPOSITION 171.**
Every intellect is an **indivisible essence**.

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For if it is without magnitude, incorporeal, and immobile, it is indivisible. For everything that is in any way divisible is divisible either by magnitude <note>By "magnitude," Proclus means physical size or spatial extension.</note>, by multitude, or by actions performed in the span of time. But the intellect is eternal in all respects; it is beyond bodies, and the multitude that exists within it is united. Therefore, it is indivisible. 

Indeed, that the intellect is incorporeal is demonstrated by its **turning back upon itself** <note>original: "conuersio eius ad se ipsum." In Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to self-reflexivity or self-consciousness. Proclus argues that because a physical object cannot occupy the same space as itself, only a non-physical "incorporeal" entity can fully "turn back" to know its own essence.</note>. For no physical body turns back upon itself. Furthermore, that it is also eternal is shown by the identity of its action with its essence; for this was demonstrated previously. Finally, that its multitude is united is shown by the continuity of the intellectual multitude with the **Divine Unities** <note>original: "vnitates diuinas." These are the "Henads," the supreme, god-like principles that sit at the very top of Proclus’s hierarchy of being, even above the Intellect.</note>; for these are the primary multitude, while the intellect comes after them. If, therefore, every intellect is also a multitude, it is nonetheless a **united multitude**. For that which is compacted and closer to the One precedes that which is divided.

**PROPOSITION 172.**
Every intellect is the **immediate creator** of things that are eternal and immutable in their essence.

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For everything produced by an immobile cause is immutable in its essence. But the intellect is immobile, existing eternally as all things and remaining in eternity; it produces what it produces by its **very being** <note>original: "ipso esse." This suggests that the Intellect does not "labor" to create; rather, its existence naturally results in the creation of lower forms, much like a sun produces light simply by being a sun.</note>. If, therefore, it always exists and exists in the same way, it always produces and produces in the same way. It is not, then, a cause of things at one time and not at another, but a cause of things that exist forever.

**PROPOSITION 173.**
Every intellect is, in an **intellectual manner**, both those things that are before it and those that are after it.

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For those things that come after it exist within it according to their cause, while those things that come before it exist within it through **participation** <note>original: "per participationem." Participation is the process by which a lower level of reality receives the qualities or "likeness" of a higher level without the higher level being diminished.</note>. But the intellect remains itself, and has been allotted an intellectual...

<summary>Proclus argues that the Intellect is an indivisible, non-physical entity that is self-aware and acts as a constant, eternal cause for the levels of reality beneath it.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Intellect, incorporeality, Divine Unities, Henads, participation, causality, self-reflexivity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of the nature of the Intellect. Note that there is a gap between the previous page (Proposition 171) and this page, which begins in the middle of the demonstration for Proposition 173 and continues through Propositions 174 and 175.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 46
is its essence. Therefore, according to its own essence, it defines all things: both those which exist in another according to their cause <note>In Neoplatonism, a cause contains its effects in a "higher" way than the effects contain themselves.</note>, and those which exist by participation <note>original: "participationem." This describes how lower things receive qualities from higher ones, like a shadow "participating" in the shape of the object that casts it.</note>. For as each thing is naturally born to be, so it participates in those things that are more excellent than itself. But it does not participate in them exactly as they are. For if it did, the more excellent things would be participated in the same way by all; but instead, different things participate in different ways. Therefore, the participations are according to the specific property and power of those participating. 

Consequently, those things which exist before the intellect are in the intellect **intellectually**. But the intellect is also intellectually those things which come after it; for it does not arise from its effects, nor does it possess them in itself as they are, but rather possesses their **causes**. Moreover, it is the cause of all things by its very being; and since its being is intellectual, it therefore possesses the causes of all things intellectually. Thus, every intellect possesses all things intellectually: both those before it and those after it. Therefore, just as every intellect possesses **intelligibles** <note>original: "intelligibilia." These are the pure ideas or forms that can only be grasped by the mind, not the senses.</note> intellectually, so it also possesses **sensible things** <note>original: "sensibilia." These are physical things perceived by the five senses.</note> intellectually, and so on.

### PROPOSITION 174.
Every intellect, by the very act of **intellection**, procreates the things that come after it; its creative act consists in thinking, and its thinking consists in creating.

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if the intelligible and the intellect are the same, and the being of each thing consists in the intellection that it has within itself, then it creates what it creates by its very being; it produces according to the being that it is. Therefore, by the very act of thinking, it produces the things it produces. For **being** and **thinking** are both one and the same. 

Indeed, the intellect and every being that exists within it are identical. If, therefore, it creates by its very being, and being itself is thinking, it follows that it creates by its very thinking. Furthermore, actual intellection exists within the act of thinking; but this is the same as being itself. Being, however, exists in the act of doing, for an agent that acts immovably <note>original: "immobiliter enim agens." Proclus argues that high spiritual principles do not "move" or change when they create; they produce lower things simply by existing.</note> always possesses being in the act of doing. Therefore, intellection is also in the act of doing, and so on.

### PROPOSITION 175.
Every intellect is primarily participated in by those things which are intellectual both according to their **essence** and according to their **action**.

### DEMONSTRATION.
For it is necessary that it be participated in either by these or by others—namely, those that possess an intellectual essence but are not always engaged in thinking. However, participation by the latter is impossible. For the action of the intellect...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Intellect, specifically focusing on how temporal beings relate to eternal intelligence and the internal structure of intellectual forms. It concludes Proposition 176 (likely the start of this fragment) and moves through Proposition 177.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

the intellect is immobile. Therefore, those things by which it is participated always participate in an intellectual action. These participants are themselves always intellectual. For because the action is sempiternal <note>original: "sempiternitate." In Neoplatonic thought, "eternal" means outside of time entirely, while "sempiternal" often refers to something that exists through all of time without beginning or end.</note>, that which possesses action only in some part of time cannot be joined to it. 

Between the extremes—just as in essences—so also in the changes of actions, there is a middle ground. Between an action that is eternal and one that is completed in a specific moment of time, there is an action that possesses perfection throughout the entirety of time. For nowhere do progressions occur immediately; rather, they happen through things that are related and similar, both according to their substances and the perfections of their actions in the same way. 

Therefore, every intellect is first participated in by those things that are able to understand throughout all time, and which always understand—even if their understanding is measured by time, rather than being strictly eternal.

->COROLLARY<-

From this it is clear that it is impossible for a soul that understands only at certain times, and at other times does not, to participate directly in the intellect. <note>Proclus argues that there must be an intermediate "Always-Thinking Soul" between the pure Eternal Intellect and our own human souls, which drift in and out of focus.</note>

->PROPOSITION 177<-

All intellectual forms are both in one another and yet remain distinct by themselves.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

If every intellect is indivisible <note>original: "impartibilis." This refers to a spiritual or mental reality that has no physical parts and cannot be cut or divided like matter.</note>, and if the multiplicity within it is united through an intellectual lack of parts, then all beings will exist together in a single unity. Being indivisible, they are united with one another and permeate "all through all." 

However, if all things are immaterial and incorporeal, they are unconfused with one another; each individually guards its own purity and remains what it is. The lack of confusion among intellectual forms is shown by the specific, distinct way in which individual things participate in them. For if the things being participated in were not distinct and separate from one another, then the participants would not be able to participate in them distinctly. Instead, there would be—to an even greater degree—a confused mixture in lower things, which exist in a lower order of reality. For from where would distinction arise, if the things that constitute and perfect them were themselves indistinct and confused? 

Conversely, the union of the forms is attested to by the indivisible substance of that which contains them...

<summary>Proclus explains that humans cannot connect to the divine Intellect directly because our thoughts are interrupted by time, requiring an intermediate "always-thinking" layer. He also argues that Divine Ideas (Forms) are perfectly unified yet remain distinct from one another.</summary>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 47<-

and a uniform essence. For those things which have their existence in an impartible <note>original: "impartibilis," meaning indivisible or lacking separate parts. In Neoplatonic thought, intellectual things don't take up space, so they don't have "parts" like a physical body does.</note> and uniform essence, exist impartibly in the same thing. For in what way could you divide that which is indivisible and one? They exist together, and they permeate one another whole through whole, in their entirety, without distance. For that which contains is not distant from them, nor is it as if they are in a physical space, where this thing is here and that thing is elsewhere. Rather, everything is together in the indivisible and the one; therefore, they are also in one another. All intellectual forms, therefore, are both in one another in a unified way, and yet each remains separately distinct.

->COROLLARY<-

If anyone requires examples in addition to these demonstrations, let them contemplate the theorems <note>The mental "theorems" or principles of knowledge stored in a person's mind.</note> that exist in a single soul. All of these, existing in the same essence, are without magnitude and are united with one another. For the soul does not possess its contents in a "large" way or locally, but impartibly and without distance; they are both united and distinct. For the soul produces all things purely, and each thing separately, drawing nothing from the rest. If these forms were not always distinguished according to their habit <note>original: "habitum," referring to a stable state or disposition of being.</note>, the active power of the soul would not be able to distinguish them either.

**Every intellect, being a fullness of forms, is comprehensive of forms—some more universal, others more particular. Conversely, the inferior intellects contain more particularly whatever those who precede them contained more universally.**

The inferior beings, being more multiplied, diminish the powers they possess. For those things closer to the One, while being more contracted in their "quantity," surpass in power those that come after; and those further from the One are the opposite. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of intellectual forms within the hierarchy of being. It completes Proposition 177 regarding the relationship between universal and particular causes, and moves into Propositions 178 and 179, which define the eternal nature and finite number of intellectual entities.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR [page number 13]<-

...by fewer causes, they produce more effects, for those forms which exist within them are more universal. And if these, through more causes, produce fewer effects, then the forms within them are more particular.

->COROLLARY<-

From these facts it follows that those things which are generated from superior causes according to a single form are produced according to several separate ideas by secondary causes. And conversely, those things which are produced by lower causes through several distinct forms are produced by superior causes through fewer and more universal forms. Indeed, that which is universal and common comes to all participating things from above; but that which is divided and specific <note>original: "proprium," referring to the unique characteristics that distinguish an individual thing from the general class it belongs to.</note> comes from the secondary causes. Thus, the secondary intellects, through more particular distinctions of their own properties, "disarticulate" <note>original: "dearticulant," a metaphor meaning to break down a unified whole into specific parts or joints, making it more complex and less unified.</note> in a certain way and thin out the formations of the primary intellects, etc.

### PROPOSITION 178.
Every intellectual form <note>original: "intellectualis forma." In Neoplatonism, these are the divine "blueprints" or Ideas residing in the divine Mind (the Intellect) that shape the physical world.</note> is a creator of eternal things.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if every intellectual form is everlasting and immovable, it is the cause of things that are immutable according to their essence and of eternal substances; it is not the cause of things that are generated and corruptible <note>original: "generabilium & corruptibilium," referring to physical things that are born, change, and eventually decay.</note>. Therefore, everything fashioned according to an intellectual form is an eternal intellectual thing. For if the form produces all the things that follow after it by its very being, and if its own being always remains exactly the same, then the things produced by it will also remain the same and will be eternal. 

Therefore, neither the genera <note>The broad categories of things, like "animal" or "plant."</note> which were created by a formal cause at a specific point in time, nor corruptible things insofar as they are corruptible, have a pre-existing intellectual form. For if they did, they would be incorruptible and unbegotten, having their substance from those eternal forms, etc.

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s systematic exploration of the Divine Intellect. ### ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 48

Indeed, that which is closer to the One is fewer in quantity <note>original: "minus est secundum quantum." In Neoplatonic thought, as one descends from the ultimate Source (the One), reality becomes more divided and "many." Therefore, the higher a realm is, the fewer individual entities it contains.</note>, while that which is further away is greater. Also, the count of intellectual beings will be smaller than every multitude that follows after it. Therefore, it is not infinite. It follows then that the multitude of minds is finite. For that which is less than something else cannot be infinite, since the infinite, insofar as it is infinite, can be less than nothing.

### PROPOSITION 180.

Every intellect is a whole, as if consisting of parts; it is united to other intellects and yet remains distinguished from them. But the unparticipated <note>original: "imparticipabilis." An unparticipated intellect is a "pure" Mind that exists independently, rather than being the specific animating principle of a particular soul or body.</note> intellect is simply universal, containing all its parts within itself in a universal manner. However, each of the particular intellects possesses the whole as if in a part, and thus possesses all things in a particular way. For if a thing is "all things" individually, then "individually" is nothing other than "particularly."

->DEMONSTRATION<-

Therefore, the "universal" exists in each of these particular intellects in a particular way, according to one specific predominant characteristic, yet it remains determined within all of them.

### PROPOSITION 181.

Every participated intellect is either divine (insofar as it depends upon the Gods) or it is intellectual only.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if there is a divine and unparticipated intellect, then the intellect that is first in line after it must be akin to it. It cannot be something that differs from it in both ways—that is, by being both non-divine and non-unparticipated. For things that are dissimilar in both respects cannot be joined together. It is clear, then, that the middle link must be similar to the primary intellect in one respect and dissimilar in another. Therefore, it is either unparticipated and not divine, or it is participated and divine. But every unparticipated thing is divine, because it has obtained a rank analogous to the One within the multitude. There will, therefore, be some intellect that is both divine and participated. 

Yet there must also be an intellect that does not participate in the divine unities <note>original: "vnitates," often called 'Henads.' These are the highest, most unified sparks of divinity that sit just below the One.</note>, but is purely an "intellectual" intellect. For in every series, the first members are joined to their own unity and share in it...

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*, specifically concluding the distinction between different grades of intellect (Proposition 181) and transitioning into the hierarchy of souls (Propositions 182–184). # PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

...can appear most nearly to those things which are in the order set above. But those things which are far distant from the first unity <note>The "Principle Unity" or the "One" is the ultimate source of all existence in Neoplatonism.</note> cannot depend upon those [higher orders]. Therefore, there is both a divine intellect and an intellect that is purely intellectual; the former indeed subsists according to the intellectual property which it has from its own unity and from the unparticipated <term>unparticipated: an "imparticipable" principle exists independently in its own right, rather than being "shared out" or used as a blueprint for a specific lower being.</term> [intellect]. This latter, however, [subsists] according to the union which it has received from a participated unity.

### PROPOSITION 182.
Every participated divine intellect is participated by divine souls.

->DEMONSTRATION<-
For if the act of participation makes the participant similar to the thing participated and produces a kinship between them, it is certainly clear that the soul which participates in a divine mind must be divine. Furthermore, directed toward the divine intellect, it must participate in the deity <note>original: "deitas." In this context, it refers to the "henad" or the spark of the One that resides at the summit of a divine intellect.</note> that is within it through the medium of that intellect. For the deity links the soul that participates in it to the intellect, and joins the divine to the divine.

### PROPOSITION 183.
Every intellect that is participated, but exists as purely intellectual, is participated by souls that are neither divine nor those subject to the change from intelligence to folly <note>original: "amentia." This refers to the state where a soul "loses its mind" or becomes irrational when descending into a physical body.</note>.

->DEMONSTRATION<-
For neither are divine souls of such a kind, nor are they [merely] participants in mind. For souls participate in the Gods through the mind, as was demonstrated before; nor are they susceptible to change. For every intellect is participated by those things which are always intellectual according to their essence <term>essence: the fundamental nature or "what-it-is-to-be" of a thing, which remains stable regardless of outward changes.</term> and according to their action. Indeed, this also is clear from the previous [propositions].

## ON THE SOUL.

### PROPOSITION 184.
Every soul is either divine, or changeable from intelligence to folly, or stays forever in the middle between these, though it remains inferior to divine souls.

<summary>This page defines the relationship between different tiers of Intellect and the Souls that participate in them, establishing that divine souls are those linked to divine intellects. It introduces a tripartite classification of souls: divine, intermediate, and those subject to the loss of reason.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s systematic breakdown of the hierarchy of being, specifically concluding the classification of souls based on their relationship to the Divine Intellect and beginning a new section on the fundamental nature of the soul as an incorporeal substance.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 49

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if the divine intellect is participated in by divine souls, then the purely intellectual <note>original: "intellectualis." This refers to the second tier of intellect, which is "intellectual" but not "divine" in the primary sense.</note> is participated in only by those souls which are neither divine nor susceptible to the change from active thinking into mindlessness <note>original: "amentiam." In Neoplatonic philosophy, this does not mean insanity, but rather a state where the soul is not actively exercising its intellectual capacity.</note>. There are, however, souls that undergo this very experience, sometimes being in a state of understanding and sometimes not. It is therefore clear that there are three classes of souls: the first are divine; the second are those derived from the divine, yet always participating in the mind; and the third are those which change at different times from a state of mind into a state of mindlessness.

### PROPOSITION 185.
All divine souls are Gods in a soul-like manner <note>original: "animasticè." This term distinguishes souls that are "gods" by participation from the primary "Henads" or Gods who exist above the level of the soul.</note>. All souls participating in the intellectual mind are perpetual followers of the Gods. All souls susceptible to change are only sometimes followers of the Gods.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if some have a divine light illuminating them from above, others always exercise their intellect, and still others only sometimes obtain this perfection: those in the first group within the multitude of souls are ranked proportionally to the Gods. Those in the second group always follow the Gods, as they always operate according to the mind and depend upon divine souls; they have the same relationship to these divine souls that the "intellectual" has to the "divine." Finally, those souls that only sometimes exercise intellect also only sometimes follow the Gods, for they neither participate in the mind always in the same way, nor are they always able to revert <term>revert: in Neoplatonism, "reversion" is the process by which a being turns back toward its source to achieve fulfillment or self-knowledge.</term> toward the divine souls. For that which only sometimes possesses intellect can in no way follow the Gods forever.

### PROPOSITION 186.
Every soul is an incorporeal essence and is separable from the body.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if the soul knows itself, and everything that knows itself reverts back upon itself, and that which reverts upon itself is not a body (since no body is capable of reverting back to itself), nor is it [separable] from the body... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the argument that self-knowledge requires a non-physical nature because physical objects cannot "turn back" to encompass themselves.</note>

<summary>This page categorizes souls into a three-tier hierarchy (divine, perpetual followers, and occasional followers) and begins proving the soul's incorporeal nature through its capacity for self-reflection.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s systematic exploration of the soul’s nature, concluding Proposition 186 and moving into the proofs for the soul's immortality and its dual status as both "life" and a "living thing."</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus was known as the "Diadochus" or "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, centuries after Plato.</note>

inseparable from the body. For that which is inseparable from the body is not by nature capable of turning back upon itself <note>original: "ad se conuerti." This "reversion" is a technical term in Neoplatonism describing a being’s ability to contemplate its own essence and its source.</note>, for it would then be separated from the body. Therefore, every soul is neither a bodily essence nor is it inseparable from the body. But it is clear that the soul indeed knows itself. For if it knows the things that are above it, it is also by nature capable of knowing itself—and even more so, as it knows itself by means of the causes that exist prior to it.

### PROPOSITION 187.
Every soul is immortal and incorruptible <note>original: "incorruptibilis." In this context, it means the soul cannot decay or be broken down into parts.</note>.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For everything that can in any way be dissolved and destroyed is either corporeal and composite, or it has obtained its existence in a substrate <note>original: "subiecto." In ancient philosophy, a substrate is the underlying matter or thing in which qualities (like color or shape) exist; if the thing disappears, the qualities vanish.</note>. Indeed, that which is dissolved is corrupted because it exists as a composite of many parts. However, that which is born to exist in another, once separated from that substrate, vanishes into non-being. But the soul is both incorporeal and exists outside of any substrate—existing in itself and turning back toward itself—and is thus immortal in its essence <note>original: "sinu." Literally "fold" or "bosom," used here to mean the soul's inner depth or core.</note> and incorruptible.

### PROPOSITION 188.
Every soul is both life and a living thing.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For that to which a soul comes necessarily lives, and that which is deprived of soul is immediately left deprived of life. For a thing lives either through the soul, or through something else and not through the soul. But for it to live through something else alone is impossible. For everything that is participated in <note>A term for how a lower reality receives a quality from a higher reality.</note> gives either itself or something of itself to the participant. If it provides neither, it will not even be participated in. Now, the soul is participated in by that to which it is present, and that which participates in the soul is called "animated" <note>original: "animatum." Meaning "ensouled" or "given life."</note>. If, therefore, the soul brings life to animated things, it is either life itself, or merely a living thing, or both life and a living thing at the same time.

But if it were only a living thing and no longer life, it would surely be composed of life and non-life; thus it would not know itself, nor would it turn back upon itself. For life is knowledge, and the cognitive power, insofar as it is such, lives. If, then, there is something in the soul that is not living, this part does not have the power of knowing through itself. But if it is only life...

<summary>This page argues that the soul must be independent of the body to possess self-knowledge and concludes that because the soul is a self-sustaining essence rather than a composite, it is necessarily immortal and the source of life.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, immortality of the soul, incorruptibility, self-knowledge, substrate, animated beings, Elements of Theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of the soul, specifically concluding the argument that the soul is a "living thing" and moving into its status as a self-sustaining, intermediary entity between the divine and the physical.</meta>

ELEMENTA THEOLOGICA. 50

...[it follows that] it will no longer participate in the intellectual life. For that which participates in life is a living thing, and not life alone. For life itself is primary and unparticipated <note>original: "imparticipabilis." In the Neoplatonic hierarchy, "unparticipated" refers to a pure principle that remains in itself, while "participated" refers to that same principle as it is received by lower beings.</note>. Those things which follow after it, however, are both a living thing and life simultaneously. The soul is not an unparticipated life; therefore, the soul itself is simultaneously both life and a living thing.

### PROPOSITION 189.
Every soul is self-living <note>original: "seu iua," a common printer's error or contraction for "se-viva" (self-living), translating the Greek "autozōos." This means the soul does not "receive" life like a body does, but "is" life by its own nature.</note>.

->DEMONSTRATION<-
If the soul is capable of turning back toward itself, and everything that turns back toward itself is self-subsisting, then the soul is also self-subsisting and sustains itself. Furthermore, it is both life and a living thing, and its existence is defined by vitality. Indeed, to whatever things it is present, it communicates life by its very being; and if the recipient is suitable, it immediately becomes animated and alive. This happens not because the soul calculates or chooses to give life, nor because a process of reasoning and judgment creates life, but by its very being, because it grants a life that can be participated in. Therefore, for the soul, to be is the same as to live. If, then, it possesses being from itself—and this being is identical to living—it possesses living through its own essence, provides life to itself, and has life from itself. If this is so, the soul is surely self-living.

### PROPOSITION 190. <margin>V</margin>
Every soul is a mean <note>original: "media." The soul is described as a "horizon" or bridge, standing between the eternal, indivisible realm of the gods and the changing, divisible realm of physical matter.</note> between the indivisible things and the divisible things that surround bodies.

->DEMONSTRATION<-
If the soul is self-living and self-subsisting, and possesses an existence that can be separated from bodies, it is set apart from and superior to all divisible things associated with bodies. For those things are entirely inseparable from their substrates <note>original: "subiectis." A substrate is the underlying matter that "holds" a quality; for example, color cannot exist without a surface to be on. Proclus argues the soul is not like this.</note>. They are partitioned by physical mass, they have moved away from themselves and their own indivisibility, and they are spread out alongside bodies. Furthermore, even if they persist in their lives, they are not the masters of their own lives, but belong to the things that participate in them; even if they exist in essence or in forms, they are not their own forms, but the forms of the things they shape. But if the soul is not these things alone, it is a self-subsisting essence, a self-living life, and a knower of its own knowledge, and in all these respects, it is separable from bodies. But it also participates in life.
O Si... <note>The text breaks off here mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that the soul is "self-living," meaning its life comes from its own essence rather than an external source, and establishes the soul as a bridge between the spiritual and physical worlds.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical proof regarding the soul's ontological status. The previous page established that the soul is "self-living"; here, Proclus concludes that because the soul receives its knowledge and life from higher causes, it occupies a middle rank between the purely eternal, indivisible divine intellects and the divisible, temporal physical world.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

If this is indeed the case, even as it participates in essence, it also participates in knowledge from other causes. It is certainly clear that it is inferior to the impartible beings <note>original: "impartibilibus." These are the "indivisibles"—pure intellectual entities that do not have distinct "parts" or stages of being, unlike the soul which can be divided by its different functions.</note>, because its life and essence are filled from elsewhere; and if its life is so filled, this is quite evident. For before there is a specific life, there exists both "unparticipated life" and "unparticipated essence." <note>In the hierarchy of the philosopher Proclus, an "unparticipated" principle is a pure, universal source (like Life itself) that remains transcendent, while "participated" versions are the expressions of that source found in individual beings like souls.</note>

It is also clear that the soul is not the primary "knowing thing." For while every soul has life, not every soul possesses knowledge. Indeed, a certain soul may remain a soul while yet being ignorant of true beings. Therefore, it is not a "knowing thing" primarily, nor is its knowledge identical to its very being. It possesses its second essence from those things which are primarily "knowing things" by their very existence. 

Since, therefore, the soul's being is distinct from its knowledge, it does not belong among the impartibles. However, it has also been demonstrated that it does not belong among those things which are divisible <note>original: "partibilia"</note> around bodies. Therefore, it is the medium <term>medium: an intermediary or "middle" state</term> between both.

### PROPOSITION 191.

Every soul that can be participated in possesses an eternal essence, but its activity exists in time.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For it must either possess both eternally, or both in time, or one eternally and the other in time. 

But it cannot possess both eternally, for then it would be an indivisible essence and its nature would differ in no way from an intellectual substance—that is, it would be a nature that is immobile, whereas the soul is by its nature self-moving. 

Nor can it possess both in time, for then it would be merely "generable" <note>Something that is born and dies, having no permanent existence.</note>, neither "self-living" <note>original: "seuiva," likely a contraction for "se-viva" or "autozōos."</note> nor self-subsisting. For nothing that is measured by time is self-subsisting according to its essence. Yet the soul is self-subsisting. For that which turns back toward itself according to its activity is also "reversive" toward itself according to its essence, and proceeds from itself. 

It remains, therefore, that every soul is eternal in one respect, but a participant in time in another. Either, then, it is eternal according to essence but a participant in time according to activity, or vice-versa. But the latter is impossible. Therefore, every soul that can be participated in is allotted an eternal essence, but an activity that proceeds according to time.

PRO-

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 51

->PROPOSITION 192<-
Every participable soul <note>original: "participabilis." A "participable" soul is one that is shared or "participated in" by a lower entity, such as a body, distinguishing it from the "unparticipated" soul which remains purely transcendent.</note> is both eternally existent and the first of all generated things.

->DEMONSTRATION<-
If a soul is eternal in its essence, it belongs to true being in its existence and is "always being." For whatever participates in eternity also participates in being forever. However, if it exists within time according to its actions, it is "generated" <note>original: "genita." In this philosophical context, "generated" refers to anything that operates within the flow of time or has a beginning in its activity, as opposed to things that are purely eternal.</note>. For everything that participates in time is always "becoming," moving through a "before" and an "after" in time; it does not exist all at once, but is entirely generated. Yet, if every soul is only generated in a certain respect—that is, according to its action—it must be the first of all generated things. For that which is entirely generated is further removed from the eternal things.

->PROPOSITION 193<-
Every soul subsists immediately from the Intellect <note>original: "intellectu." In the Neoplatonic hierarchy, "Intellect" (Nous) is the divine level of reality that contains all the eternal Forms or Ideas; it sits directly above the realm of the Soul.</note>.

->DEMONSTRATION<-
If the soul possesses an immutable and eternal essence, it must proceed from an immobile essence. For whatever proceeds from a mobile <note>meaning: "changing" or "moving"</note> essence changes entirely in its own essence. Therefore, the cause of every soul is immobile. Furthermore, if the soul is perfected immediately by the Intellect, it also turns back toward the Intellect and participates in the knowledge which the Intellect grants to those things capable of participating—

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "successor" (Diadochos) to the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...produces; it will also give to the soul constituted by it the essential principles <note>original: "essentiales rationes." These are the *logoi*, or the creative blueprints that define the essence of a thing.</note> of all things that are in itself. For everything that acts by its very being, and which is itself primarily, grants that same quality to its product in a secondary manner. Therefore, the soul possesses the images of the intellectual forms in a secondary degree, etc.

->PROPOSITION 195<-
Every soul is all things: it contains sensible things <note>Things perceived by the five senses; the physical world.</note> as their archetype, but it contains intelligible things <note>Things perceived only by the mind or intellect; the divine or mathematical truths.</note> as their image.

->DEMONSTRATION<-
Because the soul exists as a middle point between the indivisible things and those that are divisible around bodies, it produces and creates the latter; meanwhile, it pre-establishes within itself the causes from which it proceeds for the former. Therefore, it possesses the archetypes <note>original: "exemplariter." The soul acts as the "model" or "blueprint" for the physical world.</note> of those things for which it acts as a cause. But from those things by which it was constituted, it possesses their nature through participation, acting as the "offshoots" of those primary beings. 

Thus, the soul has received all sensible things through their cause; it possesses the immaterial principles of material things, the incorporeal principles of the corporeal, and the non-extended principles of things that have spatial extension. Conversely, it has received the intelligible things as an image—receiving the forms of the indivisible in a divisible manner, the forms of the uniform in a manifold <note>original: "multipliciter." The soul takes a single, simple divine truth and breaks it down into many logical steps or parts.</note> way, and the forms of the immobile in a self-moving way. Therefore, the soul is all beings: it contains the primary beings through participation, and it contains those things that come after it as their archetype, etc.

->PROPOSITION 196<-
Every participable soul <note>A soul that is "shared" by a body, as opposed to a divine soul that remains completely detached.</note> uses an eternal body primarily, which possesses a substance that is neither generated nor corruptible.

->DEMONSTRATION<-
If every soul is eternal in its essence, and by its very existence it primarily animates a body, it must always animate that body. For the existence of every soul is immutable. If this is true, then that which is animated is always animated, and always participates in life. But that which always lives must, much earlier, always exist. And that which always exists is eternal. Therefore, the body primarily animated, and primarily depending on each soul, is eternal. But indeed, every participable soul is participated in primarily by some body...

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 52

...since it is indeed participable, but not imparticipable <note>original: "participabilis... imparticipabilis." In Proclus’s system, an "imparticipable" entity remains transcendent and untouched, while a "participable" one can be shared by lower entities. The soul here is described as something that can be shared by the body it animates.</note>, and it animates the participant by its very being. Every participated soul, therefore, makes use of a primary body which is eternal, unbegotten, and incorruptible according to its essence.

### PROPOSITION 197.
**E**very soul is a vital and cognitive essence, an essential and cognitive life, and a knowledge, essence, and life; and all things exist within it at once—the essential, the vital, and the cognitive—and all are in all, yet each remains distinct.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if the soul is the midpoint between indivisible things and the forms that are divisible around the body, it is neither as indivisible as all intellectual things <note>Pure Intelligences or divine minds that exist above the level of the soul.</note>, nor as divisible as things that have the form of bodies. Therefore, while in bodily beings essences, lives, and cognitions are divided, in souls these things exist indivisibly, unitedly, and incorporeally—all together—due to their immateriality and indivisibility. However, while in intellectual beings all things exist through a state of union, in souls they are distinct and partitioned. Therefore, they are all together and yet separate.

If, indeed, they are all together and indivisible in one, they permeate each other; but if they are separate, they are divided again without confusion. Thus, each thing exists in itself, and all things exist in all. For in essence, there is life and knowledge. If this were not so, no soul would know itself, if its essence were deprived of life and knowledge in itself. Likewise, in life, there is essence and knowledge. For a mindless life, devoid of knowledge, belongs to material failings <note>original: "vitiis." Literally "vices" or "faults," referring here to the limitations and imperfections of the physical world.</note>, which can neither know themselves nor are they pure essences. And knowledge without essence and life is non-subsistent <note>original: "insubsistens." Lacking a foundation or actual existence; it cannot stand on its own.</note>. For all knowledge belongs to a living being and has obtained an essence for itself.

### PROPOSITION 198.
**E**verything that participates in time and is always in motion is measured by cycles <note>original: "periodis." These refer to the recurring orbital or cosmic time-loops.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**B**ecause it is truly measured by time, the motion participates in both movement and a limit, and it proceeds by number. Because it is always moving—and this "always" is not the same as being eternal <note>Proclus distinguishes between *aion* (eternity/timelessness) and *aition* (perpetual duration within time). The soul's motion is perpetual but still exists within the flow of time.</note>...

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

...but temporal, it is necessary to make use of periods <note>original: "periodis." In Neo-Platonic thought, a period is a complete cycle or revolution that returns to its starting point, mimicking the circularity of the divine.</note>. For motion is a certain change from some things into others. But beings are limited in their multitudes and magnitudes. Since these are limited, change cannot proceed to infinity in a straight line, nor can that which is always moved proceed in a limited <note>meaning finite or bounded</note> way. Therefore, that which is always moved will return from the same points to the same points again; thus it will effect a period.

### PROPOSITION 199.
**E**very world-soul <note>The "Anima Mundi" or the soul that animates the entire physical universe.</note> makes use of periods and returns in its own life.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if it is measured by time and acts transitively <note>meaning it acts through a sequence of steps or stages over time, rather than all at once.</note>, and this is its own proper motion—and since everything that is moved and participates in time while remaining eternal makes use of periods, revolves periodically, and returns from the same points to the same points—then surely every world-soul, possessing motion and acting according to time, will also have circuits and returns of its motions. For every period belongs to eternal things and also returns.

### PROPOSITION 200.
**E**very period of a soul is measured by time. However, the period of some souls is measured by a certain <note>specific or partial</note> time, while the period of the first soul, since it is measured by time, is measured by Universal Time.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or if all motions have a "prior" and a "posterior," then their periods also have them; and for this reason they participate in time. Therefore, that which measures all the periods of souls is time. But if the periods of all souls were the same and concerned the same objects, then the time of all would surely be the same. If, however, the returns of some souls are different from others, then the periodic time of those different returns is also different. It is clear that the soul which is first measured by time is measured by Universal Time. For if time is the measure of all motion, that which is moved first will be a complete participant in time and measured by it in its entirety. For if Universal Time were not measuring the first participant, it would not measure anything else according to its whole self. But because all other
                                            souls <note>This "catchword" at the bottom of the page indicates the first word of the following page.</note>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 53

...that more particular souls are measured by more particular measures of universal time is clear from these facts. For if they are more particular than the soul which first participates in time <note>The "First Soul" or World-Soul, which serves as the primary clock for the universe.</note>, they will not yet adapt their own periods to the whole of time. Instead, many of their own returns <note>original: "reditus." In Neo-Platonic physics, this refers to the completion of a soul's circular journey or cycle.</note> will be merely parts of the single period and return made by that soul which first participates in time. For the participation of a lesser power is more particular, while that of a greater power is more universal. Therefore, other souls are not naturally suited to receive the entire temporal measure through a single life, since they have been allotted a more diminished order—measured by the first time—than those which have been allotted a lower order <note>Proclus is arguing that individual souls live through many small cycles (lifetimes) within one grand cycle of the World-Soul.</note>.

### PROPOSITION 201.

**A**ll divine souls have triple actions: some as souls, others as those who have received a divine mind <note>original: "mentem suceperunt divinam." This refers to the "Intellect" or *Nous*, the level of reality above the Soul.</note>, and others as depending on the Gods. They exercise providence <note>term: <term>Providence (providentia)</term> The divine care or steering of the lower world by higher beings without those beings being affected by the lower realm.</note> over all things as Gods; they know all things through an intellectual life; and they move bodies through their self-moving essence.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**B**ecause they naturally participate in supermundane <note>Beyond the physical world.</note> things, and are not simply souls but *divine* souls—possessing an order analogous to the Gods within the breadth of the soul-realm—they operate not only in a soul-like manner, but also divinely, having been allotted a deified summit of their own essence. And because they possess an intellectual substance, they are also placed beneath the intellectual essences. They operate, therefore, not only divinely, but also intellectually: one action according to the "One" <note>The "One" (unvm) is the highest principle in this philosophy, above even the Mind.</note> which is in them, and another which is established according to the Mind. A third action is present in them according to their own specific existence: namely, as a mover of those things which are naturally moved by others, and as a giver of life to those things which possess a life that must be brought into them. For this is the proper operation of every soul; other operations, such as understanding and exercising providence, belong to them through participation.

### PROPOSITION 202.

**A**ll souls that are attendants of the Gods, and always follow the Gods, are indeed inferior to divine souls, yet they stand above particular <note>Individual or human</note> souls.

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

## DEMONSTRATION <note>This concludes the proof for Proposition 202 from the preceding page, which distinguishes between souls that are "divine," those that "always follow" the divine, and "particular" souls like our own.</note>

**F**or divine souls participate in both the Intellect and the Godhead. Because of this, they are simultaneously intellectual and divine, and they preside over other souls, just as the Gods themselves preside over all existing things. **Individual souls**, however, are cut off from the direct guidance of the Intellect <note>original: "intellectum directione priuantur." Proclus argues that human souls do not always remain "in-tune" with the higher realm of pure thought.</note> and are unable to participate directly in the divine essence. Indeed, they would never fall away from intellectual activity if they participated in the Intellect by their very essence, as was demonstrated previously. Therefore, those souls which always follow the Gods hold a **middle rank**; they participate in a perfect Intellect and thus excel over individual souls, yet they are not joined to the divine "Unities" <note>term: <term>Unities (unitatibus)</term> In Neo-Platonism, these are the "Henads," the highest principles of reality that bridge the gap between "The One" and the rest of existence.</note>, for the Intellect they participate in is not itself the divine source.

### PROPOSITION 203.

**I**n the entire multitude of souls, the divine souls—being greater in power than the others—are the fewest in number. Those that always follow the divine souls hold the middle rank in all things, both in power and in quantity. **Individual souls** are weaker in power than the others, but have progressed into a much greater number.

## DEMONSTRATION.

The divine souls are more closely related to the **One** <note>The ultimate, indivisible source of all existence.</note> because of their divine essence. The middle souls are related to it through their participation in the Intellect. The souls at the end of the order, however, are dissimilar in essence to both the middle and the primary souls. In the realm of eternal things, those closest to the One are more unified and fewer in number than those further away. Those further away are more multiplied. The powers of the higher souls are greater, maintaining the same ratio as the divine is to the intellectual, and as the intellectual is to the soul-like. Consequently, the quantities of the lower souls are greater; for that which is further from the One is more of a "multitude," and that which is closer is less of a multitude.

### PROPOSITION 204.

**E**very divine soul governs many souls that always follow the Gods, and an even greater number of those that occasionally take up this rank <note>original: "hunc ordinem susceperunt." This refers to "particular" souls like humans, who only sometimes align themselves with the divine order.</note>.

DE-

<summary>Proclus explains the hierarchy of souls, noting that higher souls (Divine) are fewer and more powerful, while lower souls (Particular) are more numerous and weaker, with "middle" souls acting as a bridge.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, hierarchy of souls, the One, Intellect, Henads, divine souls, particular souls, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 204 regarding the numerical relationship between divine, perpetual, and particular souls, and introduces Proposition 205 concerning the relationship between a soul and its "vehicle" or subtle body.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 54

## DEMONSTRATION <note>This concludes the proof for Proposition 204, which argues that as we descend the hierarchy of being, the number of souls increases while their individual power decreases.</note>

**I**f a soul is divine, it must possess a generative order and a primordial rank among all souls. For the divine presides over all existing things in their entirety, and not only over those that exist only occasionally. For if a divine soul only presided over those souls that follow it occasionally <note>referring to "particular" souls like human souls, which are not always focused on the divine</note>, how would these souls maintain contact with the divine soul? Since they are fundamentally different, they would neither participate directly in the Intellect, nor much less in the Gods. 

But if the divine soul only presided over those that *always* follow it, how could the chain of being proceed down to the other series? In that case, the last things in that series—the intellectual souls—would be sterile, neither born to perfect another thing nor to lead it back to its source. It is necessary, therefore, that from every divine soul there first hang those following souls which operate through the intellect; because they are restored to the intellect, these are more particular than the divine intellects themselves. 

Secondly, the particular souls must participate in the Mind and be capable of divine life through these intermediary souls. For those things that participate in the divine only occasionally are perfected by those that always participate in the more excellent part. And again, for every divine soul, there are more souls that follow it occasionally than those that follow it always. For the power of **Unity** <note>term: <term>Unity (vnitatis)</term> In Proclus's system, this refers to the 'Henad' or the unifying principle at the head of a chain of being.</note>, as it relaxes and descends into multiplicity, always advances; while it decreases in power, it increases in number. 

Since even each soul among those that always follow the Gods presides over many particular souls—imitating the divine soul and drawing more souls into the primordial unity of the entire series—every divine soul, therefore, presides over many souls from among those that always follow the gods, and over even more of those that only occasionally attain this rank.

### PROPOSITION 205.

**E**very particular soul has the same relationship to the soul to which it is essentially ordered as its own vehicle has to that soul’s vehicle.

## DEMONSTRATION

**F**or if the distribution of **vehicles** <note>term: <term>Vehicle (vehiculum)</term> Known in Greek as the *ochema*, this is the "astral body" or subtle carrier of the soul that allows it to interact with the material world.</note> is made according to the specific nature of each soul, it is necessary that the vehicle of every particular soul has the same relationship to the vehicle of the universal soul that the soul itself has to its superior. 

<summary>Proclus explains why divine souls must govern both perpetual and occasional followers to maintain the cosmic chain, and establishes that a soul's 'subtle body' reflects its ontological status.</summary>
<keywords>hierarchy of souls, Unity, multiplicity, astral vehicle, Neoplatonism, Proclus, cosmic order</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration regarding the relationship between souls and their vehicles (Proposition 205) and transitions into the cyclic nature of the particular soul's descent into the material world (Proposition 206) and the eternal origin of its subtle body (Proposition 207).</meta>

OF PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

of the one to the other. But the distribution is according to nature. For first, the things that participate are joined by their very nature to that which is participated. If, therefore, as the divine soul is to the divine body, so the particular soul is to the particular body—by the very fact that both are participating entities—then what was said at the beginning is true: that the vehicles <note>The *ochēma* or "chariot," a subtle, ethereal body that serves as the intermediary between the immaterial soul and the gross physical body.</note> have the same proportion to one another as the souls do.

### PROPOSITION 206

Every particular soul can both descend into the realm of generation <note>The physical world of change, birth, and decay.</note> infinitely and ascend from generation to true being <note>The realm of eternal, unchanging reality.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION

**I**f a soul sometimes follows the Gods, but at other times falls away from its focus on the divine, it participates in both Intellect and folly. It is clear that, by turns, it exists both in the realm of generation and among the Gods. Nor, on the other hand, will it remain in bodies for all time to come. For that which has no beginning in time will never have an end; and that which has no end must necessarily have no beginning. It remains, therefore, that every soul undergoes cycles <note>original: *periodos*, referring to the recurring orbital paths of the soul's journey</note> of ascents from generation and descents into generation, and that this process is unceasing throughout infinite time. Therefore, every particular soul is able to descend and ascend infinitely, and this will not cease; this experience is common to all such souls.

### PROPOSITION 207

The vehicle of every particular soul is produced by an immovable cause.

### DEMONSTRATION

**I**f the vehicle depends eternally upon the soul that uses it, and exists as something innately immutable in its essence, it must have received its substance from an immovable cause. For everything produced by moving causes is entirely changed in its essence. But every soul possesses an eternal body <note>The "luminous" or "astral" body, distinct from the perishable flesh.</note> which is the first to participate in it. Therefore, the particular soul exists, and the cause of its vehicle is also immovable; for this reason, the vehicle is of a supermundane <note>Existing above or beyond the physical cosmos.</note> nature.

PRO-

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 55

### PROPOSITION 208

**The vehicle of every particular soul is immaterial, indivisible in its essence, and impassible.** <note>original: *impassibile*; in this context, it means "incapable of being affected, changed, or harmed by external forces."</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION

**I**f it proceeds from an unchanging act of creation and is eternal, it must possess an immaterial and impassible substance. For those things that are by nature born to suffer <note>original: *pati*, to be acted upon or to experience change</note> in their essence are both subject to change and are entirely material; because they depend on causes that are themselves in motion, they exist in different states at different times. Therefore, they undergo every kind of change, being moved by the principles that govern them. But it is also clear that the vehicle must be indivisible. For everything that is divided is corrupted by that very division, as it falls away from the whole and loses its continuity. If, therefore, the vehicle is immutable in its essence and impassible, it must also be indivisible.

### PROPOSITION 209

**The vehicle of every particular soul descends through the addition of material "tunics."** <note>In Neoplatonic thought, "tunics" or garments refer to the layers of denser, more material energy the soul "puts on" as it moves away from the divine source toward the physical world.</note> **It is restored to the soul itself by the removal of every material thing and by a return to its proper form, in a manner corresponding to the soul that uses it.**

#### DEMONSTRATION

**F**or as the soul descends, it adopts irrational lives; but as it ascends, it casts off all the powers of "generation" <note>The cycle of birth, death, and physical change.</note> which it wrapped around itself during its descent. And having become <unclear>† † † †</unclear> <note>The crosses in the text indicate a gap or corruption in the original manuscript where words are missing.</note> it imitates the lives of the souls that use it and moves together with them, wherever they may move. By its own circular rotations, it represents the intellectual activities of some souls; through its impulses into the world of generation, it represents the "falls" of others; and through its movements toward the immaterial, it represents the purifications of others. Since it is given life by the very existence of souls and is of a shared nature with them, it is transformed in every way alongside their transformations. It follows them wherever they go: it suffers in sympathy with those that suffer, returns together with those that are purified, and is raised up with those that are led back, seeking its own perfection. For everything is perfected when it attains its own proper wholeness.

->P 2<-

<summary>Proclus argues that the soul's subtle "vehicle" is an eternal, indivisible entity that mirrors the soul's spiritual state, taking on material layers during descent and shedding them during its return to the divine.</summary>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as "The Successor" (*Diadochos*) because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, continuing the lineage of Plato's teachings.</note>

### PROPOSITION 210

**Every soul’s innate vehicle** <note>original: *vehiculum*; in Neoplatonic philosophy, this is the *ochēma*, a "subtle body" or spirit-vessel that mediates between the immaterial soul and the physical world.</note> **possesses always the same shape and size; yet it appears larger or smaller, and of a different shape, through the addition and removal of other bodies.**

#### DEMONSTRATION

**I**f the vehicle derives its essence from an unchanging cause, it is clear that it also receives its shape and size from that same determined cause, making both its form and magnitude immutable and unalterable. However, at different times, it appears differently, sometimes larger and sometimes smaller. Therefore, it is through the addition of other bodies—drawn from the material elements—and their subsequent removal, that the vehicle appears to be of a certain quality or a certain size.

### PROPOSITION 211

**Every particular soul** <note>That is, an individual human soul as opposed to the "World Soul" or a divine soul.</note> **descending into the realm of generation** <note>original: *generatio*; the physical world of birth, death, and constant change.</note> **descends in its entirety. No part of it remains "above" while another part descends.** <note>Proclus here argues against earlier philosophers like Plotinus, who believed that a part of the human soul always remained in the divine, intelligible world, untouched by the suffering of earthly life.</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION

**I**f a part of the soul were to remain in the intelligible realm, it would understand its surroundings either in an unchanging, eternal way or in a step-by-step, transitional way. But if it understands in an unchanging way, it would be "Intellect" itself and not merely a part of a soul; in that case, the soul would be a direct participant of pure Mind. If, however, it understands in a transitional way, then a single essence would be composed of that which always understands and that which only understands at certain times. But this is impossible, for these two states of being are fundamentally different, as has been shown previously. Furthermore, it would be absurd if the highest part of the soul, being always perfect, did not rule over its other powers or lead them toward perfection. Therefore, every soul descends in its entirety.

->**THE END.**<-

# BY THE SAME PROCLUS
# ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS.
### D E F I N I T I O N E S.
<note>Definitions.</note>

[Initial C]**ONTINUOUS** things are those whose boundaries <note>original: *termini*; the limits or endpoints of an object.</note> are one and the same.
**CONTIGUOUS** things are those whose boundaries are together <note>That is, they touch at a point but remain distinct entities.</note>.
**SEQUENTIAL** <note>original: *Deinceps*; following in order.</note> things are those between which there is nothing of the same kind.
The **PRIMARY TIME** of a motion is that which is neither greater nor less than the motion itself.
The **PRIMARY PLACE** is that which is neither larger than the body located within it, nor smaller.
A thing **AT REST** is that which, both before and after, is in the same place—both the whole itself and its parts.

### T H E O R E M  I.
**Two things lacking parts will not touch one another.** <note>Proclus is arguing that "indivisibles" (like mathematical points or theoretical atoms without magnitude) cannot have physical contact.</note>

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.
[Initial N]ow, if it were possible, let two indivisibles <note>original: *impartilia*; literally "partless" things.</note> **A** and **B** touch each other. However, "contiguous" things were defined as those whose boundaries are together and in the same place. Therefore, these two indivisibles would have boundaries. Consequently, **A** and **B** would not be indivisible <note>Because if something has a "boundary" or an "edge," it implies there is an interior and an exterior, meaning it can be divided.</note>.

### T H E O R E M  II.
**Two indivisibles will produce nothing continuous.**

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.
[Initial N]ow, if it were possible, let there be two indivisibles **A** and **B**, and let them form a continuum from both. But all continuous things first touch one another. Therefore, **A** and **B** must touch each other, even though they are indivisible; as shown above, this is impossible.

<meta>This page continues Proclus's "Elements of Physics" (Institutio Physica). Having established in Theorem I that indivisible things cannot touch, he now provides an alternative proof for that concept and moves into the relationship between indivisibles and the "continuum," a central concept in his Aristotelian-influenced physics.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
->ANOTHER PROOF.<-

**I**f a continuum is formed from **A** and **B**, which are partless <note>original: *impartibilia*; things that cannot be divided, such as mathematical points or theoretical atoms.</note>, then either the whole of **A** touches the whole of **B**, or the whole of **A** touches a part of **B**, or a part of **A** touches a part of **B**. But if a whole touches a part, or a part touches a part, then **A** and **B** would not be partless. If, however, the whole touches the whole, they will not form a continuum, but will simply coincide <note>original: *adaptabuntur*; they would overlap perfectly, occupying the same space, thus failing to create a larger, continuous magnitude.</note>. Therefore, **A** will not be a continuum, nor will **B** be continuous with **A**, while the whole touches the whole.

### THEOREM III.
**O**f partless things that exist within a continuum, that which lies between them is itself a continuum.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**L**et there be two partless things, **A** and **B**. I say that the space between **A** and **B** is a continuum. For if it were not, then the partless thing **A** would touch the partless thing **B**, which is impossible <note>This refers back to the proof in Theorem I, which established that points cannot touch.</note>. Therefore, the medium between them is a continuum.

### THEOREM IIII.
**T**wo partless things are not in sequence <note>original: *deinceps*; following one after another in a series without gaps of the same kind.</note> with one another.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or let there be two partless things, **A** and **B**. I say that **A** is not in sequence with **B**. Since it has been demonstrated that the medium between two partless things is a continuum, let the medium between them be **C.D.**, and let it be divided at point **E**. Therefore, **E** is a partless thing and is in the medium between **A** and **B**. But things "in sequence" were defined as those between which there is no intermediate thing of the same kind. Therefore, **A** and **B** are not in sequence with each other <note>Because there is always another potential point (E) between any two points (A and B), they can never be "next" to each other in a series.</note>.

### THEOREM V.
**E**very continuum is divisible into parts that are always further divisible. <note>This is the principle of infinite divisibility.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or let **A** and **B** be a continuum. I say that **A. B.** is divided into parts that are always further divisible. For let it be divided at **E**, into **AE** and **EB**. These parts are surely either indivisible or divisible. If they are indivisible...

<summary>Proclus uses geometric logic to argue that "partless" points cannot touch or follow each other in a sequence, concluding that any continuous magnitude must be infinitely divisible.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, physics, continuum, indivisibles, infinite divisibility, geometry, sequence, Aristotle</keywords>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY <note>Though titled "Elements of Theology" in this edition, these theorems are from Proclus’s *Elements of Physics*, where he applies mathematical logic to the physical world.</note>. 57

indivisible, the continuum will be composed of partless things <note>original: *impartibilibus*; things having no parts or extension, like mathematical points.</note>, which is impossible. But if they are divisible, they are divided again into parts; and if these again are indivisible, then partless things will be continuous with one another. But if they are divisible, then these too will be divided, and this continues to infinity. Therefore, every continuum is divisible into parts that are always further divisible.

### THEOREM VI.

**I**f any magnitude consists of partless things, then the motion that occurs over it will also consist of partless things.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et magnitude **A.B.C.** be composed of the partless parts **A**, **B**, and **C**. I say that the motion performed over magnitude **A.B.C.** will also be composed of partless parts. 
For let motion **D.E.F.** be taken over it, and let this be the motion. Let the object in motion be **G**, and let **G** move according to motion **D** over part **A**, and according to **E** over **B**, and according to **F** over **C**. 
Now, motion **D** is either partless or divisible. Let it be, if possible, divisible, and let it be divided into two parts. Then **G** would move through a half-part of the motion before the whole; but it is moving over magnitude **A**. Therefore, **A** would also be divisible. But **A** was defined as partless. Therefore, **D** must be partless. In the same way, we shall show that **E** and **F** are also partless, etc.

### THEOREM VII.

**I**f motion consists of partless things, then the time of that motion will also consist of partless things.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et motion **A.B.C.** be composed of the partless parts **A**, **B**, and **C**. Let the time of this motion **A.B.C.** be **D.E.F.** I say that this time is also composed of partless parts. 
For let the object that moves be **G**, and let it move according to motion **A** in time **D**, and according to motion **C** <note>The text likely means **B** following the sequence, but follows the letter **C** here.</note> in time **F**. I say that **D**, **E**, and **F** are partless. For if **D**—the time in which motion **A** is carried out—is divisible, let it be divided. Therefore, in half the time, a part of motion **A** would occur, and not the whole. Thus, motion **A** itself would be divisible. But it was...

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: *Procli Diadochi*. Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" to Plato’s Academy in Athens. Though this text is labeled "Theology," it contains his *Elements of Physics*, a series of geometric-style proofs about the natural world.</note>

## THE PROPORTION OF MOTIONS.

### THEOREM VIII.

**O**f things moving with unequal velocity, that which is faster covers a greater distance <note>original: *spatium*; literally "space," referring here to the length of the path traveled.</note> in an equal amount of time.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et there be objects moving with unequal velocity: let **A.** be the faster and **B.** the slower. Let **A.** move from **C.** to **D.** in time **F.G.** Since **B.** is slower, it will not arrive from **C.** to **D.** in the same time **F.G.** For the "faster" object is defined as that which reaches the finish first, and the "slower" as that which arrives later. 
Therefore, in time **F.G.**, let the slower object move so that it reaches point **E.** Thus, in the same duration of time, **A.** has moved through the distance **C.D.**, and **B.** has moved through **C.E.** Since distance **C.D.** is greater than **C.E.**, it follows that the faster object covers a greater distance in the same time.

### THEOREM IX.

**I**f things of unequal velocity are moved, certain times will be taken—specifically, a longer time for the slower object and a shorter time for the faster—in which the faster object will cover a greater distance and the slower a smaller distance.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et **A.** and **B.** be of unequal velocity, where **A.** is the faster and **B.** the slower. Since the faster object covers a greater distance in the same amount of time, let object **A.** move across distance **C.D.** in time **F.G.**, and let object **B.** move across distance **C.O.** in that same time. 
And since object **A.** moved across the distance **C.D.** in the whole time **F.G.**, it will move across a smaller distance **C.O.** <note>The text uses **C.O.** and **C.E.** somewhat interchangeably here; the logic dictates that the faster object covers the same "small" distance as the slow object, but does so in less time.</note> in a shorter time than **F.G.** Let this shorter time be taken and called **F.K.** 
Now, since **A.** moved across distance **C.E.** in time **F.K.**, while **B.** moved across distance **C.E.** in the longer time **F.G.**, and distance **C.O.** is greater than **C.E.**, and time **F.G.** is greater than time **F.K.**—in which **A.** moved a greater distance and **B.** moved a smaller distance—this is exactly what was required to be shown.

THEO-

<summary>This page presents two geometric theorems by Proclus defining the relationship between velocity, time, and distance, proving that faster objects cover more ground in equal time and require less time for equal distance.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, velocity, motion, geometric proof, Elements of Physics, time and space, Neoplatonism</keywords>
