<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding motion. Having established that faster objects cover more distance in equal time (Theorem VIII) and take more time for more distance (Theorem IX), Proclus now proves that faster objects cover the same distance in less time. This logical progression leads to the landmark conclusion that time, space, and motion are infinitely divisible.</meta>

# PHYSICAL ELEMENTS. 38

### THEOREM X.
**O**f those things moved with unequal velocity, the faster passes through an equal distance <note>original: *spacium*; here referring to the length of a path or space traveled.</note> in less time.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et there be objects moving at unequal velocities: let **A.** be faster than **B.** Let **A.** move through distance **C.D.** in time **F.G.**, and let **B.** move through a smaller distance **C.E.** in that same amount of time. Since **A.** moved the full distance **C.D.** in time **F.G.**, it will move through the smaller distance **C.E.** in a shorter duration of time. Let it move through it in time **G.K.** However, it was established that **B.** moved through distance **C.E.** in time **F.G.** Since time **F.G.** is longer than time **G.K.**, it follows that **A.** covers the equal distance **C.E.** in less time, while **B.** covers it in more.

->ANOTHER WAY.<-

**L**et **A.** be faster than **B.**, and let **B.** move through distance **C.E.** in time **F.G.** Surely **A.** moves through distance **C.E.** either in that same time, or in more time, or in less. But if it moves in the same time, it would be of equal velocity. If it moves in more time, it would be slower. But it is assumed to be faster. Therefore, **A.** will move through distance **C.E.** in less time, which is what was required to be demonstrated.

### THEOREM XI.
**A**ll time is divisible to infinity, as is every magnitude and every motion. <note>This is a foundational concept in late antique physics, opposing the idea of "atoms" of time or space. It suggests that no matter how small a unit of time or distance is, it can always be measured as smaller by comparing different speeds.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et **A.** be faster than **B.**, and let **B.** move through distance **C.D.** in time **F.G.** Since it has been demonstrated that the faster object passes through an equal distance in less time, **A.** will pass through that same distance **C.D.** in a time shorter than **F.G.** Thus, this time **F.G.** will be divisible. Let it be divided at point **H.** Since **A.** moves in time **F.H.** (and it has likewise been demonstrated that a faster object moves through a greater distance in an equal amount of time, while a slower object moves through less), the distance **C.D.** will therefore be divided. Let it be divided at point **K.** Again, since **B.** passes through distance **C.K.** in time **F.H.**, **A.** will pass through that same distance in even less time, as has been demonstrated. Therefore, time **F.H.** will be divided. And so, time will always be shown to be divided by the faster object (following the demonstration in the preceding theorem), and the distance or magnitude will be divided by the slower object, according to Theorem VIII.

But <note>Catchword at the bottom of the page, indicating the first word of the next page.</note>

<summary>This page presents two geometric proofs: first, that a faster object covers the same distance as a slower one in less time; second, that because speed can vary, time and space must be infinitely divisible rather than composed of indivisible atoms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s geometric proofs on the nature of physics. Following the previous page's conclusion that time, space, and motion are all infinitely divisible, this text transitions into proving the relationships between finite and infinite variables—specifically, that a finite amount of time cannot contain an infinite amount of travel, and a finite distance cannot take an infinite amount of time to cross.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: *PROCLI DIADOCHI*; Proclus (412–485 AD) was the last great head of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens. "Diadochus" means "The Successor."</note>

But indeed, if these are divisible, then motion is also divided to infinity. For it has been demonstrated that if motion were composed of indivisible parts, then time would be as well. But since time is divided to infinity, motion is likewise divided in the same manner, which is what was required to demonstrate.

### THEOREM XII.
**I**n a finite <note>original: *terminato*; literally "bounded" or "limited."</note> time, it is not possible for there to be motion across an infinite distance.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if it were possible in finite time **C.D.** to move across an infinite space **F.E.**, let the time **C.D.** be divided in half at point **k.** In the time **C.k.**, the object **A.** moves through either the whole space **F.E.** or a part of it. But for the whole to be moved through is impossible, because in the full time **C.D.** something of space **F.E.** is moved through, but not the whole, as has been shown. <note>Proclus is arguing that if you move through a certain distance in the full time, you must move through a smaller, finite distance in a portion of that time.</note> Let the distance **L.M.** be moved through in the time **C.D.** across space **F.E.** Therefore, the motion is finite within a finite time. And it is clear that it is also impossible for an infinite distance to be moved through in time **C.D.**, because it is impossible for the whole and the part to be moved through in the same amount of time.

->COROLLARY.<-

**F**rom this it is certainly clear that just as the whole magnitude or space relates to its part, so also the motion of the whole—the time—relates to the time of the part, in those things which are moved at an equal velocity.

### THEOREM XIII.
**N**o finite magnitude will be traversed in an infinite time, etc.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et **A.** be that which moves across a finite magnitude **B.C.**, and let the time of motion **D.E.** be infinite. Let the magnitude **B.C.** be divided in two. The object **A.** moves through half of **B.C.**; this happens either in an infinite or a finite time. Suppose first it moves in an infinite time. But everything that moves continuously moves through the whole in more time than it does the part. Therefore, the object **A.** would move through the whole **B.C.** in a time greater than infinity, which is impossible. Therefore, it moves the part in a finite time. Let that finite time be **K.L.** Again, the remaining half of **B.C.** is moved through by **A.**, but not in an infinite time; rather, in a finite time for the same reason. Let that finite time be **L.M.** Therefore, in times **K.L.** and **L.M.**, the object **A.** is moved...

<summary>Proclus provides logical proofs that finite time cannot contain infinite motion and that a finite distance cannot require an infinite amount of time to traverse, assuming constant velocity.</summary>

### THEOREM XIV.
**G**iven a faster object to a slower one in a sesquialter ratio <note term="sesquialter ratio">A ratio of 3:2, where one value is one-and-a-half times the other.</note>, demonstrate that lines are not indivisible.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et **A.** be faster than **B.** in the stated ratio, and let three indivisible lines **C. D. E.** be taken, and two lines **F. G.**, in an equal time. For the ratio of velocity to velocity is sesquialter. Let the time be **K. L. M.** Since it has been demonstrated that if magnitude consists of indivisible parts, then motion and time must also consist of such parts. Let the parts of time themselves be **K. L. M.** Therefore, object **A.** in these times **K. L. M.** traverses lines **C. D. E.** But object **B.** also in the same times traverses **F. G.** Therefore, the time **K. L. M.** will be divided into the motion of **F.** and **G.** <note>Because B only moves through two units of space, the three units of time must be divided differently to account for its slower motion.</note> It will be divided. Therefore, the indivisible **L.** will be divisible, which is impossible. And since the faster object in the whole time **K. L. M.** passes through **C. D. E.**, in half the time, it passes through half the distance. Therefore, the line **D.** will be divided. But it was assumed to be indivisible, which is impossible.

### THEOREM XV.
**T**he "Now" <note>original: *ipsum nunc*; referring to the mathematical instant of the present moment.</note> is the same in both past and future time.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**I**f it were possible for them to be different, let them be two separate points, such as **A.** and **B.**; however, these cannot be successive to each other <note>meaning there cannot be a "next" moment immediately following another without a gap</note>, as was previously demonstrated. But if both are separate, between both there will be a time divisible to infinity, as has been shown. Therefore, let the time between **A.** and **B.** be divided at point **C.** Truly, if point **A.** were the end of the whole past, and point **B.** the beginning of the whole future, there will not be past and future in the space between them. But the time **A. B.** is divided at point **C.** Thus, one part of that interval is past, and another part is future, which is impossible <note>because it contradicts the definition of A and B as the absolute boundaries of past and future</note>. Therefore, the "Now" is one and the same in both the past and the future.

### THEOREM XVI.
**T**he "Now" lacks parts.

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s investigation into the nature of the "Now" (the present instant) and its relationship to motion and rest. Following the previous page’s argument that space and time cannot be composed of indivisible "atoms," Proclus here demonstrates that the present moment is a dimensionless point that cannot contain motion or rest within itself.</meta>

# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
## PROOF.

**F**or if the same "Now" <note term="Now">The "Now" (Latin: *nunc*; Greek: *to nyn*) refers to the infinitesimal, dimensionless instant that separates the past from the future. It has no duration of its own.</note> exists in both the past and in the future, it lacks parts. For if it were divisible, the same problem would occur: there would be some part of the past in the future, and some of the future in the past. This is impossible. Therefore, the "Now" itself is indivisible.

## THEOREM XVII.

**E**verything that moves, moves in time.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**I**f it were possible, let something be moved in the "Now" itself; let one object be faster and another slower. Let the slower one first move through space **A.B.** The faster one, if it moves through the same space, will move in a smaller [amount]. Therefore, the "Now" itself is divided <note>If the faster object completes the distance in "less than" the present moment, it implies the moment has a 'lesser' part and is thus divisible.</note>. But it was indivisible, as has been demonstrated. Therefore, nothing moves in the "Now" itself. Everything, therefore, that moves, moves in time. 

**B**ut [suppose] the faster object moves in the "Now" itself. Either an indivisible space is moved through, or a divisible one. But it has been shown that an indivisible line does not exist. Therefore, let a divisible space **A.B.** be moved through, and let this be divided at point **C.** Therefore, the faster object—which moves through the whole space **A.B.**—if it moved in the "Now" itself, it would move through space **A.C.** in even less [than the "Now"]. Therefore, the "Now" itself is divisible, which is impossible, etc. <note>original: *&c.*; likely shorthand for *quod erat demonstrandum* (Q.E.D.).</note>

## THEOREM XVIII.

**E**verything at rest is at rest in time.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if it were at rest in the "Now" itself and not in time, it would also be moved in the "Now" itself <note>Proclus argues that "rest" and "motion" are qualities that can only be measured over a duration; to claim rest exists in a single instant would logically imply motion could also exist there.</note>. But this has been demonstrated to be impossible; therefore, it does not rest in the "Now" itself.

->OTHERWISE.<-

**I**f the "Now" is the same in the past and the future, as has been shown, and it is possible to be at rest in the past and to move in the future, then in the middle of them there is neither resting nor moving. But if there were, it would rest and move in the same "Now," which is impossible.

<summary>Proclus demonstrates that the "Now" (the present instant) is an indivisible point without duration, and therefore neither motion nor rest can occur within it, but only over a span of time.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, the Now, time, motion, rest, indivisibility, geometry of time, Aristotle's physics</keywords>

Proclus is using Aristotelian logic to prove that motion requires a divisible subject; an indivisible point cannot, by definition, be in the process of moving.</meta>

# PHYSICAL ELEMENTS. 60

## THEOREM XIX.
**Everything that moves is divisible.** <note term="divisible">Latin: *partibile*. Proclus argues that for motion to occur, an object must have extension (parts). An indivisible point cannot "be moving" because it would have to be either at the starting point or the ending point at any given time.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or suppose there is something moving from point **A** to point **B**. It must, therefore, be either in **A** alone, or in **B** alone, or in both, or in neither, or one part of it is in **A** while another part is in **B**. 

**B**ut if it is in **A**, it has not yet moved. If it is in **B**, it is no longer moving. If it were in both at once, it would simultaneously have not yet moved and have finished moving. If it were in neither, there would be no motion from **A** to **B**, nor would it be in the space between them. 

**I**t is necessary, therefore, for a part of the object to be in **A** and a part to be in **B**. Consequently, everything that moves is divisible <note>In other words, a moving object must "straddle" the space it is traversing; if it were a single point, it could only ever be at a single location, making the transition of motion logically impossible.</note>.

## THEOREM XX.
**If the parts of any motion belong to the parts of some continuum, then the whole motion belongs to the whole continuum.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et the motion of the segment **A B** be represented by **D E**, and let the motion of the segment **B C** be **E F**. I say that the entire motion **D F** belongs to the entire object **A C**. 

**F**or it is necessary that the motion **D F** belongs either to **A C**, or to the parts of **A C**, or to something else entirely. For since it is a motion, it must belong to something that is moved. 

**B**ut the whole motion [**D F**] does not belong to the individual parts of **A C** (for the motions of these parts are only parts of the motion, not the whole). Nor does it belong to any other thing. For if the whole motion **D F** belonged to some other subject, then its parts would also be motions of the parts of that other subject. But we already established they were motions of the parts of **A C**. 

**A**nd it is impossible for a single motion—numerically speaking—to exist in multiple subjects at once. Therefore, the motion **D F** belongs to **A C**; which is what was to be demonstrated.

## THEOREM XXI.
**Everything that has changed, at the moment it has first completed that change, exists in that state into which it has changed.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et us suppose that object **A** has changed from state **B** into state **C**. I say that **A** is now in state **C**. For it must be either in **B**, or in **C**, or in some other state. That it is not in **B** is clear, for... <note>The text breaks off here; Proclus is likely about to argue that if the object were still in B, the change would not have occurred yet.</note>

<summary>Proclus demonstrates that motion requires a divisible subject with parts and that the motion of a whole object is the sum of the motions of its parts.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, kinematics, divisibility, continuum, mathematical physics, Aristotelian logic, motion, change</keywords>

...for it left behind. Nor indeed in any other. For let it be in **D**; it would then be necessary for it to change again into **C**. For there was no change from **B** into **D**, which is impossible. For it is not possible to change into that which it has already changed into. Therefore, that which has changed into **C** is [already] in **C**.

## THEOREM XXII.
**Everything that has changed, has first changed in an indivisible [moment]; etc.** <note term="indivisible moment">Latin: *indivisibili*. In Aristotelian and Proclian physics, the "moment" of completion is an instant with no duration, like a point on a line.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et **A** be that which has changed in the [time] **BC** first. I say that **BC** is indivisible. For if it were possible, let it be divided at point **D**. Certainly, the change occurred either in both parts, **BD** and **DC**, or in only one of them. 

**B**ut if it changed in both, it also changed in the [earlier] part. Therefore, it did not change "first" in the whole **BC**, but in the earlier **BD**. However, if it changed in both, it is also assumed to have changed in the whole. On the other hand, if it changed in only one of the parts, it will no longer be in the whole of **BC**, but only in a part of it. Therefore, **BC** is not divisible, which is what was required to be demonstrated. <note>Proclus is arguing that the exact "first" moment of having completed a change must be a single point in time; if that moment had duration, you could find an even earlier moment within it where the change was already finished.</note>

## THEOREM XXIII.
**No change has a [discrete] beginning of changing.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if it were possible, let **AC** be the beginning of the change **AB**. If **AC** is indivisible, then an indivisible would be contiguous with another indivisible <note>In geometry and physics of this era, points or instants cannot be "next to" each other; they must be separated by a line or a duration.</note>. If, however, it is divisible, let it be divided into **AD** and **DC**. If, therefore, it was changing in both of these, it was also changing in the whole. But it was posited that it changed through the whole. 

**B**ut if it was changing in one and had [already] changed in the other, it is no longer changing "first" in the whole. And if it changed in both before it changed in **AC**, then it changed in **AD**. Therefore, a [specific] "beginning" of change cannot be identified. <note>This highlights the "Continuum" theory: you can never find the very first "step" of a movement, because any step can be divided into a smaller, earlier start.</note>

## THEOREM XXIV.
**If a change occurs according to some quantity, it will not be possible to identify a "first" [part] of it.** <note term="quantity">Latin: *quantum*. This refers to changes in size, weight, or distance—anything that can be measured on a scale.</note>

->DE-<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if it were possible, let the change be in magnitude **AB**. I say that it is not possible to take a first [part] in **AB** itself. For let **AC** perhaps be taken; if it has changed first according to this [part]. If indeed **AC** itself were indivisible, an indivisible thing would be contiguous to an indivisible thing <note term="contiguous">In ancient physics, two "indivisibles" (like points) cannot be next to each other to form a line; there must always be a space or a divisible line between them.</note>. But if it is divisible, there will be something of **AC** prior into which it has changed, and another part prior to that, and so on to infinity. Therefore, there is no part of a magnitude of which some first part has changed. <note>original: "non ergo aliquid magnitudinis est, cuius primum aliquid mutatum est." Proclus argues that because magnitude is infinitely divisible, you can never point to the "very first" tiny piece of distance covered.</note>

## THEOREM XXV.
**If the first time of any change is taken in any part of time, it will also be a part of the change.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or let the first time of change **AB** be taken as **HR**. Since all time is divisible to infinity, let it be divided at point **K**. Therefore, either in both [parts] **HK** and **KR** there is change, or in neither, or in one or the other. But if in neither, the change would not be in the whole time. But if it were in only one of the two parts, **HR** would no longer be the "first" time of the change <note>Because a smaller part of it would be the actual time of the change.</note>. Therefore, in both **HK** and **KR**, there is change. Which was to be demonstrated.

## THEOREM XXVI.
**Everything that is moving has been moved before.** <note>Proclus here explores the relationship between the continuous process of "moving" and the completed state of "having been moved."</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et magnitude **AB** be moving in the first time **HR**, and let the first time be divided at point **K**. Therefore, some of magnitude **AB** has been moved. And indeed, while it was moving in the whole time **HR**, in the time [ending at] **HK** it has [already] been moved. For the limit of time **HK** is the **"Now."** <note term="Now">Latin: *Nunc*; Greek: *to nun*. In this context, the "Now" is the instantaneous boundary between the past and future. It has no duration.</note> In this [instant], indeed, it is possible to *have been moved*; however, to *be moving* is not at all possible <note>One cannot "be in the process of moving" in an instant that has no time in which to move; one can only be at the point of completion.</note>. Similarly, we shall indeed show by dividing time **HK** that before "moving" is "having been moved." For the **"Now"** is in all time. And thus, "having been moved" [is also in all time].

<summary>Proclus demonstrates that because time and magnitude are infinitely divisible, every instance of "moving" is preceded by an instance of "having been moved," and no "first part" of change can exist.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s geometric proofs regarding the nature of motion and time. While the previous page established that there is no "first part" of a change because magnitude and time are infinitely divisible, this page applies that logic to the relationship between the thing moving and the distance it travels, particularly when infinity is involved.</meta>

PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
### THEOREM XXVII.

**Everything that has already moved was moving before.**
<note>original: "Omne quod iam motum est, mouebatur prius." Proclus is arguing that motion is a continuous process; there is no point where you can say "it has moved" without there being a preceding period where it "was moving."</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et something have changed from A to B. It must have changed either in a period of time or in the "Now" <note term="Now">The Latin "Nunc" refers to an indivisible, infinitesimal instant—a "point" in time that has no duration.</note> itself. But if it changed in the "Now" itself, it would be in A and in B simultaneously during that same "Now." For if it is in A at one instant and in B at another, there will be a span of time between the two; for one indivisible thing cannot be contiguous to another indivisible thing. Therefore, it changed from A to B during a period of time. But all time is divisible. Therefore, it could also have been changing in the half of that time, and in the half of that half, and so on to infinity. Thus, everything that has moved was moving before; which is what was necessary to demonstrate.

### THEOREM XXVIII.

**If that which moves is infinite, it does not traverse a finite magnitude in a finite time.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et the moving thing be an infinite **A**, the finite magnitude it traverses be **B**, and the finite time be **C**. If **A** moves through **B**, it is clear that **B** also moves through **A** <note>This is an early description of relative motion: from the perspective of the moving object A, the stationary distance B appears to be passing by it.</note>. Since **A** is infinite and **B** is finite, [the finite object] is moving through an infinity in a finite time. Which is impossible. For when **A** moves through **B**, **B** also moves through **A**. But **A** moves through **B** in a finite time (therefore **B** also moves through **A** [in that same time]), which is impossible, as was demonstrated in Theorem XII.

### THEOREM XXIX.

**If that which moves is infinite, it does not traverse an infinite magnitude in a finite time.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et the infinite moving thing be **A**, the infinite magnitude be **B**, and the finite time be **C**. If the infinite **A** moves through the infinite **B**, it also moves through the parts of **B**. Let a part of **B** be taken, called **D**. It will also move through this **D**, and since **C** is a finite time, the time for [traversing] **D** itself will also be finite...

<summary>This page presents three theorems by Proclus (27-29) regarding the physics of motion, proving that all completed motion implies a prior process of moving, and that infinite objects cannot traverse distances (whether finite or infinite) within a finite span of time.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Proclus regarding the limits of motion, specifically addressing infinite motion and the motion of indivisible points. It follows the previous page's demonstration that an infinite object cannot traverse a finite space in finite time.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS. 62

[...continued from previous page] has been shown. Let **I** be this time. Therefore, the infinite **A** does not pass through a bounded magnitude **D** in a finite time **I**, which is impossible, as was shown in the preceding theorem. Therefore, an infinite thing does not move through an infinite space in a finite time; which is what was necessary to demonstrate.

### COROLLARY.

From these points, it is clear that infinite motion does not exist, except in the sense of the same motion being repeated many times <note>Proclus is likely referring to circular motion or cycles, which can be "infinite" in duration but occupy a finite space.</note>. For if motion were truly infinite, either an infinite thing would move through a finite space, or an infinite through an infinite. However, all these scenarios are impossible. Therefore, motion is not infinite.

### THEOREM XXX.

**Everything that moves through space is, as a whole, in its first location in the "Now."**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**F**or if it were not in the "Now" <note term="Now">original: "Nunc." In ancient physics, the "Now" is a dimensionless point in time, much like a geometric point is a dimensionless location in space.</note> but in a span of time **A.B.** that it is in its first location, and if that time **A.B.** is divided into **A.C.** and **C.B.**, then the period **A.C.** must come before **C.B.** However, if the moving object is in the "whole first location" for the entire duration of **A.B.**, it would be in the same place during both the "before" and the "after." But something that remains in the same place during a sequence of time is at rest. Therefore, the moving thing would be at rest, which is impossible. Consequently, it is only in the "Now" itself that the moving thing is in its first location.

### THEOREM XXXI.

**Everything that is indivisible in quantity is, by its very nature, immobile.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**L**et us suppose, if it were possible, that an indivisible thing **A** moves from **B** to **C**. Since everything that moves does so in a period of time, then during that time in which it moves, it must either be entirely in **B**, or entirely in **C**, or part of it must be in **B** and part in **C**. But if it is entirely in **B**, it has not yet moved, but is at rest. If, however, part of it is in **B** and part of it is in **C**, it must have parts. <note>This is a classic logical proof: if an object is "halfway" across a boundary, it must have a front half and a back half, meaning it cannot be "indivisible" (impartile).</note> Therefore, that which is indivisible cannot move; which is what was necessary to demonstrate.

<summary>Proclus concludes his arguments against infinite motion and demonstrates that "indivisible" points cannot technically possess motion because they cannot occupy two places at once or be "between" places.</summary>
<keywords>infinite motion, the Now, indivisibility, impartile, Aristotelian physics, spatial location, Zeno's paradoxes</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Book One of Proclus’s Elements of Physics. It provides an alternative proof for the preceding argument concerning the motion of indivisibles and concludes with Theorem XXXII, which demonstrates the logical impossibility of a dimensionless point moving across a distance equal to its own "size."</meta>

# BY PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR

### ANOTHER PROOF.
Let **A** be an indivisible <term>original: "impartile." An entity without parts, such as a geometric point.</term>, and let it move through the distance **B**. Since everything that moves must move across a distance equal to or smaller than itself before it moves across a distance greater than itself, **A** must move in a similar fashion. However, if a larger or smaller part of it moves, it would necessarily have parts <note>This is a logical trap: if something can be divided into a "part that has moved" and a "part that hasn't," it is by definition not indivisible.</note>. If, on the other hand, it moves across a distance equal to itself, then the distance **B** would have to be composed of indivisible points, which is impossible, as has already been shown. Therefore, an indivisible thing does not move.

### THEOREM XXXII.
**That which is indivisible does not move a distance equal to itself.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

**I**f it is possible, let it move, and let the time of its motion be **A.B.** Since all time is divisible, let the time **A.B.** be divided into **A.C.** and **C.B.** Therefore, in the lesser time **A.C.**, the moving object—the indivisible—will move a lesser distance. But in the total time **A.B.**, it was moving a distance equal to itself. Since that which is equal to an indivisible is itself indivisible, there would then exist something smaller than an indivisible <note>If the object moves a distance equal to its "width" in time A.B., then in the shorter time A.C. it must move across a distance smaller than its "width," which contradicts the definition of being indivisible.</note>. This is impossible. Therefore, an indivisible will not move a distance equal to itself.

# THE END OF THE FIRST BOOK.

# PROCLI

# BY THE SAME PROCLUS
### THE ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS.
### BOOK II.

#### SUPPOSITIONS.

<note>Large decorative initial 'O' depicting a seated figure among foliage.</note> **E**VERY physical body is movable in space.
Every local motion is either circular, straight, or a mixture of these.
Every physical body moves with one of these motions.
Every physical body is either simple <note>In ancient physics, a "simple body" refers to one of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) or the "aether" of the heavens, which moves according to a single, unmixed direction.</note> or composite.
Every simple motion belongs to a simple body.
Every simple body moves according to one natural motion.

#### DEFINITIONS.

<note>Decorative initial 'R'.</note> **F**ast things are said to hold a mutual ratio to one another, which is the same as the ratio of the spaces through which they are moved <note>This defines relative speed: if two objects move for the same amount of time, the ratio of their speeds is equal to the ratio of the distances they cover.</note>.
Heavy is that which moves toward the center <note>In the geocentric model, "the center" refers to the center of the Earth and the universe.</note>.
Light is that which moves away from the center.
That which is carried continuously from the same point to the same point is said to move in a circle.
Contrary motions are those which occur from contraries to contraries <note>For example, upward motion is contrary to downward motion.</note>.
One thing is contrary to one other thing.
Time is the number <term>original: "numerus." Here meaning the measure or counting.</term> of the motion of the celestial bodies.
A single motion is one that is uniform in kind, belongs to a single subject, and occurs in continuous time.

#### THEOREM I.

<note>Decorative initial 'Q'.</note> **T**hose things that move in a circle, according to their nature, are simple bodies.

R 2 DE-

<summary>This page introduces the foundational axioms and definitions for the second book of Proclus's physics, establishing the relationship between body types (simple vs. composite) and their natural motions.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, physics, circular motion, simple bodies, definitions of weight, speed, time, celestial bodies</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the definitions of motion and weight into the formal proofs regarding celestial bodies. Proclus here uses the axioms established on the previous page to demonstrate that circular motion—the motion of the heavens—belongs to a unique class of "simple" bodies that are fundamentally different from the four earthly elements (earth, water, air, and fire).</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
## PROOF.
Let **A B** be that which is moved in a circle according to nature <note>original: *secundum naturam*. In ancient physics, "natural motion" is the movement a body performs spontaneously to reach its proper place in the universe, as opposed to "forced motion" caused by external power.</note>. I say that it is **simple**. Since circular motion is a simple motion, and every simple motion belongs to a simple body, the body **A B** is therefore **simple**. Therefore, those things which are moved in a circle are simple.

### THEOREM II.
Those things which are moved in a circle according to nature are neither the same as those which are moved in a straight line, nor are they the same as those which are composed of them.

### PROOF.
Let **A B** be that which is moved in a circle according to nature. I say that it is not the same as those things which are moved in a straight line. For if it were the same as any of those, it would be moved according to nature either upward or downward <note>Straight-line motion was thought to be either "up" (away from the center of the Earth) or "down" (toward the center of the Earth).</note>. But every simple body is moved according to a single simple motion. Therefore, that which is moved in a circle according to nature is not the same as any of those things which move in a straight line. Furthermore, it is not the same as any of the composite bodies. For it has been demonstrated that everything moved in a circle according to nature is entirely simple. But that which consists of those things moved in a straight line is a composite. Therefore **A B**, which is moved in a circle according to nature, is neither the same as those things which move in a straight line, nor those things which are composed of them.

### THEOREM III.
Those things which are moved in a circle according to nature participate in neither **heaviness** nor **lightness**.

### PROOF.
Let **A B** be moved in a circle according to nature. I say that it participates in neither heaviness nor lightness. For if **A B** is heavy or light, it is moved according to nature either toward the center or away from the center <note>In this geocentric framework, "heaviness" (gravity) is defined as the tendency to move toward the center of the universe, while "lightness" (levity) is the tendency to move away from it.</note>. For it has been supposed that that which moves toward the center is heavy, and that which moves away from the center is light. But that which moves toward the center, or away from the center, is the same as one of those things which move in a straight line. Consequently, **A B** would be the same as something that moves in a straight line, while also being moved in a circle according to nature, which is impossible.

### THEOREM IV.
Nothing is contrary to circular motion.

PR-

<summary>Proclus provides geometric proofs to show that bodies moving in circles (the heavens) are simple, unique from earthly elements, and lack weight or lightness, as their motion has no contrary.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s geometric proof regarding the nature of circular motion. Having established that circular motion is "simple" and belongs to a unique class of bodies (the heavens), he now moves to demonstrate that circular motion has no contrary. In the Aristotelian physics Proclus follows, if a motion has no contrary, the body performing it cannot be destroyed by an opposing force, making the heavens eternal.</meta>

PHYSICAL ELEMENTS. 64
### PROOF.

For if it is possible, let there be a circular motion from **A** to **B**, and let there be a motion contrary to it—either one of those that occur along a straight line <note>original: *per rectam*. In this system, straight-line motion (up or down) is characteristic of the four earthly elements: earth, water, air, and fire.</note>, or a circular one. 

If, therefore, upward motion is contrary to circular motion, and downward motion is also contrary to it, then upward and downward motion would be one and the same <note>The author is using a logical "reduction to absurdity." If two different things (up and down) are both the contrary of the same third thing (circular), then logically those two things must be identical to each other, which is impossible in this physics.</note>. But if downward motion were the contrary, then the upward and circular motions would be the same as each other. For to one motion, there is only one contrary motion toward opposite places. 

But if the motion from **A** is contrary to the motion from **B**, there will be infinite spaces between the two contraries. For between the points **A** and **B**, infinite circumferences <note>arcs or curved paths</note> can be described. 

However, let there be a semicircle **A B**, and let the motion from **A** to **B** be contrary to the motion from **B** to **A**. If that which moves along the semicircle from **A** to **B** stops at **B**, it will never be a circular motion. For a circular motion was defined as moving from one point back to the same point continuously. But if the other semicircle is also moved continuously, **A** will not be the contrary to **B**. And if this is not the case, neither is the motion from **A** c... <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence, continuing the geometric argument that points on a circle do not have the same kind of "oppositeness" found in straight-line motion.</note>

**THE WORKS OF PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR**

...moves in a circle according to nature, it has neither beginning <note>original: *generationem*, literally "coming into being" or generation.</note> nor passing away <note>original: *corruptionem*, the process of decaying or ceasing to be.</note>.

**THEOREM 6.**
**E**verything that moves in a circle is bounded <note>original: *terminatum*, meaning it has a limit or a finite boundary.</note>.

**DEMONSTRATION.**
**F**or if it were possible otherwise, let there be some circle **A.B.** that is infinite starting from its center. Let **C** be taken as the center of the circle **A.B.**, and from this center let there be lines **C.A.** and **C.B.** Therefore, the lines **C.A.** and **C.B.** are infinite. Consequently, the space of the angle between them is also infinite. For if it were bounded, it would be possible to extend the angles **A.C.** and **B.C.** to a single distance from **A.B.** But this is impossible, for the lines from the center are infinite. Therefore, the angle **A.C.B.** is also infinite. Thus, whatever moves from point **A** will never traverse the space **A.B.** Yet, surely, whatever moves in a circle returns to the same point from which it began. Therefore, it is not infinite.

**ANOTHER WAY.**
**L**et that which moves in a circle, **A.B.**, be infinite, and let a bounded angle **C.D.** be taken within it. If, therefore, **A.B.**, beginning from **A**, arrives back at the same point, it will return to that point despite being infinite, passing through the bounded angle **C.D.** in a finite <note>original: *terminato*, bounded or limited.</note> time. For all parts of **A.B.** itself will pass through the angle **C.D.** However, this is impossible. For it was shown in the first [book] that an infinite thing does not pass through a finite space in a finite time.

**THEOREM 7.**
**T**he powers <note>original: *potentia*, referring here to physical force or capacity, such as weight or influence.</note> of bodies that are infinite in magnitude are themselves infinite.

**DEMONSTRATION.**
**L**et there be an infinite body **A.B.**, but let its power be finite, **C**. Let this power be its weight <note>original: *grauitas*, the heaviness or downward tendency of a body.</note>, and let a part **B.D.** be cut off from the infinite body **A.B.** Let the weight of this body **B.D.** be **E**. Therefore, the weight **E** either measures <note>In Euclidean geometry, "to measure" means to divide exactly into another quantity, like a ruler.</note> the weight **C**, or it does not. First, let it measure it. As many times as the weight **E** measures **C**, so many times let the body **B.D.** measure the body **A.B.**, which was said to be infinite. Therefore, as **E** is to **C**, so will **C.D.** be to **A.B.** By permutation, as **E** is to **B.D.**, so **C** will be to **A.B.** But the weight **E** belonged to the body **B.D.**; therefore, the weight **C** will belong to the body **A.B.** But it was also stated that **C** was the weight of the whole body **A.B.** Thus, the power of an infinite body and a finite body would be the same and equal. This is impossible. For let a part **B.I.** of the body **B.Z.** be taken...

**BI**. Therefore, the segment **IZ** itself either has weight <note>original: *grauitatem*, referring to the physical property of gravity or heaviness.</note>, or it does not. If it does not have weight, then weight will not exist in the infinite body as a whole, but only in a part of it. But if it does have some weight, then **BI** will be heavier than **BZ**. ### THEOREM 8.
**T**he powers of bodies that are finite in magnitude are not infinite.

### DEMONSTRATION.
**I**f it were possible otherwise, let there be an infinite power **B** belonging to a finite body **A**. Let a half-part of **A** be taken, which we shall call **C**, and let its power be **D**. It is certainly necessary that **C** has a smaller power than **B**, for a part possesses less power than the whole. Let it be, therefore, that as **C** is to **A**, so **D** will measure **B**. Therefore, the power **B** is also finite, and the ratio of **C** to **A** is the same as the ratio of **D** to **B**; and by permutation, as **C** is to **D**, so **A** is to **B**. Since the power **D** belongs to the magnitude **C**, the power **B** will belong to the magnitude **A**. Therefore, magnitude **A** has a finite power **B**. But it was assumed to be infinite, which is impossible. For it is impossible for a power of the same kind to be both finite and infinite in the same object.

It follows the argument that bodies with greater power move through the same distance in less time, or move through more distance in the same time.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: *Procli Diadochi*. Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...since $B$ moves through distance $CE$ in time $DR$, while in time $DZ$ it moves through distance $CI$. Therefore, as $CE$ is to $DR$, so $CI$ is to $DZ$; and by permutation, as $CE$ is to $CI$, so $DR$ is to $DZ$. Moreover, as $B$ is to $A$, so $CE$ was to $CI$. It follows then that as $B$ is to $A$, so $DR$ is to $DZ$. But $A$ moves through distance $CI$ in time $DR$, and $B$ moves through the same distance in time $DZ$. Therefore, the powers <note>original: *potentiæ*, referring here to the inherent force or energy a body possesses to produce motion.</note> of those things which are moved in unequal times correspond to the times of their motions.

### THEOREM 10.
Infinite gravity, or lightness, does not exist.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it were possible, let there be an infinite gravity $A$, and let the body possessing it move through distance $B$. Since everything that moves, moves in time—as was demonstrated in the first part—$A$ will move through distance $B$ in a certain time. Let that time be $C$. Now, let $D$, which possesses a finite power, be moved through $B$, and let the time of its motion be taken as $E$. Therefore, time $E$ is greater than time $C$, for a greater power moves through the same distance in a shorter time. 

And since that which has infinite power moves in time $C$, and that which has finite power moves in time $E$: truly, for those things which move with unequal velocity, their powers correspond to the times of their motions. Thus, as the thing which has infinite gravity relates to that which has it finite, so the time $E$ relates to the finished <note>original: *terminatum*, meaning a finite or bounded duration.</note> time $C$: which is impossible. For the infinite has no ratio <note>original: *rationem*, a mathematical proportion or relationship.</note> to the finite, nor could it possibly have such a ratio if it were finite. The same reasoning also applies to lightness <note>original: *leuitate*, considered by the ancients as a physical force of "upwardness" opposite to gravity's "downwardness."</note>. Therefore, there is no such thing as infinite gravity or lightness.

### THEOREM 11.
No infinite thing can be acted upon by a finite thing.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For let $A$ be infinite, and $B$ be finite, and let $A$ be acted upon <note>original: *pati*, to be affected by, or to undergo a change from an external source.</note> by $B$ in time $C$. Let $D$ be taken, which is less than $B$, acting in the same time $C$, and having a power similar to $B$. Being smaller, it will act less while acting in the same time $C$. For that which is smaller moves something less than that which is larger. Let $E$, then, be the lesser amount acted upon by $D$; and let it be that as $D$ is to $B$, so $E$ is to some other thing $Z$. And since it is that as power $D$ is to pow- <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstrations from Proclus's "Elements of Physics," specifically concluding Theorem 11 and proceeding through Theorems 12 and 13. However, it was assumed that the infinite, which was $A$, moved with power $B$ in time $C$. Therefore, the infinite cannot be affected <note>original: *pati*, meaning to receive action or be acted upon by an external force.</note> by the finite.

### THEOREM 12.
Nothing finite <note>original: *terminatum*</note> can be affected by the infinite.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it were possible, let some finite thing $BZ$ be affected by an infinite power $A$ in time $C$. Then, let a finite power $D$ be taken, and let it be of the same kind <note>original: *eiusdem speciei*</note> as $A$. Truly, this power $D$ will act on less than $BZ$ in time $C$. Let it act on $Z$, which is less than $BZ$. Now, let the ratio be such that as $Z$ is to $B$, so power $D$ is to power $E$. And since as $Z$ is to $BZ$, so $D$ is to $E$, by permutation, as $Z$ is to $D$, so $BZ$ is to $E$. But $Z$ is affected by power $D$ in time $C$; therefore, $BZ$ will be affected by power $E$ in the same time. Consequently, power $E$ is the mover <note>original: *potentia motiva*</note> of $BZ$ in time $C$. But the power of $BZ$ was already $A$, which was infinite, and power $E$ (derived from $D$) was finite, yet both are moving it in the same time. Therefore, in equal time, an infinite power and a finite power move the same thing, which is impossible.

### THEOREM 13.
No infinite thing can be affected by another infinite thing.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it were possible, let $A$ be some infinite acting thing, and let $B$ be an infinite thing being affected. Let $CD$ be the time in which $A$ acts and $B$ is affected. Since $A$ acted on the whole of $B$ in the entire time $CD$, it follows that it will act on a part of it in a shorter time. Therefore, let $E$ be a part of $B$, and let $D$ be the time in which $A$ acts upon it. Let it be that as time $D$ is to time $CD$, so $E$ is to part $Z$ of $B$. For since the times are finite <note>original: *terminata*</note>, and $E$ is also finite, it is possible to take a ratio where, as $D$ is to time $CD$, so $ET$ is to another finite part of $B$, which is $S$ of the infini— <note>The text cuts off mid-word as it moves to the next page.</note>

### THEOREM 14.
Simple bodies are limited according to their kind <note>original: *secundum speciem*, referring to the specific form or category of an element</note>.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
Let a simple body be magnitude $A$. Since a simple body moves with a simple motion, $A$ moves with a simple motion. If its motion is circular, it has one nature and one form. If, however, it is among those things that move in a straight line: if it moves only away from the center, it will be fire. If it moves only toward the center, it will be earth. If it is light toward one thing and heavy toward another, it will be one of the intermediate elements <note>referring to air and water, which sit between the "heavy" earth and "light" fire</note>. Therefore, the forms of simple bodies are limited.

### THEOREM 15.
No sensible body <note>a physical object that can be perceived by the senses</note> is infinite.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it were possible, let $A$ be an infinite sensible body. Since every physical body is either simple or compound, let it first be simple. And since the motion of every simple body is also simple, the motion of $A$ itself will be simple. Since there are only two simple motions—straight and circular—then $A$ will move either in a circle or in a straight line. But if it moves in a circle, it is not infinite, as has been demonstrated <note>Proclus refers here to Aristotelian cosmology, where a rotating sphere must have a center and a circumference, and thus cannot be infinite</note>. If, however, it moves in a straight line: if downward, it will have infinite gravity; if upward, it will have infinite levity <note>buoyancy or "lightness"</note>. For this too has been demonstrated: that the moving powers of infinite bodies are infinite. But it is impossible for gravity or levity to be infinite, as has also been demonstrated. Therefore, the infinite body $A$ does not move in a straight line. It has been shown that it does not move in a circle either. Therefore, it is not one of those things which move with simple motion; nor, therefore, is it simple...

<summary>This page concludes a proof against infinite interaction and establishes that simple bodies (elements) have finite forms based on their natural motions, ultimately arguing that no perceptible physical body can be infinite.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, simple bodies, motion, elements, gravity, levity, sensible body, infinite magnitude, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Theorem 15, which argues that no sensible (physical) body can be infinite. It presents four distinct logical proofs (syllogisms) to support this claim, focusing on the nature of composite bodies, the relationship between power and magnitude, and the requirements of physical motion.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS. 67

...is complex. For every simple thing was moved by a simple motion according to nature. Let **A** be a composite body. But if it is composite, it is either made of finite parts or infinite parts. If it is from finite parts, both in number and in magnitude, then the body itself is finite. If, however, it is from infinite parts, they are either infinite in number, or in magnitude, or both. But there are not an infinite number of forms of simple bodies, as has been shown <note>referring to Theorem 14 on the previous page</note>. It remains, therefore, for them to be infinite in magnitude. But if it is one of those things that move in a circle, it has been demonstrated that it must be limited. If indeed it is one of those that move in a straight line, this too has been demonstrated to be finite. Therefore, body **A** is in no way infinite; it is neither simple nor composite.

### ANOTHER WAY.

Let there be an infinite and sensible body **A** <note>original: *sensibile*, a body perceptible to the senses, as opposed to a mathematical abstraction</note>. Since it is infinite, it has infinite power. For this has been shown. But if it has infinite power, it will have either an active or a passive power. If it has an active power, it will act either upon a finite thing or upon an infinite thing; and if it has a passive power, it will be acted upon <note>original: *patitur*, literally "suffers" or receives an action</note> by either a finite thing or an infinite thing. However, it has been shown that the infinite cannot act upon the infinite, nor upon a limited thing, nor be acted upon by them. Therefore, the natural body **A** is not infinite. For every natural body has power, either active, or passive, or both.

### ANOTHER WAY.

Let there be an infinite body **A**. If, therefore, **A** is a physical body, it will be mobile according to place <note>that is, it must be capable of changing its position in space</note>. Moreover, everything mobile according to place either moves from one place to another in succession, or it moves within the same place. If, therefore, **A** moves in the same place, it will move around a center; and since it would have a center, it would not be infinite. If, however, it changes its place, it will not be everywhere, but in some part of the universal place. But that which is infinite is extended everywhere and in every direction. Therefore, **A** itself will not be infinite.

### ANOTHER WAY.

If **A** belongs to those bodies that move in a straight line, it moves either by force or according to nature. But if it moves according to nature, it passes from an alien place into its own proper place. Therefore, it is not everywhere.

<margin>S 2</margin>
Because <note>original: *Quòd*, a catchword indicating the first word of the following page</note>

<summary>This page provides several logical proofs demonstrating that no physical body can be infinite. It argues that whether a body is simple or composite, or whether its motion is circular or linear, it must possess finite dimensions and defined boundaries to exist within the natural order.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, infinite magnitude, composite bodies, active power, passive power, natural motion, sensible body, physical place</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page concludes the final logical proof of Theorem 15 regarding the impossibility of an infinite physical body and begins the treatment of Time and Motion. It establishes Theorem 16 (the eternity and continuity of time) and begins the proof for Theorem 17 (the eternity of circular motion), maintaining the geometric style of demonstration common in Proclus's physics.</meta>

->PROCLUS DIADOCHUS<-

...Because if it is moved by force, that which applies the force will be more powerful than the object itself. But nothing is more powerful than the infinite. For the infinite also possesses an infinite moving power.

### THEOREM XVI.
Time is continuous and eternal.

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it were not continuous, nor eternal, it would have some beginning. Let time, therefore, be represented by the line **A-B**, and let its beginning be **A**. If this point **A** is itself time, it is divisible, and thus we would not yet have the beginning of time. Instead, there would be another beginning before that beginning <note>Proclus argues that any "beginning" that has duration is not a true beginning, as it can be divided into earlier and later parts.</note>. If, however, the <term>Now</term> <note>original: *nunc*; the Greek *to nyn*. In ancient physics, this is the dimensionless "instant" that connects the past and future.</note> is indivisible, it must be the boundary of another time. For the "Now" itself is not only a beginning but also an end. Therefore, there was time before **A**. Again, if **B** is the end of time, and if **B** is time, it is divided infinitely and into many boundaries within itself. But if it is the "Now" itself, it will also be a beginning. For the "Now" is not only a boundary <note>original: *terminus*</note> but also an end.

### ANOTHER WAY.
Let time be **A.B.** If it is not eternal, it has a beginning and an end. But if this is the case, it will at some time be a being, and at another time a non-being. Whatever is of such a nature exists within time and then does not; therefore, time would exist within time <note>This leads to a logical contradiction where time requires a pre-existing time to facilitate its own beginning and ending.</note>.

### THEOREM XVII.
Circular motion is eternal.

### DEMONSTRATION.
Let there be a circular motion of the circle **A.B.**; I say that it is eternal. For since time is eternal, it is necessary that motion also exist always. And since time is continuous (for the "Now" itself is the same in both the past and the future), it is necessary that some single motion be continuous. For time is the <term>measure of motion</term> <note>original: *numerus motus*. Proclus follows Aristotle’s definition of time as the "number" or "calculation" of motion in respect to before and after.</note>. However, all other motions are not eternal, for they occur from contraries to contraries <note>In rectilinear motion (moving in a straight line), an object moves between two opposite points, such as up and down or center and edge, which implies a stop or a change in direction.</note>. Only circular motion, therefore, is eternal; for to this nothing [is contrary]...

<summary>This page presents proofs for the eternity of time and circular motion. Proclus argues that time cannot have a beginning or end because any "instant" of time is simultaneously a beginning of the future and an end of the past, and concludes that circular motion is the only motion that can be eternal.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Physics, eternity of time, the Now, circular motion, Aristotelian physics, continuity, measure of motion</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Theorem XVII regarding the nature of motion between contraries, following the previous discussion on the eternity of circular motion. It establishes that only circular motion can be eternal, while motion between contrary points must have a beginning and an end.</meta>

PHYSICAL ELEMENTS. 63

...nothing is contrary <note>In the previous section, Proclus argued that circular motion has no contrary, unlike upward and downward motion.</note>. As has been shown.

That all motions which occur between contraries are limited and not eternal, we shall demonstrate in this manner.

Let there be a motion **A-B** between two contraries **A** and **B**. This motion is therefore terminated by these points **A** and **B**, and is not infinite. Furthermore, the motion originating from **A** is not continuous with the motion originating from **B**. Instead, when that which is moved returns, it will come to a stop at **B**. For if the motion from **A** and the motion from **B** were one and continuous, that which is moved from point **D** would be moved toward the same point. Thus, it would move in vain, since it would already be at **A**. But nature does nothing in vain; therefore, it is not a single motion. If, then, the motions that occur between contraries are not eternal—since it is impossible to move along a straight line to infinity, because the contraries themselves are boundaries—neither do they create a single motion when they return <note>Proclus is arguing that a "back and forth" motion is actually two distinct motions separated by a moment of rest, not one eternal continuous motion.</note>.

### ANOTHER WAY.

That the motion from **A** is not continuous with the motion from **B**. For if they were continuous with one another, there would be a contrary motion to a contrary motion at the same time and concerning the same object. For that which is at **B** would have simultaneously arrived there and be moving away from it, and both would be in <term>actuality</term> <note>original: *actu*; the state of currently happening or being fully realized, as opposed to potentiality.</note>, which is impossible. Therefore, the <term>Now</term> <note>original: *nunc*; the instantaneous "present" that divides time.</note> in which it arrived at **B** is different from the "Now" in which it departs from **B**. Between these different "Nows" there is a period of time; therefore, the object is at rest and is moved by neither "Now."

### THEOREM XVIII.

That which moves an eternal motion is itself eternal.

### DEMONSTRATION.

Let **A** be the mover of an eternal motion; I say that **A** itself is also eternal. Because if it were not, it would by no means be moving at that time when it did not exist. But if this mover were not moving, there would also not be the motion which previously existed. But the motion was supposed to be eternal. Furthermore, if nothing else were moving it, that which is moved eternally would become immobile. But if it were moved by something else, the motion would not be continuous, which is impossible. Therefore, that which moves an eternal motion is itself eternal.

### THEOREM XIX.

The immobile precedes both moving things and moved things.

DE- <note>The text breaks off here at the start of the word "Demonstratio" (Demonstration).</note>

<summary>This page concludes the proof that motion between contrary points cannot be eternal or continuous, and introduces the principle that the cause of an eternal motion must itself be eternal.</summary>
<keywords>contrary motion, continuity, the Now, eternal mover, immobile mover, Proclus, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the origins of motion, specifically addressing the necessity of a "First Mover" to avoid an infinite regress. It transitions from the nature of continuous motion to the definition of the primary mover as an indivisible, eternal entity.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: *Procli Diadochi*. "Diadochus" or "Successor" was the title held by the head of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens, signifying Proclus as the heir to Plato’s philosophical lineage.</note>
## DEMONSTRATION.

Let that which is moved be **A**, moved by **B**, and this in turn by **C**. I say that this sequence will eventually come to a stop, and it is not the case that every mover is itself moved by something else. 

For if it were possible, let it be so: either the motions will occur in a circle <note>Proclus means a "causal loop" where A moves B, B moves C, and C moves A.</note>, or they will proceed to infinity. But if there were an infinite number of both movers and things moved, there would be an infinite multitude and magnitude. For everything that is moved is partible <term>partible: capable of being divided into parts; having physical extension</term>, and it moves by way of contact. Therefore, that which is composed of an infinite number of parts would itself be infinite in magnitude. But it has already been shown that it is impossible for a body—whether composite or simple—to be infinite.

If, however, the motion occurs in a circle, then something that is only moved for a finite time would be the cause of an eternal motion, provided all things mutually move and are moved in a circle. This is impossible. For that which causes an eternal motion must itself be eternal. Therefore, among those things that are moved, the motion is neither a closed circle of causes nor an infinite progression. There exists, consequently, a mover that is both immobile and eternal.

### COROLLARY.

From this, it is indeed clear that not all things are in motion, for there is also something immobile; nor are all things at rest, for there are some things that move. Nor is it the case that some things always rest while others always move; for there are some things that sometimes rest and sometimes move, such as those that are moved from one contrary to another. Nor do all things sometimes rest and sometimes move. For there is that which is moved eternally, and that which is eternally immobile.

### THEOREM XX.

Everything that is moved is moved by something.

## DEMONSTRATION.

Let **A** be that which is moved; I say that **A** is moved by something. For it is moved either according to nature or contrary to nature. If it is moved according to nature, nature itself will be the motor <note>The "motor" or mover is the internal or external principle that initiates the change.</note>. But if it is moved contrary to nature, then that which exerts the force is the mover. For every motion that occurs contrary to nature is violent <note>In ancient physics, "violent" motion is any movement forced upon an object that opposes its natural tendency, such as an object being thrown upward.</note>.

### THEOREM XXI.

The primary mover of circular motion is impartible <term>impartible: having no parts; indivisible and therefore non-physical or incorporeal</term>.

<summary>Proclus demonstrates that the chain of motion cannot be infinite or circular, necessitating a first "immobile mover." He further categorizes types of motion (natural vs. violent) and concludes that the primary cause of celestial circular motion must be indivisible.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, First Mover, infinite regress, partible, impartible, natural motion, violent motion, circular motion, Neoplatonism, physics</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page concludes the logical sequence from the previous page, moving from the impossibility of an infinite chain of movers to the specific nature of the First Mover as an incorporeal entity of infinite power.</meta>

PHYSICAL ELEMENTS. 69

☙ DEMONSTRATION.

For let **A** be that which initiates the first motion <note>original: *primum motum*. In Aristotelian and Neoplatonic physics, this refers to the outermost sphere of the heavens, which imparts motion to all other things.</note>. It is necessary that such a thing exists, since everything that is moved is moved by something else. Indeed, if **A** is the first mover, it must be immobile. For that which is immobile precedes all things that cause motion. 

And since it causes an eternal motion, it possesses an infinite power of moving; for a finite <term>finite: having a definite limit or boundary; not infinite</term> power also has finite actions, since action proceeds from power. Therefore, if the action is infinite <note>The "action" here is the eternal, unending rotation of the heavens.</note>, the power must also be infinite. 

It is therefore necessary that the first mover of circular motion is either a physical body or incorporeal <term>incorporeal: lacking a physical body or material form; purely intellectual or spiritual</term>. If it were a body, it would have to be either finite or infinite. However, an infinite body does not exist; and even if it did, it could not move a finite body, as has already been demonstrated. 

But if the body is finite, it cannot possess infinite power; for the powers of things that are limited in magnitude are themselves limited, as has also been shown. Therefore, that which initiates the first circular motion is not a body. It is, consequently, incorporeal and of infinite power—which is what was to be demonstrated.

THE END.

<note>A decorative woodcut ornament depicts a stylized face or mask with elaborate headgear and intricate scrollwork, a common printer's mark used to signal the conclusion of a major section of text.</note>

<summary>This page provides a logical proof concluding that the 'First Mover' of the universe must be an incorporeal, immobile entity with infinite power, as a finite physical body cannot sustain eternal motion.</summary>
<keywords>First Mover, infinite power, incorporeal, circular motion, Neoplatonic physics, Proclus, Aristotelian logic</keywords>
